mr president, i would like to thank you. as we now discuss kyoto, the small island nations are preparing for their conference on climate problems that are forgotten on the other side of the world, despite the threat of expulsion of their entire people because of the destroyed environment. 15 large icebergs are floating up to new zealand. winters are 40 days shorter in my hometown than when i was a kid. almost all the glaciers are melting. more storms are blowing, as was evident over northern europe last weekend. you can't ignore the effects any longer. while not every storm and damage can be blamed on climate change, an increase in frequency is a sure consequence of man's lack of responsibility. the eu must now focus on the problems facing the world today. we cannot be satisfied with seminars and figures, but must take serious action in legislative decisions that have a concrete impact. then let us show you the way. the commission, only you, have the right to propose the minimum tax on fossil fuels. the introduction of a carbon tax on flights within the eu, you don't have to wait. it also introduced a climate levy on imports from countries which do not meet the kyoto targets. i think that's gonna give the bush regime a boost, especially if progressive states get exemptions. but kyoto is not enough, we don't have to wait until 2012 to realize that. we must now draw up an action plan to reduce emissions by at least 30% by 2020. we already know that this is the least we can do for the raw material needs of future generations and the raw material needs of poor countries and a stable climate.
the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you. in the committee on employment and social affairs we were in a large majority in favour of the draft new constitution. we did not do it because we thought the proposal was perfect in all its parts, we would like more decisions by qualified majority in the social field. i myself would like to see the possibility of cross-border trade union rights. we did so because this proposal in the social field is better than the present proposal. we must not make the best the enemy of the good when we consider the proposal for a new constitution. let me give you some examples of improvements. firstly, the objective of full employment. it is the first time that we have this objective in any treaty in the new constitution. secondly, the social clause in the third section, which obliges the union to meet the social objectives in all areas of competence. thirdly, the fundamental rights of the charter of civil rights, which also includes trade union rights. fourthly, the role of the social partners is strengthened by recognising the importance of social dialogue and the social tripartite. fifthly, the balance between employment policy and macroeconomic policy is emphasised. sixthly, the legal basis for services of general economic interest. seventhly, the possibility of initiative by the citizens of europe. the draft new constitution makes the eu more open, more efficient and more democratic. for us who defend the social model, it is quite easy to support the proposal for a new constitution because it introduces a long series of improvements which will make it much easier to develop the social model in the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the worst sentence in the constitution, which goes against the basic idea of the coal and steel union, is: 'member states shall undertake to improve progressively their military capabilities.' if the aim had been simply to reduce the defence budget through efficiency, it would have been so. the obvious aim now becomes to increase the eu's military clout. we're moving from disarmament to rearmament. if we combine this with the text that 'the union's relations with the outside world are aimed at affirming and promoting its values and interests', we are back to a 500-year-old colonial policy. it may worry the world, who remembers the last time we tried to spread our civilization with the help of the army. it may bring peace to us, but it brings trouble to the earth. this is not a text for the future, it is a return to the old imperialism and colonialism. i'll pass on that one.

for our citizens. the opponents should point out that the alternative is the treaty of nice, which in no way increases democracy within the eu. nor does it make it easier for us to work with so many mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. a constitution should define the rules of the game of politics and the rights of citizens in a democracy. a constitution should not determine the content of policy. social policy, taxation, energy policy, police policy, alcohol policy are all things that citizens should be involved in shaping together in the democratic process of each country. when the will of the people changes, these conditions can be changed in the country. the proposal before us is something quite different. it is an expression of the political class's own ambitions for the european project. it regulates in detail, it deals with tourism and agriculture, it legitimises a corporate society, it forces each member state into a monetary union, it points to the concentration of power and a technocratic europe. what is presented here as a constitution is an attempt by the political establishment to usurp the democratic right of the european peoples to shape their own future. the junilista therefore rejects this proposal.
commission. as the honourable member has pointed out, the proposed directive on services in the internal market is at the heart of our efforts to reform the european economy. services account for approximately 70% of eu gdp and employment. the removal of internal market barriers in the area of services, as foreseen by the proposal, is essential for meeting the growth and employment targets which lie at the heart of the lisbon agenda. the potential economic opportunities arising from this proposal are indeed huge. this has been underlined by recent dutch economic research which shows that implementation of the proposal in its current form could result in an increase in bilateral trade and direct foreign investment in commercial services by 15% to 35%. the commission has proposed a horizontal directive for several different reasons. first, a broad range of services are dealt with because many of the barriers identified are common to various service activities. the most efficient way to address these barriers is in a horizontal way. secondly, a large scale and detailed harmonisation process through sectoral directives would be unnecessary, unrealistic and inconsistent with better regulation and subsidiarity principles. thirdly, the services proposal takes into account the specificity of certain activities, proposes specific harmonisation where this is deemed necessary and adopts a phased approach to implementation. it is clear that the proposal has provoked a great deal of discussion. however, this is exactly what a proposal this ambitious and far-reaching can be expected to do. it shows that the proposal is addressing some very important questions. at the same time, this also means that there is a lot of work to be done to achieve a common goal. the country of origin approach is a central element of the proposal, as far as the cross-border supply of services is concerned. it eliminates the problem of cross-border services being subject to a multitude of different rules. this will stimulate the offer of cross-border services and improve the competitiveness of the eu economy. the country of origin approach is in particular essential for smes, who do not have the means to establish a subsidiary or an office in another member state and therefore can export their know-how only by the temporary cross­border provision of services. i wish to note, however, that the country of origin approach does not apply for services provided through a fixed commercial presence in the host country. at the same time, i wish to reiterate that the country of origin approach does not operate in isolation. over and above the specific harmonisation foreseen for certain activities, the proposal also foresees the further development of administrative cooperation between member state authorities and administrations. this harmonisation and cooperation will generate the degree of confidence between member states required for the effective application of the country of origin approach. finally, a number of derogations are foreseen for services demonstrating a particular sensitivity because of the need to protect consumers, public health or public security, or where the current divergence of member states' legislation does not allow the application of the country of origin approach. as pointed out in my earlier reply, the commission is confident that the proposal is the best way to allow the economic potential of the services sector to materialise, in the interest of our workers, our consumers and our companies.
commission. the country of origin approach which lies at the heart of the services directive will allow for the circumstances mr crowley has outlined. as i said in my reply, it will allow companies to operate in another member state without having to go through a multitude of further tests. that is the basis of the country of origin principle as it would apply to the circumstances outlined.
commission. i welcome mr mitchell's contribution, but would like to point out that the matters referred to come under the remit of my colleague, mr spidla. mr mitchell has gone into further problems relating to growth within the european community. the lisbon process is about addressing these matters and, as you will be aware, mr barroso has made the lisbon agenda his top priority for our period in office. i note what the honourable member has said regarding access by women to the labour market and his comments about childcare. i am sure other colleagues in the commission will address these matters.
i would also like to welcome commissioner mccreevy. it is clear that not everyone has understood the need for the services directive. i therefore think it would be appropriate, and i will also ask the commissioner if it would not also be appropriate, to draw up a collection of examples which clearly show how, in concrete terms, businesses have been prevented from contributing to economic development. for example, a french company that wants to produce gravestones is stopped by germans and is subject to a fine. can the commissioner come back with a list of all these problems, which we have seen in solvit and in other contexts, which companies are facing and which are hindering economic development, a concrete list?
mr president, i welcome the presidency and congratulate it on its priorities. i have four questions.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted today in favour of the report on the treaty establishing a constitution for europe, a6-0070/2004. we believe that the constitutional treaty should respect the institutional balance. we therefore reject the proposal to introduce an elected president of the council and to elect the president of the commission by the european parliament. we also reject the procedure provided for in article iv-444, commonly known as the corridor, because we consider it essential that treaty amendments be ratified by each member state before they enter into force. the constitution makes the basic rules of cooperation in the european union clearer. we support reinforcements in the area of subsidiarity and the instruments which reaffirm and further develop the role of national parliaments in european cooperation. the law of the union, as it has been applied and applied, is defined in terms of the relationship between eu law and national law, that is to say, community law takes precedence over national law, but always taking into account the constitutional traditions of the member states. the limits of eu competence are also clarified and the legislative procedure simplified. we therefore support the proposed constitutional treaty.
in writing. we have voted against this report. our definitions of 'subsidiarity' and 'superstate' are clearly different from those of the rapporteurs. we define subsidiarity so that political decisions are taken as close as possible to the citizens and that the issues to be decided at the appropriate level are decided by the national parliaments and not by the eu institutions. our definition of a 'superstate' is a european union state where decisions are normally taken by majority in the council, where not all member states are represented in the commission and where the european parliament has a vote on all matters. the union is no longer a federation of states, as we believe it should be, but a federal state, a federation. a common foreign and asylum policy, a rapid reaction force for action in the outside world, an increased eu budget by giving the eu taxation rights and more are all strengthening this political power of the 'superstate'. this draft constitution should be replaced by a new draft intergovernmental treaty which puts the political responsibilities of national parliaments at the centre. religious issues should not be included in the eu treaty texts. we protest that the majority of the european parliament is putting 340 000 euros of taxpayers' money into a spectacle during the debate on this report to launch the campaign for the adoption of the 'european foundations' in the member states.
in writing. i have decided to withdraw from my group and will vote against the report on the treaty establishing a constitution for europe. i believe that the draft constitution means that the eu is moving from being an intergovernmental cooperation based on treaties to a state based on a constitution. i believe that the proposal strengthens the primacy of eu law over national law, that it takes more power away from the member states and that it gives more power to the larger countries than to the smaller ones. this is something i'm against. i voted in favour of sweden joining the eu in the 1994 referendum, and i strongly support the idea of international cooperation to solve common problems. there are important tasks which we must carry out jointly, not least in the areas of the environment, human trafficking and social dumping. but i am against the union's current direction towards greater supranationality. i want european cooperation at the intergovernmental level.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when the tsunami came, it affected the poor and the rich alike. everyone was running for their lives, and the children were the ones who were hit the hardest. the nightmare of not being able to protect their children affected thousands of people. we all now have a responsibility to give them our support and listen to their needs. i would ask you, mr president, to convey our deepest thanks to the people of thailand and other affected countries. they fulfilled our ideals of humanity, humanism and solidarity. they unselfishly and without discrimination helped all victims regardless of culture, religion, or color. and not least, many of my countrymen received great support. it shows that these concepts are universal and not christian or european. we can begin to fulfil the same ideal by providing real support to the affected countries and establishing trade solidarity, fighting poverty and hunger and making our rescue services help each other more effectively.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the tragedy in asia is still unfathomable to many of us. we share a common grief, regardless of nationality, and our compassion grows out of this solidarity. let us use this feeling of solidarity to make better priorities for the future. environmental scientists claim that the disaster could have been avoided, if not entirely avoided, with much less loss of life and property if the coral reefs and mangrove forests had been allowed to remain. if human security were the primary priority over military security, we would probably not have to experience the death and destruction that we have now. if governments made some objective analysis of what threatens not only their countries but all mankind and the earth and prepared for it, there would be warning systems and the rescue work could be better planned. rich and powerful nations are ready to wage war, including nuclear war, at a moment's notice. but there is no warning or warning for poor people in asia. this is while the world is spending grotesque sums on its war on terrorism, a small problem in terms of the number of lives lost and a war that is currently only producing more terrorism. is there no serious attention being paid to the problems of poverty and ecological security? let's learn sometime.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the indian ocean tsunami disaster is in many ways a turning point in modern world history. for the first time, we have a real wave of sympathy for people who have been affected from pretty much the whole world, not just a part of it. cynics point out that this is related to the fact that there were so many tourists from rich countries around the indian ocean when this happened, who were also affected. that is true, but it should be remembered that this also shows that tourism, like trade in goods and services, does indeed bring together the people of the world and make us realize that we belong together. i am, however, uncomfortable with the fact that the eu seems particularly interested in presenting its positions in the face of this tragedy. if we look at this, we find that it is warning systems that are needed, not the eu. it's a global issue, closest to the un. money does not have to be channelled through the eu, it comes directly from the countries and from the citizens. we have the question of coordinating operational assistance, which is the main task of the un. where's what? rapid action to evacuate citizens of eu countries; the problem was not that the eu lacked a policy, but that some countries, like italy, did very well, while others, like my own country, did poorly. we should not try to use tragedies for eu purposes, but look at what the eu actually has to contribute. it's probably a consular matter.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to begin by expressing my deep sympathy to all those around the world who have been affected by the asian tsunami. at the moment, the focus must be on emergency response to alleviate human suffering, care for the injured, identify the dead, be on hand for the bereaved and facilitate and support reconstruction. as decision-makers, we must also take our long-term responsibility. the earthquake disaster in southeast asia is undoubtedly greater than the earthquake that struck iran last year. the lack of effective disaster preparedness is as palpable now as it was then. for rescue operations to be effective, there must be a well-coordinated international organization that can respond to crises such as earthquakes, floods or terrorist attacks. the eu should therefore have a rescue service that operates both within and outside europe's borders and that always sends aid immediately, whether it is spanish, swedish or somali victims. what could be more important in international cooperation like the eu than saving lives? a european emergency response service would reinforce and complement the member states' own emergency response and civilian crisis management and, of course, work closely with the un. the important thing is that it has a fixed structure and is given responsibility for analysing and planning the need for future rescue operations, planning and conducting joint exercises, coordinating and mobilizing resources for evacuation, care, transport of food, medicine, clothing and other relief materials in the crisis situation. i would urge the council to really set up such an eu rescue service. i hope that the commission will develop the civil crisis management responsibilities it already has today, so that we can take even greater responsibility in future disasters, for which i regret to say that we must prepare.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission's response was good, given the emergency preparedness organization we have today, but the disaster is cause for reflection. much could have been done differently, both at eu level and in the countries most affected. historically, the problem is that disaster preparedness has received too little attention in general. it is relatively easy to mobilize large sums of money from both the public and governments in an emergency situation. getting funds for disaster prevention and preparedness is difficult. for example, it has long been known that there was no warning system for the countries around the indian ocean, not only against tsunamis but also against other severe disasters, especially cyclones. nothing has been done by the governments concerned or by the various donors. it is really only bangladesh in the region that has drawn the conclusions of the risks and established effective preparedness. similarly, the civilian crisis force at eu level now being discussed could already have been established. this is not the first time the need for such a force has been demonstrated. the eu should act more coordinatedly in this area. coordination and cooperation are needed to set up this force, but also to organise all aid dispatches in an emergency situation. this is where the coordination is off. the un has the primary responsibility, but we know that the un does not have the capacity. a much better coordinated eu - i am talking about the commission and the member states - could have been very helpful in the emergency phase. it is something we must stake our future on. finally, the reconstruction. like many other speakers, i wish that the 350 million euros that mr barroso spoke of this morning were fresh money, not taken from the regular budget. if speed requires the use of funds already allocated, we should ensure that compensation is then made by means of a top-up. otherwise, asia as a region will lose €150 million in development aid. finally, i would like to address my final point to the commission. take seriously all the reports that show the bad ecological damage in the area before the disaster by improper exploitation. environmental considerations in reconstruction are vital to prevent further erosion of living conditions and to reduce the risk of new disasters.
for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. the road community between the eu and the us is a large one. we have many historical ties and there is a great gratitude to the united states which has helped us many times. together we have a great responsibility to work for peace, democracy and human rights throughout the world and a common interest in close cooperation in the fight against terrorism, for global security, disarmament and the functioning of international institutions. it is no secret that there are deep differences of opinion on the war crimes tribunal, kyoto, multinational agreements, the treatment of prisoners at guantánamo, some trade issues and the death penalty. these are important issues for the eu and for our citizens, and they are always present in discussions with the united states. relations with iraq have also become very strained and we can debate for ever - and we have done so in this house - what went wrong, how and why. we must put this behind us and take responsibility together for the iraqi people to enjoy peace, security and democracy. the chances of a new start in the middle east peace process have also increased following the palestinian elections. here the eu and the us can play an important and constructive role together. we have a very important six months ahead of us. the two eu-us summits must lead to a new impetus in our relationship. together, we can accomplish much. it was nice to hear the luxembourg ambitions, even if the presidency is not wearing its headphones at the moment. we hope that the bush ii administration will realise that there is much to be gained from trusting cooperation with europe. the eu also has to make a great effort and that is why i am pleased with the constructive tone here. we need to formulate a clear short- and long-term strategy for what we want to achieve and how. a number of issues have been raised, but also how we are to set up a free trade area and a transatlantic parliamentary assembly. the eu has partnerships with most countries in the world. it is strange that we have such a fluffy agreement with this particular country, our very closest neighbour and our most important partner. i therefore look forward to what the presidency and the commission will present to parliament shortly.
in writing. we have voted in favour of today's resolution on climate change. we share the concern about the environmental impact of this project. but that does not mean that we are confident that the kyoto protocol is the solution to all problems. the complexity of climate change and its causes means that measures should also be questioned and subject to a more critical scrutiny than the resolution expresses.
in writing. global climate change is a major problem which we believe there are good reasons to address at international level. the kyoto protocol is an important step in this process. we are critical, however, of several phrases in the resolution. we do not believe that the european parliament should call on those countries which have not yet ratified the kyoto protocol to do so. we believe that each country should decide its position on this protocol at national level. we also oppose the commission and the member states emphasizing the need for climate protection in the context of dialogues with other countries. we believe that each member state should have the right to determine its own national position and to represent it in international negotiations. this safeguards the right of national initiative.
in writing. we support the rapid intervention of the international community to resolve the terrible humanitarian disaster in asia. the necessary resources must be prioritised, both at national and international level, to help people in need and to support reconstruction efforts. the eu shall not take funds from other aid operations to finance the tsunami relief. the appropriations shall be taken within the existing limits of the general budget of the european union. we believe that the eu member states should guarantee their share of the eur 1 billion loan from the european investment bank that should be offered to the affected countries. the un, with its long experience and global reach, is the best organization to coordinate humanitarian aid. the eu should therefore not compete with the un by creating new institutional arrangements.
in writing. we support several of the resolution's proposals, including enhanced economic cooperation between the eu and the us, ratification of the kyoto protocol and support for the international criminal court. however, we have chosen to abstain from voting on the resolution as a whole, as it is broadly aimed at strengthening the eu as a global actor in relation to the us and the rest of the world.
in writing. we are in favour of various forms of international aid to developing countries. we do not, however, believe that the european parliament has the competence to make recommendations on debt relief for developing countries. this issue should be addressed at national level as well as within the framework of existing international structures, such as the 'paris club'. we also note that money lent out immediately by the eu must be written off and therefore be entered in the accounts as a budgetary expenditure. for the reasons stated above, we have decided to vote against the proposed resolution.
in writing. i voted against because the resolution does not even achieve the objectives declared by the presidency. our amendments seeking to write off debts were voted down.
mr president, i would like to thank you. my mother always reminds me that the person who focuses on everything is out of focus. i therefore hope that the commission will dare to prioritise and focus on the most important thing, growth. creating growth is not a matter of magic. everyone knows what needs to be done and why europe is lagging behind, but few dare to talk about it. what is needed is not more power or more money for the eu; what is needed is political courage. it takes courage to stand up and implement a number of far-reaching reforms to get the competitiveness we need and to get away from the over-regulation and over-taxation of the economy. free trade is an important tool for growth. the eu should be the power in europe, and the strongest power in the world, fighting for a free world trade. the project we have built here is based on free trade and on creating peace, prosperity and freedom. it can be very useful in other parts of the world as well. unfortunately, the eu often does the opposite and instead creates new barriers to trade. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'customs duties'. the immorality of this was made all the more evident when in december the european union raised tariffs on perfumes and spices from countries such as thailand and sri lanka. the countries that have been hit by the flood disaster need trade to rebuild their countries on their own, and they certainly do not need new tariffs. that europe is also losing out on this policy and that it is immoral is becoming clearer than ever now that thailand is talking about refusing to buy the prestigious european airbus aircraft as a consequence of the trade policy which is imposing tariffs on fishermen in thailand. i would therefore urge the commission to do everything it can to be at the forefront of free trade and to try to go down in history as europe's most free trade-friendly commission.

mr president, i would like to thank you. president barroso, if you are going to break the working time directives, you can take comfort in the fact that it is in any case for the best of purposes, namely to discuss global justice and the role of civil society. in porto alegre, i was one of 155,000 delegates from 135 countries. at the same time, 20 heads of state and 70 ministers met in davos. but did they listen in davos? from a theoretical european perspective, one might think that free trade, capitalism and liberalisation are the solution to the problems of development. but the theory doesn't fit with the everyday lives of millions of people, and it's time we started to bring the theories into reality, not the other way around. it is time that trade becomes a tool for the people - not the other way around. we must stop demanding the liberalisation of water and other sensitive sectors as a counter-offer to countries for good treatment. barroso, when you meet bush and the eu heads of government, remind them of their 30-year-old promise to give 0.7 percent aid. this aid would not even be necessary if the profits from production in the developing countries were to remain there to a greater extent. barroso, you are most welcome to the next world social forum, somewhere in africa. i can lend you some fair-labelled organic clothing so you can attend one day in cognito, as a regular delegate and really experience the atmosphere there, without the interference of security guards. the very joy and spirit of cooperation, without hierarchical structures, that exists between everyone in the world social forum, that is a hope for the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this debate is about trust and confidence. yet in every member state, in every prison, in every detention centre, there are people who feel that they have received a fair trial because they do not know the language, because the evidence has not been assessed correctly, because of the brutal treatment by the police or in detention. this is despite the fact that we have rules in place in the european convention on human rights and civil liberties, articles 6 and 13, and above all perhaps in rules that belong to community law within the eu. if people are to dare to use the four freedoms, we must also have safety rules not only for goods and capital but also for people. the present rules require that national remedies be exhausted in order to assert a right. a court may request an advisory opinion during the trial, but not an individual. we must therefore introduce the possibility for an individual to request personal inquiry or special representation so that individuals have the same rights as courts. only then can we talk about trust and confidence.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the common legal area is based on mutual recognition. we recognise each other's judgments, and then we must be able to trust the quality of the justice systems, trust equal treatment, trust the functioning of due process, trust fair trials with lawyers and interpretation when necessary. let's compete upward in the quality of justice. thank you, antonio costa, for a good report. then there's the exchange of information, which is more sensitive. antonio di pietro has improved the proposal, but i would like to stress three points. first of all: the quality of data protection in law enforcement shall be equivalent to that in the internal market area before the exchange is applied. secondly: the edps shall give an opinion on sensitive issues. thirdly: in addition to informing the member states of the use of the data, the individual shall also be informed of the information disclosed. we have a commissioner who i know listens and understands these questions. i hope that commissioner franco frattini will include these three elements in the future when it comes to increasing data protection to ensure that we get the same quality of data protection in the area of law enforcement that we already have in the internal market. i really hope that franco frattini will continue to do so and i would like to thank him for the work he has already done in the area of data storage and i believe that we will also see the effects of this in this area.
member of the commission. mr president, it is a great pleasure to be here for my first debate with you in plenary, and the subject could not have been better chosen. it is no coincidence that today's agenda started with the commission working paper. the president of the commission underlined how important the work programme is for this term of the commission. mr frattini took the lead by explaining the priority we attach to criminal matters and justice. and now it is for me as competition commissioner to explain our position on what is in mr jonathan evans' report. i welcome the support of the european parliament, and i acknowledge the support you have given in the past to the european commission, and especially to mr monti, as you rightly emphasise. there is no doubt that i have inherited a great legacy from mr monti. the greatest tribute i can pay him is to continue his work and build on it. under the leadership of president barroso, the european commission has recently proposed a new partnership for growth and jobs. competition policy, as mr evans rightly mentioned, has a crucial role to play here. competition is the central driving-force for competitiveness and growth, and we badly need that growth. that was clearly stated this afternoon by speakers from both right and left. if we are to deliver on the fundamental social and environmental objectives which are at the heart of our european values, then economic growth is a must. there are three ways competition policy can contribute to partnership. firstly, later this spring we will launch a debate on reviewing the state aid rules within the overall objective of 'less and better', which here means better focused aid. aid should be concentrated where it can add most value: for innovation, research and development, risk capital and the development of those regions lagging furthest behind. secondly, we will maintain a fair and firm approach to enforcement and will remain tough on cartels. there is no doubt about that. that was also mr monti's approach. as the report highlights, it is not acceptable to let the global advantages of the single market be undermined by cartels and other unfair business practices among a handful of undertakings. thirdly, we will look at new ways of promoting competition in the internal market, by using sector investigations and competition screening to uncover and tackle the remaining, often hidden, regulatory and private barriers to free competition. in taking these priorities forward, i will take account of the helpful observations presented in the report before us. i can assure you of my commitment to ensuring an ongoing and constructive dialogue on competition issues with this house.
for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to express my appreciation for the report by mr jonathan evans and its conclusions. i would also like to express my appreciation for the contribution made by neelie kroes and to underline that i am one of those who look forward with great anticipation to the further work she can contribute. i think we should be aware that the driving force in the european union at the moment, in the development and modernisation of our society, is competition. we have the external competitive pressure resulting from globalisation and the internal competitive pressure which has been reinforced by enlargement and the single market. it unleashes a lot of dynamism and power in european society, not just in terms of development, growth and new jobs. i also believe that in practice this is the most powerful tool for european integration in terms of the cross-border spread of ideas, goods and services. this is what is happening right now, and i think there is a risk that we are underestimating it. i would therefore ask the commissioner to try to highlight this dimension of what competition brings in future competition reports. it is not just economic, but also european cooperation in its most concrete sense. i believe that we have a duty to spread the conditions for competition to new areas. it is the knowledge society with research, education, health care. this is the case for energy and telecommunications markets, as jonathan evans points out in his report. i would also like to emphasize to the commissioner the part of the report which deals with the importance of also creating good conditions for european businesses to compete globally, that in competitive assessments one should look at the whole internal market and not at national and local submarkets. the companies of the future will require a large domestic market. that is what the internal market is for and that is how competition is to develop.

member of the commission. mr president, i would first of all like to congratulate the two authors of the two reports under discussion, mr goebbels and mr karas, and all the members of parliament who in the committee on economic and monetary affairs have contributed to the drafting of the text we are discussing today in this plenary session. i must say that i substantially agree with the diagnosis in both reports. in the case of the so-called lisbon strategy, five years after its adoption by the european council, we can all agree to share again the objectives and the pillars of that strategy, to ratify our agreement with them, and to regret the delay in the implementation of the policies necessary to move towards the fulfilment of those objectives. in the case of the state of public finances and the existing framework for monitoring the development of budgets and public accounts - the stability and growth pact - we identify ourselves with the reference values, with the pillars set out in the treaty. the development of public finances in the economic and monetary union over the years of the pact has been moving in the right direction, but as mr karas said, there are too many countries, currently ten countries, in a situation of excessive deficit; there are member states of the union with public debt levels in relation to their gross domestic product above the reference value set in the treaty, 60%, and therefore there is also a need for reflection and the search for improvements in the implementation of the pact, and for strengthening the instruments available to bring this fiscal governance framework to fruition. i would like to make the commission's point of view on the first of these reports. it is clear that the european union, and in particular the euro area, has a lower growth rate than our competitors, than the other economic regions of the industrialized world, not to mention emerging countries which have growth rates of 6, 7 and even 10 per cent. this low growth creates a situation of high unemployment and a lack of jobs and raises serious doubts about the sustainability of our social model and, in general, about the sustainability of our social model. structural reforms are therefore necessary and, in this respect, as the honourable members know, the commission has set out in its communication of 2 february a series of guidelines for moving towards a review and improvement of the implementation of the lisbon strategy, setting out a series of priorities around three axes: firstly, making europe a more attractive place to invest and work, by extending and developing the internal market, improving regulations, ensuring open and competitive markets, extending and improving european infrastructure networks; secondly, improving knowledge and innovation as basic factors for growth, by increasing and improving research and development, facilitating innovation and the adoption of information technologies, and contributing to the creation of a strong european industrial base; thirdly, pursuing the objective of creating more and better quality employment by establishing more and better policies, attracting more people to the labour market, modernizing the social protection systems, investing in more and better adapting enterprises, and investing in more human capital. it is in this spirit that we can regain the momentum that the creators, the founders of the lisbon strategy - if i may use the expression - had in mind five years ago, and make up for lost time; we must also act with greater capacity for ownership at national level, because many of the actions needed to bring the lisbon strategy to fruition are undoubtedly dependent on national competences. the commission considers that the role of macroeconomic policy in this respect is to support growth. but increasing growth potential is a basic function of structural reforms, we cannot rely on macroeconomic policy to achieve it. that task should be entrusted to structural reforms such as those included in the list presented by the european commission. with regard to the second of the reports, 'stability and growth pact, the state of public finances', which mr karas has just presented, i should like to point out that the negotiations in the framework of ecofin are progressing quite satisfactorily: at last week's meeting there was further progress. there will be further meetings, of the eurogroup on 7 march and of ecofin on 8 march, which will be very useful in bringing us closer to an agreement. i am optimistic about the chances of reaching an agreement at the european council on 22 and 23 march, which will maintain the principles and reference values of the treaty and the pillars of the framework for fiscal and budgetary discipline set out in the treaty, which we must all respect. the instruments should be improved with this agreement so that the governance of the stability and growth pact avoids the failures we have seen in the experience of recent years, helps countries with excessive deficit problems to regain an adjustment path, to meet the treaty reference values, both in terms of deficit and debt, to put more emphasis on sustainability and the long term, because the challenge of ageing populations is approaching, and at the same time to better link budgetary discipline which remains a necessary element for growth and the strategy of growth, more jobs, sustainability, which is the lisbon strategy, because they are two interlinked strategies. macroeconomic balance is a necessary condition for growth, but more policy is needed: structural reforms are needed. it will be very positive if at the march european council these two important issues, which parliament is discussing today, which the commission is discussing and which the council is discussing, coincide so that we can reach a forward-looking agreement better linking the two strategies and, of course, keeping the principles of both clear. one, more sustainability, more social cohesion, more growth, more employment and more competitiveness; the other, better budgetary discipline and more sustainable public finances as a necessary basis for economic stability and to create the conditions that all investors, all wealth creators, require to look to the future with more confidence.
for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would say that the public deficits in a number of countries are a symptom of the problems in the national economies. we will not solve that problem by allowing the deficit to increase. in fact, we risk eroding the stable basis for growth, which is low interest rates. we risk draining the stimulus to households that low interest rates are, and we weaken the prerequisite for long-term growth that low interest rates are. it is therefore important to stress, as the commissioner did in his speech, the importance of structural reforms. it is europe's economies that need flexibility, not the common rules we have. it is by creating a common framework for businesses in operation that we can create new wealth and new jobs. it is regrettable that the socialist group in the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy chose to abstain when we voted on robert goebbels' report. the report now contains a number of concrete proposals to create a stronger growth force in europe. we believe that there must be better conditions for work, but not, as robert goebbels said earlier, by increasing people's working hours. we want to create better conditions for working for a longer period of time, for being able to work more at different stages of life, whenever one wants to, and for more people to be able to have a job. this requires changes in business, research and the internal market. we are in the midst of a transformation that is significant because of the competition that we see. the important task now is to make europe's industry even stronger by increasing competition. it is about the services directive, it is about deepening competition in more areas. that is our task and then we will have better growth, more jobs and the best wages in europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. growth in the eu area is clearly below what is potentially possible. unemployment is high, the eu has a surplus in its foreign exchange balance, and inflation is below the monetary policy target. as the report emphasises, there is a great need for structural reforms. the report, however, places too little emphasis on the need for a more expansionary policy in several countries. one reason for the excessive policy is the design of the stability and growth pact. in my view, it is misconstrued, leading to too strict policies in some countries. the problem is that too much attention is being paid to the budget deficit. instead, the focus should be on the debt ratio. a country with a debt ratio below 60 percent of gdp and keeping its inflation rate within 2 percent will have a declining debt ratio if it grows in real terms by one percentage point or more and if the budget deficit reaches 3 percent. if a country grows by five percent and has two percent inflation, its nominal gdp grows by seven percent. the budget deficit could therefore be 7%, and the debt ratio is stable in the event of such a deficit. such a country is not a burden on the euro area. debt growth is stable and therefore sustainably defensible. the country should be likely to have a good credit rating and should not pose a credit risk to the euro area as a whole. such a budget deficit is tenably defensible. a limited deficit of 3% means that the government debt as a percentage of gdp is continuously falling. as a debt ratio of 60% of gdp is economically reasonable, there is no reason to impose such a financial restriction on the country. for newly emerging countries, it is natural to have significant exchange rate and capital import deficits. private savings may not be enough, but the government also has a budget deficit. the bottom line is: allow countries with debt ratios below 60% of gdp and inflation under control to grow faster and pursue more expansionary policies. this will provide a stimulus for the whole of the eu. i recommend a change in the stability pact in this direction.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to express my regret that the ppe-de leader, gunnar hökmark, has left the debate. the proposal which the committee has put forward is unacceptable to us social democrats for three reasons. firstly, there is no need for a general extension of working time. what we need is more people in work, not a general extension of working hours. secondly, an overall tax cut in europe will not solve the problems. how is it that the nordic countries with the highest tax burdens have the highest growth rates in the eu? it is a sign that the ppe-de group is on the move. thirdly, we need balance in the lisbon process. as it has now become in the proposal before us, it is even worse than the commission and it emphasises only the first pillar of the lisbon process when reciprocity between the pillars is needed. structural reforms are needed - i share that view - but they must be carried out with social responsibility and with the participation of the social partners, which is the most important thing. gunnar hökmark comes from sweden, where his party is now trying to get close to the social democrats. they're not talking about any tax cuts or any extension of working hours at home in the national debate. but they do here. sweden has had a highly successful economic policy in the post-war period with one exception, 1991-1994. at that time gunnar hökmark was one of the leading politicians in sweden. it would be a pity if the ppe-de group were to adopt this neoliberal policy and make it its own. then we have political differences. we social democrats want a balanced policy and not a neoliberal policy. neoliberal policies are not the solution to europe's problems.
in writing. we, inger segelström, jan andersson, anna hedh, ewa hedkvist petersen and åsa westlund, voted in favour of the report, but would like to make clear that we have a different view on one issue. we cannot support the second sentence of paragraph 1 (f) which recommends that the council should bring closer the national rules on the handling and evaluation of evidence. the principle of free examination of evidence is fundamental to swedish criminal procedure law. in several other member states, rules exist that prohibit certain types of evidence. it is therefore very important for us swedish social democrats to keep the national system intact. moreover, it is doubtful whether there is any legal basis for harmonisation in this respect, either in the current treaty or in the future constitution.
in writing. strengthening mutual trust in european judicial decisions by setting up an evaluation system may seem to be commendable, even if a common reference framework for member states already exists today through the european convention on human rights and the case law of the european court of justice, which regulates minimum standards for access to justice. however, the proposal is another example of the creeping expansion of the eu's competences and is part of the attempt to create a harmonised european criminal and procedural justice system, a legal system which will in practice be outside direct citizen control. moreover, recommending the use of constitutional provisions before the entry into force of the draft constitution because the current treaty does not go far enough is unacceptable as it flagrantly prejudges the democratic process. the junilist therefore votes against this report.
in writing. we fully support the idea that cultural diversity is a fundamental right. the eu is and will remain a mosaic of minorities and cultures. as liberals, we always put the individual at the center of all political decisions. we therefore consider it of the utmost importance to establish in international law that each city, state or group of states has the right to freely determine the cultural policy it wishes to pursue, and to strengthen the policy of international cooperation and solidarity in cultural matters. we therefore believe that the question of cultural capitals is not an issue in which the eu should be involved. the european union is also committed to the development of a european cultural area. we therefore vote against the report by mrs christa prets (a6-0017/2005) on the european capital of culture for the years 2005 to 2019.
in writing. we are in favour of the possibility of choosing two cultural capitals at a time from 2009. however, the explanatory memorandum of the report states that the financing of this project is unclear and refers to the forthcoming 2007-2013 budget plan. we cannot at present support something which involves financial commitments in future budgets. there is no need to rush through a decision on future cultural capitals at this stage. first, the 2007-2013 budgetary plan must be examined in its entirety, after which this report can be considered. the idea of a european capital of culture is a good one. it is even so good that it can be financed in a variety of other ways, including local sponsorship. financial resources from the european union should not be necessary for the survival of this project. we therefore vote against this report as such, but we are not opposed to the idea of action itself.
in writing. i want a more proactive competition policy, but i don't want supranationality or it to be in the constitution. that is why i chose to abstain, which is the vote corrected for a mistake in the minutes.
in writing. the junilists are in favour of the completion of the internal market and support common rules on subsidies for public services and fair procurement requirements, including for public services. in principle, public support should also be accounted for and readily available. the only exception to this rule is where a member state has chosen to provide the service only through a monopoly and does not intend to allow private operators at all (for example, a member state which only allows public healthcare). however, junilista believes that the responsibility must be primarily at member state level. if it is found that a member state is abusing its position and distorting competition, it is important that the party who may be disadvantaged can have his case tried in court. the junilista has therefore voted against the proposal that the data should be reported to the commission.

for the group. i thank you for your reply, commissioner borg, and i wish you the best of luck in your fight in the fao. nobody makes any money off bycatch. it is our duty to find ways of reducing them. fishermen are forced to discard fine cod caught in excess of quotas when caught as by-catch. the rules shall be set so that no fish is caught before it has been able to reproduce at least once. increasing the minimum size may be a win-win situation for some species as we increase reproduction, biomass and catch in the long term. we must then ensure that the mesh sizes of the nets are adapted to these minimum sizes. it is also time to pay more attention to by-catches of non-commercial fish. international opinion is growing against these catches, especially of yellow marine mammals, but also of other species. the rules have focused on commercial species, but for the well-being of the ecosystems and commercial species, we must also conserve and protect non-commercial species. increased selectivity and research on how to reduce these by-catches is necessary. it is important to remember in this context that avoiding one species must not lead to an increase in by-catch of another species. if we do not find a solution to these problems, our oceans will soon be as empty as this room is at this moment.

it is also a rogue state, of course. the liberal group regrets this and would very much like to see resolutions on iran and china, because they are the countries which are the worst violators of human rights. we are also very concerned about the situation in darfur and regret that the abuses and genocides there are not being brought before the international criminal court. there are a number of themes and countries that could be addressed and the excellent report by hélène flautres, which we support, mentions the priorities of parliament. i would just like to highlight chechnya, zimbabwe and colombia for the liberal group. it is also very important that the un commissioner for human rights, her staff and various bodies are given sufficient resources to be able to work on the issue. the three themes that the eu will pursue are very good, and we stand behind them. we'd also like to have one about freedom of speech and journalists. last year, more journalists were killed in the line of duty than ever before, and there are many, many more in prison around the world. the courage of these people is absolutely essential for us to find out about human rights abuses and is therefore a prerequisite for the geneva session. so it is our duty not to forget the work they do.
in writing. the commission proposes that the council authorise the member states covered by the eu's common visa, asylum and immigration policy (including sweden) to ratify, in the interests of the community, the ilo convention on identity documents for seafarers. the convention does not require any amendment of the regulation governing the area, which expressly provides for exemptions from the visa requirement for civilian crew members on board ships. it is therefore not reasonable for the member states to require the authorization of the council, a view clearly shared by france, which has already ratified the convention. the junilist therefore sees no obstacle to the member states deciding independently whether to accede to the convention without any authorisation from the eu.
in writing. we believe that it is good to harmonise driving licence rules and the appearance of driving licences within the european union as far as possible. but the commission's proposal for a directive has become too detailed, as have the amendments tabled in the european parliament. we believe that subsidiarity and the principle of country of origin should apply to the rules on motorcycle licences and moped licences. the validity of driving licences and the medical examination of licence holders are also issues where we believe in the principle of mutual recognition and have confidence in the ability of member states to take well-considered and wise decisions. it is often said that the member states must trust each other in the area of laws and regulations. we believe that this should also apply to driving licence regulations. we are voting against this report because we believe that the harmonization of driving licence regulations can be achieved without this detailed proposal for a directive. in addition, there is already a driving licence directive which has regulated the appearance of driving licences issued within the union.
in writing. the june list votes against the proposal on the recognition of seafarers' certificates. the government office fact memorandum (2003/04:fpm113) notes that there has been a 'significant increase in the number of fraud cases related to the certification procedure', and the maritime administration also expresses concern that the right to impose the necessary conditions will disappear if this directive is adopted. the junilists are generally in favour of the principle of the country of origin and support the idea of removing the obstacles which impair the functioning of the internal market. in this particular case, however, there are strong indications that the european union is not yet ready to take this step.
in writing. the moderate delegation in the european parliament voted today in favour of the ries report on the european environment and health action plan 2004-2010. we believe, however, that it is wrong for the european parliament to call on the commission to take initiatives on lifestyle-related diseases or various measures against smoking. decisions on these issues are a matter for the member states and should ultimately be made mainly by individuals and not be subject to political decision-making. we moderates believe that the eu is an important tool in the quest for a better environment, but the eu's responsibility is limited. the eu should only be responsible for environmental issues which are clearly transnational. it is therefore wrong to call on the commission to promote the eco-labelling of building materials, the drawing up of lists of dangerous workplaces and the establishment of environmental ambulances.
in writing. the health effects of environmental pollution are a key societal issue requiring broad engagement. we have therefore voted in favour of the report as a whole. we are, however, critical of the fact that the report does not stress the principle of subsidiarity. a european environment action plan shall exclusively include transboundary environmental issues. unfortunately, this is not the case with the present action plan. we do not believe that the eu should work to combat ill health caused by tobacco, alcohol, poor diet and lack of exercise (paragraph 17) nor does the commission work to combat smoking in enclosed spaces (paragraph 20). the above paragraphs deal with areas with obvious health consequences. however, these are typical issues where the eu does not have competence and where the principle of subsidiarity must be applied. we also do not think that the subsidiarity principle applies in paragraph 28, which emphasizes that special attention should be paid to people living near polluted installations. finally, it is important that the commission clarifies the total amount to be released for the financing of the action plan and whether it considers that a new financial instrument should be set up for the financing. the costs of the action plan shall be included within the limits set by the budget.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the hunger and injustice in the world are issues of great importance. what factors make the eu the most appropriate international actor in this matter? rather, the eu's unfair trade rules and protectionist agricultural policies have contributed to global poverty. while the eu contributes economic resources in the form of development aid to poor countries, it prevents these countries from achieving growth through a bizarre agricultural and trade policy. this is inconsistent and not very credible. many developing countries see the eu as a problem maker rather than a problem solver. the united nations, on the other hand, is a more appropriate organization for combating poverty and hunger. the un has a broader global footing and more member countries. the eu is still too dominated by national interests. let us fight together for a more just world. the division of responsibilities between the eu and the un could be as follows: within the framework of the eu, we can work on reforming agricultural policy and try to achieve a fairer trade policy. through the united nations, the nations of the world can channel financial and other aid to people who are starving and in need. let us avoid unnecessary institutional power struggles in our common goal of ending poverty.
for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. with the abolition of quantitative import controls for the teak industry, there is great concern about the future of the teak industry in the eu. there is reason to put this in a historical perspective. as a young student of economics, i wrote an essay in 1962 about the sharp decline of the swedish tea industry after world war ii. the reason was the fierce competition from low-wage countries such as germany and italy. i felt that the structural change in the industry had been fortunate. workforce had moved to more well-paid jobs in the engineering industry and the surviving tech industry had moved to more advanced products. there was no unemployment and the trade unions had been active in the structural change. it all seemed like an economic and social sunshine story. during the 1960's and 1970's, this process continued. the surviving swedish teak companies then moved a large part of their production to low-wage countries such as finland and eventually portugal but kept design and marketing in sweden. special products such as men's suits and car clothes were still made in sweden, and were profitable. today we laugh at the idea that advanced finland, nokialand, still appeared to be such a low-wage country in the late 1960s. today, there is very little left of the production of textile products in sweden, but large swedish clothing chains employ a multitude of people to design and market clothes around the world. we've had ten years to adjust. if the eu is to become a leading, dynamic and knowledge-based region, our socioeconomic system must be able to compensate for a shrinking tech industry by shifting to more sophisticated tech products and by strong growth in other sectors. otherwise, the lisbon agenda will be a soap bubble that will be punctured.

for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. first, let us agree that everyone wants to provide the fastest possible assistance in the most efficient way possible. it was a nice idea to export old fishing boats, but it's not working. this is a clear case of the emperor is naked. everyone has realized that the idea does not work, but no one dares to say it publicly and scrap the proposal. the fao has not received any request for boats. on the contrary, both indonesia and sri lanka have rejected such offers. in addition, a conference of regional fisheries organisations met last week in sumatra, with representatives from all the affected countries. they're opposed to the export of old ships. they would rather have support to build up their local yards and their fishing fleets. that's what we have to help them with. our amendments are at any rate intended to reduce the risk that this export of boats will destroy the seabed and the local economy and damage fish stocks with the wrong equipment. i hope the commission will follow its good spirit, but your voting recommendations could have been more generous. the council! is there anyone from the council here? you can see the emperor is naked and scrap this proposal. let go of the prestige, don't have a european perspective, but see it from the side of the victims.
for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. when you first hear this proposal, it sounds very sympathetic that boats left in the eu should be sent to the disaster areas where so many ships have been destroyed. but when you think about it a second time, you start to think about all the difficulties that arise. on the third point, the question arises whether this is not creating more problems than it solves and, definitely, whether it is not costing more than it tastes. first of all: what's in it for you? if the equivalent of this total cost were used to produce boats locally, it would have a much more positive effect on local fisheries. it would also have the effect of supporting the local economy and the local shipyard industry which needs support in this disaster situation. second, the practical. is it really that practical to collect and transport these boats to this region? how will distribution and distribution be handled? third, consider the ecology. it's about different types of fishing, different types of gear, different catches. the risk is that you will have less sustainable fishing and less ecological fishing if you send a very different type of boat than the one traditionally used in the area. fourthly, the commission do the countries that are supposed to receive these boats really want them? the answer to this is what we have received from the un agency, the fao, which coordinates this, that 'no, they do not want to'. no request for these boats has been received, but instead they want to be able to build their own boats, with their local tradition and their local technology. after thinking about it once, twice, three times, it becomes quite obvious that this was a good idea, which, on reflection, should be abandoned. the proposal could certainly be improved by adopting the amendments, particularly those tabled by the greens. then that's a better suggestion. but the wisest thing would be for the commission to realise the situation, withdraw the proposal and instead try to get the member states to give at least an equivalent amount to build boats locally in the disaster-stricken countries. it would be of greater benefit to those whom we all want to help, namely the victims of the terrible tsunami disaster.
for the group. mr president, i would like to thank you. on christmas eve, one of the greatest natural disasters of modern times occurred. hundreds of thousands lost their lives, millions lost loved ones and their livelihoods. the fact that the world has responded with a historically unique solidarity and generosity has been a ray of hope in the midst of this tragedy. the fishing industry was particularly hard hit by the tsunami. the survivors have often lost the means of their livelihood - the boat. the proposal to provide assistance to countries affected by the tsunami with eu ships is therefore morally laudable, but it is a drop in the bucket that makes it almost ridiculous. a much better alternative would be to buy boats in the affected countries and in other countries in the vicinity, adapted to local purposes and needs. this would also mean a much needed local economic injection. the role of the eu as an international actor in these contexts is also not self-evident. there is a tendency for forces within the eu to use disasters to advance their positions. new eu authorities, programmes and measures are being proposed and those who question this development appear, at this historic moment, to be lacking in solidarity. the truth is, however, that in these situations the eu countries should often coordinate their financial and material resources through other international bodies, particularly in the framework of un cooperation. this is the case, for example, of the urgent establishment of a tsunami warning system in the indian ocean of the kind already in place in the pacific. i am opposed to the use of international tragedies to give the eu new powers. the eu's competences should be discussed in the context of the principle of subsidiarity in normal circumstances and not decided by quick decisions in disaster situations. the european peoples' commitment to global solidarity must not be misused to undermine the principle of subsidiarity.
in writing. the conflict and general developments in côte d'ivoire constitute a major political issue. the united nations is involved in this conflict and has decided by resolution 1572 (2004) to impose restrictive measures against côte d'ivoire. this resolution was adopted by the un security council. the un is and should be involved in international affairs. the junilists do not believe that the eu countries should coordinate their respective commitments in the un within the framework of eu cooperation. there is a general tendency for the eu to try to move its foreign policy positions forward. we oppose this development and advocate a national foreign policy and international cooperation within the un. our basic position is that the eu should not be involved or engaged in foreign policy matters. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. the proposal for a directive on unfair commercial practices aims to bring about greater harmonisation of the rules on unfair commercial practices which harm the economic interests of consumers. the directive aims to improve the functioning of the internal market and to strengthen consumer protection. the proposal prohibits unfair business practices. we are fully behind the implementation of the internal market and good consumer protection. the reason why we cannot support the proposal for a directive in its present form is that it risks leading to sweden not being able to maintain its ban on television advertising directed at children in the long term. we do not want to contribute to the weakening of the existing rules on advertising to children and have therefore decided to abstain in the final vote on the bresson report as a whole.
in writing. the tsunami disaster is a great international tragedy in which global solidarity and generosity are desirable. we have voted yes to the motion. we believe, however, that the un is the international body best placed to provide humanitarian aid in the event of international disasters. the un has a broader international footing and more experience than the eu in dealing with crisis situations. we are critical of the eu taking every opportunity to move forward and strengthen its position and influence. there are also good reasons to question whether the most appropriate aid is to send ships from eu countries to the countries affected by the tsunami. an alternative contribution would be to buy the vessels locally. this would facilitate relief efforts and help develop the local economy in the countries affected by the tsunami.
in writing. the european parliament voted today on a proposal to transfer small fishing vessels no longer used within the eu to fishing communities affected by the tsunami. i fully agree with the importance of the recovery of the fisheries sector in the affected countries. however, i do not think that the transfer of fishing vessels, in line with the proposal for a regulation, is the right way forward. the transfer of a number of fishing boats will not cover the existing needs. in addition, support for the fisheries sector in the affected countries should be adapted to local needs and local demand.

in writing. the junilista group votes against the own-initiative report on health and safety at work. the own-initiative report deals with important issues and the junilist supports many of the basic ideas proposed. a good working environment is important for all employees. the junilista group, however, believes that each member state must be allowed to choose how it wants to achieve a good working environment. for example, in the swedish case, it is a matter of negotiation between the social partners and not a matter that should be regulated by eu legislation.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to begin by making a quick analysis of the world to show what it looks like in the europe for which we are jointly responsible. we have the very large oil-producing countries in the middle east, where there is unrest and where oil resources are limited. on the other side of us we have a very large country, russia, where putin has seen that energy is power and is gathering more and more of this power in the form of the various energy companies in russia in his own hand, literally in the kremlin. we have ten new member states which are very dependent on energy imports from russia. we also have old member states, such as germany, where half of the gas imports come from russia. in the field of security policy, this is a very unbalanced and worrying situation. we also have huge environmental problems, which we all agree must be solved, and very ambitious environmental targets. at the same time, energy prices are rising. the consumption curve is rising. european industry needs more and more energy, as do transport and households. as i have already mentioned, the import dependence of some countries is enormous. we have a huge problem with the power grid. in sweden we have had a storm which has left up to 50 000 households, and of course also industry and small businesses in the affected areas, without electricity for more than two months after this storm, which is completely unacceptable. we also remember when all of italy went dark for many hours. so we have an instability in the energy grids that is not acceptable. the european union has defined the so-called ten projects as extremely important, but we see them being stalled in area after area and not having the pace of expansion that was in the original decisions. all in all, we are therefore in a very worrying situation. as far as energy policy in europe is concerned, we must stabilise the supply of energy. we must ensure that the transmission networks are functioning and stable, that companies invest in new technologies, so that simple storms or similar events do not cause long-term power cuts. we need to make sure that the ten projects work. so my first question is: how does the commission ensure that energy supply is stabilised? we also need to transform europe's energy supply, reform which means that biomass could be produced on parts of agricultural land in europe that are currently being taken out of use. we have an industry that needs energy. we also have environmental and employment objectives to meet. secondly, i would like to know how the various parts of the commission are working together to find a solution to these problems. thirdly, we need to revitalise the energy supply, i.e. produce more. my question is what is being done to ensure that european basic industry, through district heating, district heating, co-generation, etc., also produces more energy.
