the rapporteur's alternate. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by expressing my regret that the rapporteur, mr proinsias de rossa, is unable to be present. he is the member who has worked on this matter and knows it best, but unfortunately he is ill at home in ireland and cannot take part in this debate. i am therefore replacing him, which is not difficult, because both my group and i share the views of mr de rossa on the position to be taken at the second reading of parliament. we think we should say yes! let me begin by saying something about the meaning of regulation 1408/71. it is essential for free movement within the eu to make it easier for citizens to move between different member states while benefiting from different social benefits. since its entry into force in the 1970s, this regulation has been subject to a thorough review. in addition, improvements have been made during the journey. improvements have been made, which is also the reason for the rapporteur's position. when we discussed the matter in committee, there was a large majority in favour of us agreeing to the council's common position. the vote was 30 for and 1 against. if i am not mistaken, this 'no' vote was from someone who, in general, does not like free movement and cooperation within the eu. there was therefore a high degree of agreement in the committee and in the adoption of the rapporteur's position. since then there has been a change in the original position, namely on paragraph 2, which is that we knew already during the committee procedure that there was a disagreement between the commission and the council on some specific benefits. we have therefore asked the legal service of the parliament to amend paragraph 2. the commission has therefore tabled an amendment to eliminate this legal uncertainty, with the support of its group and the greens and gue/ngl group. the rapporteur's position remains the same. there is also an amendment which seeks to reject the common position. i would argue, however, that we should follow the rapporteur's recommendation. firstly, we should not delay this process any further. we know that there has been a parallel review of the regulation itself. the implementing regulation is also now to be reviewed. it would not help this process if parliament were to reject the common position. secondly, if we look at the whole picture and what we said at the first reading, the council has indeed taken very much into account the views expressed by parliament, indeed very much so. the two points i am now raising, namely that we should not delay the process and that we have been largely satisfied, mean that we should accept the common position, but with the amendment tabled to avoid legal uncertainty. as regards the benefits on which the commission and the council differ, this should be decided by the judicial authorities, if the commission so wishes. we must not prejudge the decision that may be taken by the judicial authorities. i do not see that it is in anyone's interest to delay this process or to reject the common position. it is not in the interest of the commission, it is not in the interest of the council, it is not in the interest of parliament and it is certainly not in the interest of the citizens.

in writing. junilista supports the creation of a well-functioning internal market for natural gas that facilitates market access for new players. the establishment of basic principles and rules governing the conditions of access to gas networks, leading to increased competition in the eu and, consequently, lower gas prices, is thus positive, as long as incentives for investment in infrastructure and the assumption of business risks are maintained by guaranteeing reasonable levels of compensation for access.
in writing. the free movement of persons within the internal market is one of the greatest achievements and advantages of the european union and it naturally requires a well-functioning common labour market. at the same time, however, the right of member states to choose their welfare model must be respected. some choose a combination of high taxes, large public service production and generous subsidies, others choose low taxes and a more rudimentary welfare model. it is not possible to allow european citizens to opt for low taxes in one country and at the same time take with them the right to generous subsidies and aid from another country. if this is allowed, the member states will be forced to adopt the same welfare model. the ability of the peoples of europe to choose their future is undermined in yet another area. the junilist wants to see a proper review of the growing influence of eu rules in social and labour market policy. we therefore vote against the report which calls for further restrictions on the freedom of action of the member states in some of the most central areas of democratic economic policy.
in writing. we support the report on the social situation in the european union. however, we voted against the demand that the commission withdraw its proposal for a directive on services in the internal market, partly because the demand is unrealistic and partly because we believe that we can influence the content of the directive in the european parliament. as regards the harmonisation of the status of paid employment with its associated rights and guarantees, in particular in the field of social protection and lifelong learning, we assume that such harmonisation concerns minimum levels. as for introducing measures such as a 'minimum income for life', we interpret it as referring to what we in sweden call social contributions and not the introduction of a minimum wage system.

mr president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. i agree with the reasons for your action on computer-related patents. my question is about the services directive. the commission's role is to be the guardian of the treaties, after all, is it not? yet, today, on 8 march, international women's day, the commission is taking the whole health sector out of the services directive, i.e. a sector dominated by women. if you have multiple employers to choose from, we know that the chance of higher wages is much greater. in this context, you will therefore try to limit the opportunities for women to receive higher wages, so that they have one or very few employers. it is a major reason to get higher wages and to be able to have several employers! what does the commission intend to do about this? will the commission put forward a new proposal in the health field? it is indeed the commission's role to give the freedoms of the treaty to all europeans - women and men!
mr president, i would like to thank you. there are many people here today who have spoken out against the services directive. i therefore think it is important to state that it is the most significant single reform we have today for european integration and for giving new impetus to the lisbon process. we need not just a services directive but a directive which will increase trade in services across europe's borders. the service sector accounts for 70% of the european economy. we will never have the most competitive knowledge economy in the world if we do not open up to the service sector. it is against this background that there is reason to be concerned about what the commissioner has said, since large parts of the services sector are excluded. it is important to analyse the question: how much of the european services sector will be left after the exemptions that are now being made? half of europe has had the privilege of living in freedom and peace for 50 years and has been able to develop a highly knowledge-based service sector; we have that competitive advantage. another part of europe has other competitive advantages. how will the commissioner ensure that all countries in europe have competitive advantages through the principle of country of origin and through free and open trade in services? these are crucial questions for our success.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i think the commission's position on the services directive is in the right direction; the criticisms have been heard. however, there are some ambiguities, particularly in relation to the labour market. do you agree with me that it is not enough to exempt by the posting of workers directive, but that the whole labour market must be exempted, and then the host country principle should apply? secondly, if the labour market is excluded and the host country principle applies, environmental standards, consumer law, etc., how can a directive be regarded as the fundamental principle of the country of origin? these are perhaps the most important areas where the host country principle should apply. the third question i would like to ask concerns public services. i think it is good that health and social services should be excluded. can you be clear about the areas you are referring to when you talk about the scope of the directive?
i share the view that there has been improvement in the agricultural policy. however, i do not think that the improvements are sufficient. there are also complaints from the poorer part of the world against the agricultural policies of the eu and the us, because we do not provide access to markets and dump prices on the world market. i would like to see further improvements, because i believe that fair world trade is just as important as aid policy. do you share my view that further changes are needed to benefit the poor parts of the world? if so, how do you propose to achieve this?
i agree with the previous speaker that it is urgent to make the eu more efficient. however, in the context of modernising the eu institutions and implementing the good governance practices that commissioner kallas talks about, we must also demand more from the tax money we spend. such efficiency would be possible if all eu decisions were actually implemented. i've come to understand that a lot of the decisions we make here aren't. this could be important measures to combat environmental degradation or legislation to improve equality and fairness in europe. my question is how the commission intends to work to ensure that the decisions taken by the eu institutions are also implemented and become reality and improvements in people's everyday lives.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i feel very uncomfortable with the whole preference system as such. the fact that we are reducing trade barriers for poor countries is of course a good thing, because trade contributes to freedom, development and, not least, to the prosperity that poor countries so badly need. the sad thing is that a special system of preferences for the poor countries is needed in the first place. that europe's general trade policy has such consequences that we have to make exceptions to it for the poor so that they can sleep well at night. experience shows that the preferential system has been largely symbolic. few poor countries have taken advantage of it, not least because it has been so complicated. the aim must of course be to achieve the liberalization of trade policy so that we do away with the preferential system altogether. the european union, which is founded on the wise insight of the beneficial effects of free trade, should dare to take the plunge. if we do not, how can trade and the world become freer? however, in dealing with this matter, i doubted whether some members had not forgotten the basis of free trade on which european cooperation was built. here too, several attempts were made to protect european sugar production and the textile industry, which already operate behind high customs barriers - all at the expense of producers in poor countries. i voted against these attempts, of course, but it still tells us that we have a long way to go. for my part, i would like to see the new preferential system go further, eliminating already low duties, improving the opportunities for regional processing and simplifying the system further. in conclusion, i would like to reiterate that i hope that in the not too distant future we will reach a point where european trade policy is so free, open and unhindered that we do not really need any preferential system. the european trade policy must be imbued with the same basic idea that created the union, free trade, otherwise europe will not live as europe actually teaches.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank mr bowis for his report. there is much to be said for acp cooperation and for the work of mr bowi in strengthening the acp-eu assembly. given the concern of our colleagues in the acp countries about budgetary matters, i think it is important to strengthen the role of the acp-eu assembly in this area. if it is acceptable to your colleagues, i would like to propose a small amendment to paragraph 11 to further strengthen the text. the incorporation of the european development fund into the eu budget 'should be accompanied by a strengthening' of the role of the jpa, rather than 'should enable strengthening', which is becoming clearer. we are expressing a clear desire to ensure that our friends from the south are given a say. the low attendance at the brussels committee meetings is a major problem. last time, we were just a handful of eu nationals, which disappointed our guests. one representative from africa summed up: in africa it is considered impolite to invite guests in through one door and to leave through the other. this situation is damaging our relations with other countries. we are forced to choose between important votes on legislation and important cooperation with the outside world. mr president, i therefore ask the conference of presidents to reconsider its decision and allow us to hold acp meetings at some point during the weeks of the political groups' meetings, when no legislative votes are held and we can decide our agendas internally in the groups.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i, too, would like to thank john bowis for a job well done. it is also clear that the work of the joint parliamentary assembly has developed positively in recent times. we have had much more focused discussions, which is largely due to the fact that we have a system where we work in committees and prepare the cases. it is also a positive change that we have meetings in different eu countries. however, there are several problems which we must address in the near future. as carl schlyter mentioned, our attendance at committee meetings is disastrously low. i fully support the call for these meetings to be held during the weeks of the political groups' meetings instead, so that we can have a little more presence. some acp countries still send diplomats instead of parliamentarians, which is not very good. we also need better follow-up on many of the decisions we take. a very large part of the work of the acp-eu joint assembly is concerned with human rights and the resolution and prevention of armed conflicts. it is therefore striking that the international criminal court (icc) is not mentioned once in the report, even though it is one of the most important tools we have in this field. we have worked with the icc on several occasions, but we could develop that work considerably. it is one of the most important and effective tools we have to hold those who commit genocide and war crimes to account. we have a common interest, the eu and several acp countries, which have set up this court and which run it. the first two cases brought before the court concern uganda and the congo, which are acp countries. the biggest obstacle to the development of the criminal justice system today is the fierce opposition of the united states. we can see how the united states today is actively punishing acp countries in the caribbean, africa and the pacific who dare to sign the agreement on the criminal court. we in this house should do more to defend these countries, politically but also economically, when the united states withdraws its aid as punishment.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to limit my comments to paragraph 28 of the dombrovskis report, namely the one concerning the costs of having to work one week a month in strasbourg instead of concentrating its activities in brussels alone. the direct costs of this are estimated at eur 200 million per year. it does not include the loss of efficiency or the extra costs of members. the total cost would be at least close to eur 300 million per year. given the eu's lisbon objective of becoming the most competitive knowledge-based region in the world, the european parliament's unwillingness and inability to shut down strasbourg seems absurd. voters cannot respect a political leadership that cannot even cope with such a self-evident change, which the business world must always cope with. i do not think it is reasonable to include the eu's long-term financial perspective in an accumulated cost of eur 2 billion to maintain double jobs. alternatively, these funds could be used for technical research or other growth-enhancing activities. it is not possible to argue for a larger budget as long as this anachronism persists. therefore, the long-term budget should include this and a decision should be taken to move the operations to brussels by 2013.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i also hope, of course, that the commission will take the information from romania seriously. let us not be lulled into any confidence, because there will be different messages from different governments. we have seen that the inventiveness of the exploitation of women's bodies is great in every way. the debate today, a good number of hours ago, dealt with the fourth world conference on women. many members of parliament, the commissioner responsible and council representatives condemned the trafficking of women which was then being debated. now, many hours later, the debate is once again about plans to further exploit women's bodies through the trade in eggs. a cynical proposal such as buying and selling eggs should not be possible in a civilized humanist society. if this were allowed, one could consider the continuation of such a buying and selling market. what other organs should be able to be bought and sold on a market? now, of course, i am not talking about voluntary and unpaid donations of human cells and tissues. it is something quite different from this marketplace. i hope that the commission will take this seriously and that a unanimous parliament will agree to these calls for the commission to take all possible measures to prevent what i see as an illegal act. trade in eggs for payment shall not be allowed! it has to be unambiguous!
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the so-called kok report argues that there are synergies. one euro spent by the eu on development projects can be worth much more than the equivalent amount spent by individual member states. eu projects can then reduce the need for government funding to individual member states. increased eu funding therefore need not lead to increased tax burdens. in the case of large projects, such as space and fusion power, there may be economies of scale in running the projects at eu level. i also believe that the lisbon process should be financed mainly by the member states themselves. the lisbon process must not become the launching pad for a large number of new projects at eu level that require funding from the member states. in general, i think it is an important question whether national initiatives can be replaced by eu projects. i wonder if there's any empirical evidence for the existence of such effects. my experience seems to indicate the opposite effect. the more the eu invests in the development of its bureaucracy, the more legislation and the more directives, the more the public sector in the member states grows. for each new official in brussels, the total number of officials in the member states increases by a factor of 5, 10 or 25. the so-called public choice theory expects each national bureaucracy to seek to maximise its influence and role. the more the eu takes over from the bureaucracy of the member states, the more it expands. the reasons for this are many: the new directives must be implemented, controls must be tightened up, and politicians who have lost their right of initiative are looking for new areas. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. as an example of this process, i would like to mention what happened when the euro was introduced. as the national central banks lost their proper function of conducting monetary policy, their staffs increased considerably. similar phenomena can be observed in several areas. i believe that the commission should take the initiative of allowing independent researchers, economists and political scientists to study the way in which the bureaucratic multiplier works within the eu. it is important to stop this process, including through fewer legislative directives and more voluntary agreements. if this is not done, the most important growth sector is likely to be the public bureaucracy. growth risks being held back by its own ambitions to harmonize, legislate, intervene in every conceivable issue, etc. the eu should therefore instead make more use of the institutional competition inherent in differences.
mr president, five years after the launch of the lisbon strategy it is clear that we need to give it a fresh start. this new start should not, however, follow the line of the commission proposal. i am not critical about what is contained in the proposal, but rather what is not in the proposal. separating economic growth from social and environmental development must be a mistake since these three areas are linked. when president barroso presented the mid-term review in this parliament more than a month ago he used the metaphor of having three children and having to pay most attention to the sick one. the other two, in this case environmental and social development, will have to await their turn for president barroso’s attention. my conclusion is the opposite. to cure the eu economy we need the health of the whole family. investment in environmental technology leads to more jobs, boosts innovation and creates sustainable growth. when the forestry and pulp industry in the nordic countries made a major shift towards cleaner green technology, the companies concerned became even more competitive on the international market. new technologies for saving energy are another example of business interests being in tune with environmental concerns. there is a saying that competition brings out the best in products and the worst in people. this is definitely true of business, and it is important to create an internal market that is a level playing field. we need to create a competitive and sustainable economy in europe; this also means investing in research and development. the heads of state must take the situation seriously. we can set up ambitious goals, but if we lack the necessary political leadership we will never reach them. in europe we look at the situation in the us and wonder why it has double the growth that we have. the us wonders why china has twice the growth it has. the eu has a great potential to be the world’s leading knowledge-based economy. the proposals from the commission are good, but to reduce the lisbon strategy only to a matter of economic growth would be to take steps in the wrong direction while time is running out. the major groups have negotiated this resolution and the clear message is that economic growth, social cohesion and environmental development have to be seen as a whole. we demand the same from the council and the commission. i want to thank my fellow negotiators because it is a major achievement to table this resolution – i almost said revolution! – and to have a large majority in the chamber.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i am glad that we seem to have a large majority in the european parliament for this resolution. a large majority is the only way for parliament to exert political influence. i believe that our influence lies in the fact that in our resolution we have managed to maintain what is fundamental to the lisbon process, namely the balance between the three pillars of growth, social cohesion and ecological sustainability. in the original commission document i do not think that was achieved. it was said that we need growth to cope with welfare and ecological sustainability. that is true, but the opposite is also true, namely that we need social cohesion and long-term sustainable investment to cope with long-term growth. it's the timing that counts. if we in parliament can contribute to this at the council meeting and if it becomes the policy of the future, then we have succeeded.
madam president, i would like to thank you. in the light of the shortcomings of the lisbon process, it is worth stressing that the key questions are not what we want to achieve but how. it's not about others being better at certain things, it's about why. even when we take pride in the social model, we see unemployment, exclusion, stagnation and contradictions. we must also ask ourselves why and what decisions we are going to take to change this. what avril doyle just said is true. for every area where we counteract competition, the result is less competitiveness. those who are now fighting the services directive with a free and open labour market are in fact opposing the new jobs of the future and european growth. there is no contradiction between the internal market and the social security of citizens, since nothing is more essential to social security than a job and an income. nothing is more crucial to social cohesion than the possibility of new businesses and new jobs. the most important process of integration in the european union is taking place as we sit here. it is a flow of people, goods and services that carries knowledge and ideas across borders. we must then ask ourselves whether we want to enable all member states to exploit their various competitive advantages in this process. the old member states have had the privilege of developing their social economies and competitiveness in freedom and peace for 50 years. the new member states have suffered oppression and poverty during the same period. the single market must be characterised by the fact that everyone can exploit his or her competitiveness. it is the commission's duty to see and highlight that this is about solidarity, integration and the possibility for everyone to create welfare. that is why the services directive is important, and that is why it is important to remember that it is about integration in europe which is crucial for competitiveness. we have to do that.
mr president, i have an oral amendment. i would like the second half of paragraph 11 to read as follows: ‘stresses that inclusion of the edf in the budget of the european union should be accompanied by reinforcement of the political role of the jpa in monitoring the funds committed to the framework of the acp-eu partnership’.
in writing. the undersigned have voted in favour of the report as a whole, as it contains in many parts important improvements to the commission's original proposal. (for example, the elimination of the requirement for double processing, etc.) ) however, we object to amendment no 31 (article 13 (1)), because we believe that the gsp is an instrument for reducing poverty in the world and should not be used to protect our european textile industry.
in writing. this motion for a resolution is an unmanageable mass of text in which everything that civilized people would like to see implemented is mentioned without the slightest regard for the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. no one can be against the fight against poverty and unemployment and the speedy abolition of them. no one can object to taxes being uncomplicated and fair rather than complicated and unfair. however, the principle of subsidiarity, which is to be the guiding principle of european cooperation, means that the member states should be free to seek different balances in the conflicts of objectives which the democratic form of government has to deal with. the underlying aim of this proposal is to shift even more of the political decision-making from the member states to the eu institutions. the junilista has a mandate from its voters to oppose such a centralisation of power in europe and therefore rejects the proposal as a whole. we have also rejected several amendments which we would in principle support in the swedish parliament but which we oppose at eu level because of the concern for popular government in the member states. this is the case, for example, with amendment no 32.
in writing. the resolution on the lisbon strategy contains a lot of good things. however, i would have liked to see an even stronger emphasis on the importance of 'policy integration', i.e. that investment in growth must be made in ways that carefully weigh up the social and environmental consequences. these three dimensions of development must be mutually supportive. the idea of 'growth first, then we'll take care of the rest' is wrong and represents an unfortunate return to the debate before the adoption of the eu's sustainability strategy in gothenburg in 2001. the eu is facing a number of difficult environmental challenges. the risk of irreversible changes in the climate system is particularly serious. to address this problem, we need to re-engineer europe's energy and transport systems. this transition must be achieved primarily through a strong focus on innovation and the development of new technologies. such an initiative would stimulate growth and create new jobs. at the same time, european companies would develop excellence in a field where the whole world is crying out for new solutions. the commission proposal and the parliament's resolution both place sufficient emphasis on these aspects. i hope that the european council at its meeting on 22 march will give much more attention to these matters.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i am glad that we have this debate, which has been initiated by the alde group. the situation with the bulgarian nurses and the palestinian doctor is truly a nightmare. for six long years these men, who have dedicated their lives to saving lives and caring for fellow humans, have been imprisoned in libya under appalling conditions. the situation is kafkaesque. the charges against them are baseless, as scientists have also proved. on 29 march a court will finally decide on the death sentences of these six people, which is why this date is of course extremely critical. the eu must do everything possible to secure the immediate release of those concerned. in just a few years, bulgaria will be a member of the european union. we therefore have a responsibility to these citizens. in the process of gradual rapprochement between the eu and libya under the barcelona process, this issue is a critical test of the direction in which relations between us can go. if the death sentences are carried out, i believe that future cooperation is impossible. at the same time, of course, it is a terrible tragedy for the children infected with aids and their families. the eu can do more. i am very pleased that the commissioner supports the action plan and sees opportunities for the eu to support libya in this way, so that there can be better care in the future for people living with hiv. mr president, this is a very important question. we expect the council and the commission to give it priority and to follow very, very closely the developments until 29 march when the court will again give its opinion on the case.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. when we take a position in this house on various issues, we must always consider whether these should really be decided at eu level. the principle of subsidiarity is not something to be referred to only in solemn contexts, but it should be the guiding principle of all our activities. we all know that this is not the way things work in brussels and strasbourg. political power is continually shifting from the member states to the eu institutions. the driving forces are twofold: the quest for a superstate and political unprincipledness. a majority of eu deputies and officials want to create a new superstate. for them, the principle of subsidiarity is not a guiding light but an inconvenient obstacle. the political unprincipledness means that the members of the house are usually prepared to participate in decisions that they like, even when it is obvious that these are strictly national matters. the end justifies the means. the locatelli report also completely ignores the principle of subsidiarity. it does not ask what the eu should do, since the member states cannot do it themselves. the question is how the eu can promote the research that an establishment wants. how are we to deal with the research needs of smes? how can we get young people and women interested in science? how can we strengthen the links between science and industry? how are we to bring total research expenditure up to 3% of gdp, and so on? all of these are matters which the member states are better placed to deal with themselves. finland and sweden already allocate 3% of gdp to research. if other countries want to set aside as much, it's up to them to do so. there will be no more money for research because the member states send it through the eu authorities. but it's more bureaucracy and more costs. competition between countries to find good solutions to this type of problem normally leads to faster progress than central governance at higher levels. in the academic world, it's called institutional competition. competition is important, not only between companies, but also between institutions. competition between countries in different fields is one of the main reasons for the great progress made by the west in recent centuries. locatelli should have asked the question: what can the eu institutions do for research in europe that the countries themselves cannot do? there are many such questions. they fall under five headings: basic research, free movement of researchers within the eu, large-scale research in areas such as fusion energy and space technology, research network infrastructure and common security issues for the eu. this is what the eu's research policy should be limited to, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, which is being celebrated in banquets and draft constitutions. the eu's research policy is on the wrong track.
mr president, this shows us that science and research last in the long run. i will continue in swedish. herr talman! den resolution vi har antagit adderar europeiskt mervärde till både forskningspolitik och ekonomisk utveckling. hela den resolution om lissabonstrategin som har antagits av parlamentet i dag med mycket stor majoritet, och som vi från alde-gruppen har lagt ned mycket arbete på, är grundad på kunskapen att en hållbar utveckling bygger på både ekonomisk tillväxt, social sammanhållning och miljömässiga hänsyn. den forskningspolitik och det betänkande som vi diskuterar i dag, hjälper till att bygga en hållbar ekonomisk tillväxt genom att satsa på excellens och teknikplattformar, hjälper till att skapa den sociala sammanhållning vi vill ha, bl.a. mellan män och kvinnor i ett genderperspektiv samt mellan olika regioner. dessutom hjälper de till att producera de medel som behövs för vårt trygghetssystem och ser till att vi kan fortsätta arbeta med miljödriven teknikutveckling. det gläder mig att detta arbete har samma balans som den antagna lissabonresolutionen, och jag ser fram emot ramprogrammet.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i am sure that commissioner frattini has read our questions and will certainly answer them tonight. i am also sure that he knows that parliament is in favour of a balanced approach when law enforcement meets the issue of privacy, as in this case with data retention. we have spent the whole day discussing the lisbon process and how to become more competitive. it is not the right time to impose on companies, authorities and citizens huge costs for which we have no impact assessment, that is to say, no evidence. it seems to me completely unreasonable and illogical to take decisions before they have been established, while at the same time discussing competitiveness and lisbon. it would be unreasonable to let the citizens pay for this surveillance themselves. i believe that it must always be the person who requests the information who pays. otherwise, the big brother society will benefit. moreover, it is not established that this approach is necessary, which must be the case before any decisions can be taken. if the commission makes a proposal, it is logical that the member states should withdraw theirs. i hope and believe that mr frattini understands that it is wise, even necessary, to ensure that the protection of the privacy of citizens in the third pillar is at least as good as that in the first pillar. i share the view of my colleagues that it would be interesting to hear how you intend to deal with this.
mr president, i would like to thank you. on the question of passenger data transfer, i would like to agree with the question marks and criticisms put forward by my colleagues from the green group and the pse group. i myself would like to concentrate my speech on the question of the storage of personal data. the proposal now before the council is a very clear conflict between personal privacy and respect for the private sphere, on the one hand, and the need for surveillance by society, on the other. i think this proposal goes too far as a violation of privacy. it is based on the observation of people without suspicion of crime. it's actually based on some kind of underlying suspicion of everyone. this is in clear violation of the european convention on human rights, in particular article 8 thereof on the protection of private and family life, the protection of one's home and the right to correspondence. the eu member states have signed this council of europe convention with the idea that the eu as an institution should do the same. are we serious about this if we can at the same time pass legislation which is a clear breach of international conventions? this is a key issue of credibility which the commission must address. another important point is the strong criticism of this proposal by the so-called article 29 group. these are some of the leading experts we have in the union on data protection. it would also be interesting to hear the commission comment on the criticism of the article 29 group in this area. it is in crisis that the rule of law is tested, and it is in crisis that the rule of law is most needed to protect citizens, including against unjustified surveillance by society itself. we have seen, after the terrorist attacks in the united states a few years ago, that this border is getting closer and closer as we cross it. it is important to uphold the fundamental values of the rule of law. the commission and the council must also be prepared to debate this legislation in good time. we have discussed these questions too often, too many times without answers, and then it was too late.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the member who honestly declared his interests. today the commission is my ally, even though the reform does not go far enough. sugar policy is driven by big industry and the selfishness of the big farmers. small farmers, consumers and poor countries are affected. the present sugar policy in the eu has a few thousand winners and 450 million losers. when you consider the impact on millions of poor people around the world, today's politics is a scandal. my colleagues on the committee on agriculture are welcome to mali in april to defend today's report before the acp-eu assembly. the committee on development and the committee on agriculture should share responsibility. weakening reform will literally lead to famine and misery. it is hypocritical to defend in today's proposal subsidised sugar production in every corner of the eu while at the same time demanding total reform in all the poor countries. everyone has to do their part. finally, the commission the criticism of the 'everything but arms' initiative is wrong. the problem is not that the 'everything but arms' initiative exists, but that it is not complemented by social considerations, environmental considerations and guarantees for small farmers.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted yes to the resolution, but would like to highlight some problems we see with the text. the resolution regards women in the world as a homogeneous group, which we consider very unfortunate because the situation of women in the various countries differs very dramatically. the situation of women in the third world is being confused with the situation of women in europe as if they were comparable problems. problems such as war rape, honour killings and genital mutilation cannot be compared with the number of father's months, lifelong learning and quotas as has been done in the resolution. the resolution mixes issues that belong at eu level with those that are clearly national issues or purely individual decisions. we would prefer to focus on women in the third world, where women are in a very vulnerable situation. the third world is a major issue which the eu should highlight in its common foreign and security policy and in its aid policy. we had hoped that the resolution would protect equal rights for men and women and defend human rights throughout the world.
in writing. we moderates do not believe that the issue of human egg trafficking should be dealt with in the way that it is now dealt with in a resolution. the resolution we are voting on today has not been the subject of detailed preparation. nor does it illuminate this whole complex issue of inherent moral and ethical considerations. it is not reasonable to call for a categorical ban on the trade in ova and embryos. women who are unable to have children of their own will always be able to make arrangements with other women who are willing to donate eggs. childlessness is a very painful trauma for many. it would be a mistake to criminalise such agreements. the issue of egg donation involves very complex moral considerations. both the question of what interventions in reproduction should be allowed and the consequences for both the donor and the childless who want help need more light than the present resolution provides. the reason why we are voting for the resolution is the situation which is at the root of the resolution, namely the exploitation of poor women in romania, which we take seriously without knowing all the facts.
in writing. nuclear weapons pose, not only in countries like north korea and iran, a serious threat to our global security. the junilists support the call for the limitation of the proliferation of nuclear weapons, which should lead in the long term to the elimination of all nuclear weapons. the united nations (un), with its experience, expertise and global reach, is the best organisation to ensure the success of the 2005 non-proliferation treaty. that is why the un must be the priority in the global disarmament effort. the resolution, however, contains proposals that progressively strengthen the eu's foreign and defence policy role, which risks weakening the un's position.
in writing. we welcome the development of a peaceful and democratic lebanon as an important part of a peaceful and democratic middle east. we are opposed to the constant attempts to strengthen the eu's foreign and defence policy role, but we welcome the fact that individual european countries are taking their responsibilities and are participating in the peace process. this means that the eu is taking resources that would have been better managed by the united nations. this in turn leads to a weakening of the world community.
in writing. i abstained on the vote on hezbollah because 'any necessary action' against terrorist activities would mean that they too were put on the terrorist list. the environment party opposes the terrorist list as it does not have objective criteria and the decisions cannot be appealed, no rights are given to those who are put on the list. hezbollah has many parliamentarians and civil activities and the complex situation of its status in lebanon is not resolved by total struggle against all parts of the organization.
in writing. we abstained on the request that the commission should defend the proposal that the eu budget should amount to well over 1% of gdp. we want to give priority to the lisbon strategy, where research is an important part. however, it is too early to commit ourselves at this stage to the level of the eu budget.
in writing. in view of article 44 the world's most important environmental problem is global warming. the eu has joined the kyoto protocol, whose main objective is to reduce carbon emissions. expansion of nuclear power is one of the most important means of reducing these. that is why i want to support all aspects of eu nuclear research. however, i do not support eu defence research. this is a matter for individual countries to decide or agree among themselves.
in writing. we support a comprehensive reform of the eu sugar market with a view to liberalising it as soon as possible and allowing sugar-producing countries outside the eu to compete on fair terms. the resolution does not take the question of the eu sugar market in this direction, since all the amendments to parliament's resolution tabled by mr van den berg to that effect were rejected. we have therefore voted against the motion for a resolution.
in writing. i believe that it is of the utmost importance that the eu's agricultural policy, including the organisation of the sugar market, be reformed. the current systems cost a lot of money and, in addition, the high level of agricultural subsidies today means that eu products are dumped on the world market, with serious consequences for developing countries. today the european parliament voted on a resolution following an oral question by mr joseph daul and mr jean-claude fruteau. i chose to vote against the resolution as a whole, as the main thrust of the resolution is to preserve the status quo. sugar producers in certain developing countries will also be affected by the reform of the common organisation of the market in sugar. these are now relatively favoured by the present system. however, their situation should not be resolved by a less far-reaching reform of the sugar market. instead, these countries should be supported through other measures such as increased development cooperation and trade technical assistance.
in writing. today the european parliament voted on a resolution following an oral question by mr joseph daul and mr jean-claude fruteau. i chose to vote against the resolution as a whole, as the main thrust of the resolution is to preserve the status quo. reform of the eu's agricultural policy - including the organisation of the sugar market - is of paramount importance. the current system costs the eu taxpayer a lot of money to maintain, and the high level of agricultural subsidies currently means that eu products are dumped on the world market, with serious consequences for the export and production capacity of developing countries. the reform of the common organisation of the market in sugar will affect not only european sugar producers but also producers in certain developing countries, which are currently relatively well served by the current system. however, their situation should not be resolved by a less far-reaching reform of the sugar market. instead, these countries should be supported through increased development cooperation and trade technical assistance - proposed to be drawn from the resources released in the context of the sugar reform. finally, it is important to allow these countries a sufficient transitional period to adapt to the new situation.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the commissioner for his answer to the question. as has been rightly pointed out, the tibetans are being denied their most basic political and democratic rights. what we are seeing, and have seen for a long time, is the systematic destruction of their cultural heritage and of their ability to practice their religion. the example given by the commissioner, namely that monks are imprisoned for having portraits of the dalai lama, is just one example of the absurdity of this oppression. as you know, the eu has said this on several occasions. the question is, is it enough? it would be interesting to hear more about your plans for the future. how to increase pressure on china on the tibet issue? china today is an economic and political giant that is also increasingly becoming a global player. however, we must be clear about china. the positive economic development is in no way matched by a corresponding positive democratic development. we don't see any of that today. on the contrary, part of china's economic progress is based on the systematic denial of the most basic rights to its own workers. the china policy therefore becomes a test of the eu's credibility in human rights issues. economic interests are opposed to the support of the political and democratic rights of tibetans and other chinese. that is the test the eu must pass. it would be completely unreasonable to do what is now being proposed, namely to lift the arms embargo against china. it's giving two signals at once. it is criticising in words, but at the same time showing in action that it wants a different relationship with china and does not care much about the oppression. the eu must take a more consistent stance.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the right to a fair trial is one of the most fundamental human rights. yet this fundamental right is being violated in all our member states. i myself have followed the case of a british citizen in sweden and that of a swedish citizen in greece where the right to interpretation into his mother tongue has not been respected. in the case of calle jonsson, a swedish citizen, the family has been forced to sell their house and borrow €50,000 to cover the costs of interpreting. this is obviously not acceptable in any way. at the same time, there is the whole of the great european convention on human rights and civil liberties. there is extensive case law from the court of justice in strasbourg. what we need to add to the european parliament and the european union is the dimension of compliance with these rules that this parliament, the commission and the council can add. when i listened to the luxembourg representative earlier, i was very concerned because i think it is important that the council representative should speak out in all circumstances for the full respect of human rights and the right to a fair trial. i think the rapporteur has done a very good job. much remains to be done in terms of access to a lawyer, legal translation and minimum standards in general, as well as the protection of the suspect in a trial. it should be understood that a suspect is not always the same as a convicted person. the judicial process is intended to separate the innocent from the guilty, and it is important to ensure that there is sufficient support in this context. today's debate and the decisions we will take in parliament will not be enough, but further action will be required.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would first like to thank all those involved for the work they have helped us with and the willingness to cooperate that has existed among the agencies. discharge is not just a matter of how money is used properly. it is also about how to achieve the objectives of the programmes and how to achieve efficiency in general. i would therefore like to emphasise the gender aspect. we have an obligation to achieve the equality objectives we have set. i have to say it's a scandal that only half the agencies have gender equality plans. this is something they must correct before next year, otherwise i will not give them a discharge. the reforms will be accelerated if we achieve real equality. old hierarchical and patriarchal decision-making systems and working methods will be broken up. today, almost all agencies have 4/5 men in the top positions and 4/5 or more women in the bottom positions. i can see that we in the european parliament are no better. in addition, in many countries we now have a situation where more women than men are entering university studies. there should be a good recruitment base to change this situation. another issue i would like to raise is the european agency for reconstruction. as you know, it was an official who was suspected of helping a company in a procurement of energy infrastructure. here is an example of how a scandal should be handled. the director immediately suspended the official, immediately handed the documents to olaf, which started its investigation quickly and was completed within ten months. the judicial authorities were informed and the proceedings could be started quickly. we are also now waiting for the commission to complete the disciplinary procedure. if we could deal with scandals in this way and stop covering them up and trying to hide them, our institutions would function better. this shows how important transparency is. however efficient the court of auditors may be, it can never discover everything. but if we have a public policy, we have 450 million citizens who can control what everyone does. it is the best preventive measure against fraud and corruption.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. terence wynn has done a brilliant job, as has, for example, the co-rapporteur, carl schlyter. but our parliamentary control system is inadequate. the eu's reputation and legitimacy are very low among the european population, and rightly so. the key words that come to mind for an average european citizen are top management, establishment, waste, corruption. why is it this way? it turns out that institutional control through the court of auditors, olaf and the committee on budgetary control is not enough. it's not working. there continues to be a lot of irregularities and corruption in our system. it also turns out that free speech, democratic debate, journalists digging in, i.e. external auditors of that kind, are not working either, they are not enough. what does it take? it also requires what we call whistle blowers. we need officials in the entire eu machinery, in all bodies and at all levels, who are loyal not to their superiors but to the citizens and taxpayers of europe. we have had whistle blowers, famous ones, rewritten all over the world: van buitenen, mart andreasen, hans-peter martin, the list goes on. how have those who have exposed, called attention to, the fraud in the system fared? usually you lose your job. now we have another example of such a deal in the committee of the regions. i therefore call on parliament to support our amendment no 2 to section vii, paragraph 12, by geoffrey titford, jens-peter bonde and myself. this has to stop.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in most languages there are proverbs that require one to clean one's home before demanding anything from others. that's why it's important that we get our benefits in order. travel allowances are not compensation for wages but travel allowances which should be paid after actual costs. if we want to equalize wages, we will have an extra budget line which is publicly announced. my colleague staes explained the problems with the pension fund, the supplementary pension. it should be self-financed and the deficits covered by those who participate. it is unreasonable for the taxpayers to bear the loss when stock speculation fails and the participants to reap the profits if it succeeds. our general expenses are generous. if you want to get the full amount, you should report how you spent it. those who can't do that should be paid less. it is true that it takes time to report, but it is time well spent to increase the credibility of parliament. during the campaign, i noticed that the section of the information on the rules for our benefits has disappeared from the public website. since it's not classified, it should be published there immediately. it's one of the most common questions you get from your constituents. i hope that the rapporteur's amendment no 6 and the other amendments which strengthen the text will be supported.

and from the point of view of the citizens, completely incomprehensible - part of the union budget: the subsidies for tobacco farming. each year, european taxpayers are forced to pay nearly 10 billion swedish crowns in subsidies to tobacco farmers. it is a terrible waste and a rather scary way of dealing with the taxpayers' money. in addition, the tobacco subsidized by the eu is of such poor quality that no european producer wants to use it. instead, it is used to compete with tobacco companies in poor countries. the grants to tobacco growers are made no less bizarre by the fact that the commission has now launched an anti-smoking campaign estimated to cost sek 700 million. i would call it the height of hypocrisy. in the debate on the commission's discharge, parliament can make it clear that tobacco subsidies must be abolished. but unfortunately, with his voting rights in the committee on budgetary control, nils lundgren of the junist party has ensured that we have a parliamentary situation today where the european parliament is likely to defend the most shameful agricultural subsidies. i appreciate that lundgren yesterday publicly acknowledged that he was wrong, the problem is that the damage has already been done. the fact remains that, although junilista promised before the european elections to work for the abolition of tobacco subsidies, the most lasting result to date of junilista's election to parliament is that it has helped to ensure the survival of tobacco subsidies. i hope and believe that parliament in plenary will do everything in its power to correct the actions of nils lundgren and vote in favour of an opinion stating that parliament wants to see the tobacco subsidies abolished.

in writing. the junilists abstained in the vote on this report. the report is an own-initiative report based mainly on the commission communication on short sea shipping. the european parliament would never have had to produce this document. we understand that the commission may need to evaluate shipping activities in order to examine how the internal market can be better developed. but we do not think it is necessary for the european parliament to write a report on this subject at this stage.
in writing. the eu's definition of common minimum standards for basic common legal safeguards for suspects and accused persons in criminal matters may seem to be commendable. the proposal is, however, part of the establishment of a common criminal justice system. guarantees of these minimum rights are already provided for in the european convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, which has been signed by all the member states. it is doubtful whether the principle of subsidiarity has been respected and whether there is any legal basis in the treaty for the harmonisation of the criminal justice systems of the member states. in addition, legal uncertainty will arise as to whether the european court of justice or the court of justice of the european communities has a primacy of interpretation. the junilista therefore votes against the proposal.

in writing. taxpayers' money must always be treated with the utmost respect. of course, trustees must follow established codes of conduct. it must be the discretion of the elected representative to decide how best to use the appropriations for the performance of the mandate. what literature and newspapers one reads cannot be subject to the control of others, what telephone calls are legitimate can never be decided by other politicians, politicians should not have to disclose who they consult or talk to. they need to be able to protect sources and voter contacts. in this context, we have voted against the requirement of annual audits of the remuneration of members. on one point, we were forced to vote for an annual audit for technical reasons, because we support the demand that the deficit of the european parliament's pension fund should not be covered by public funds. a majority of members of the european parliament must never be allowed to influence the ability of other elected members to represent their party and their country. swedish meps are accountable to swedish voters for their actions and not to political opponents in other countries. we have therefore voted against the possibility for parliament to suspend members from office.

common for members to engage in debate and decision-making which, under the principle of subsidiarity, has no place in this house, but in this case the matter is clear. the eu has a common trade policy. parliament therefore has strong grounds for monitoring compliance with the rules of global free trade. such a system is the most important way to increase prosperity in both poor and rich countries, but it requires that individual countries and trade blocs do not pursue protectionism, often in the form of tariffs and other trade barriers. it may also take the form of subsidies or special rules for certain production to attract or retain activities which would otherwise not be able to compete internationally. such matters are regulated by the wto. i join the other speakers in this house who have called for the commission to take swift action against countries and states in unions that are in breach of wto rules. it is important, however, to distinguish in principle between prohibited and permitted measures, between pro-production policies and dumping. it is not unacceptable to choose a generally low level of taxation to promote growth. what is not allowed is to favour selected companies or sectors with tax advantages. it's called tax evasion. it is also permissible to have relatively low ambitions in a country's environmental policy during a phase when the country is poor and must prioritise growth. that's what rich countries do today when they were poor. what is unacceptable is to grant special exemptions from existing environmental requirements in order to favour individual companies or sectors. it's called environmental dumping. i note that the commission, in its analysis and its actions, clearly distinguishes between legitimate rules to promote growth and prosperity and illegitimate dumping practices.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as we have heard today, there are several examples of how it is still easier for a hammer to cross the baltic sea than for a carpenter to go along and hammer a nail. i therefore welcome the proposal for a services directive which will soon allow us to speak of four freedoms - the free movement of goods, services, people and capital - and not just three as is the case today. however, in the present treaty, several of the things that are happening in my own country, sweden, for example, with state-sanctioned and explicit discrimination against people from the new member states are totally unacceptable. let me give a very brief but very frightening and unfortunately far from unique example of how trade unions and public authorities together deny the new members of the eu access to the internal market. the swedish case started with a municipality building a school, and as a consequence of complying with european public procurement rules, a latvian construction company (lp-bygg) was hired. soon the construction union was there, blocking the work site, stopping work, carrying signs and chanting "go home, go home". it was argued that the reason was that the latvian company was obliged to sign a swedish collective agreement and that the latvian one, although it paid better than the swedish one, was not valid. the decision was made: the latvians would go out. the company appealed to the authorities and the labour court, in which the union sits, naturally ruled in favour of the trade union movement. our minister of labour, who has recently been the head of the trade union movement, also took a stand for the trade union movement. it's times like this that i'm ashamed to be swedish. exactly one week ago, the latvian company was forced into bankruptcy. the result is that we have schoolchildren without school, taxpayers with extra charges and unemployed people. all this in order to ensure that the privileged society on the swedish labour market continues to function. buoyed by its success, the union is now running a campaign around the country with the support of the government to demand that people who do not "look really swedish should wear clear id badges". this is unacceptable and i wonder what the commission intends to do to put an end to this racism and protectionism which is raging in europe. (applause) i would like to thank you.
in writing. despite good practice, the current fisheries policy still means that the eu is buying the right to exploit the natural resources of the poor. it's a modern form of colonialism. we need a real reform of fisheries policy and a complete change of approach to our relations with the poor countries. we have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. the kuhne report deals with the european security strategy and the brok report deals with the eu's common defence and security policy. we are voting against both resolutions as a whole because they aim to further militarize eu cooperation and make the union a great power that speaks with a voice in international contexts. this development is leading to a large amount of resources going to the eu that could be better used in other organisations. we believe that the osce and the council of europe should be given priority in conflict prevention work at european level. at the global level, the united nations - with its reach, experience and legitimacy - is the most appropriate. the un should also have the primary responsibility for global disarmament and for preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons and weapons of mass destruction. the un should also be provided with resources for military crisis management. there is no reason to lift the arms embargo against china, because human rights demands are not being met. strict control of swedish exports of war material should be encouraged. we oppose brok's call for the proposals in the eu constitution to strengthen the eu's defence and security policy to be implemented already - even though the constitution has not been approved in all countries and thus has not formally entered into force. it shows a startling arrogance towards the voters of europe.
the commission proposal aims to tackle the excessive fish mortality and the problems of adequate reproduction in these areas. the international council for the exploration of the sea, for example, reported that the mortality of fisheries has led to such a low level of juvenile cod and sea trout in the cantabrian sea and the iberian peninsula that the stocks are no longer replenished by reproduction and are now threatened with collapse. there is no reason to vote for a watered-down version of the commission's proposal, which is what the committee on fisheries voted for, once the commission takes steps to deal with the problems. we need a real reform of fisheries policy and a complete change of approach to our relationship with poor countries and to the sustainable use of our natural resources. i have therefore voted against the two reports.
in writing. we support the accession of croatia, serbia and montenegro, bosnia and herzegovina, macedonia and albania to the eu. when countries fully meet the copenhagen criteria, they should become members. it is a natural development of the enlargement of the eu which began with the 10 new member states in may 2004. we are, however, opposed to the strengthening of the eu's foreign policy, which is in many respects the aim of the resolution.
in writing. in general, i support the development of the common foreign and security policy. in some emergencies, it is natural to use both military and civilian resources. at the same time, it is important that humanitarian principles are not watered down or that humanitarian operations are not used for military purposes (as has unfortunately happened in iraq and afghanistan). it is also important to ensure that resources earmarked for long-term development cooperation are not used for other purposes. for these reasons, i have chosen to abstain because the wording of both paragraphs 45 and 46 may allow for arbitrariness in these aspects.
in writing. we consider the fight against doping in sport to be a very important issue. it is so important that it must be dealt with by the national parliament in each of the member states. we do not consider sport and sports to be eu affairs. we demand, however, that each member state have effective control of the eu's external borders and combat the trade in illegal drugs. the draft resolution, in recital e, refers to the fact that the treaty establishing a constitution for europe provides an appropriate legal basis for the development and implementation of community action in the field of sport. one of the reasons for opposing the draft eu constitution is precisely that it unreasonably regulates matters which are for the member states to decide. we therefore vote against this resolution.
in writing. we see this as a matter between the member states and unesco, even though the council of ministers has authorized the commission to negotiate on behalf of the eu those parts of the unesco draft text which fall within the eu's competence. we believe that these issues should not be on the eu's table. it is absurd that the eu should speak with one voice for 25 member states on issues concerning the diversity of cultural content and artistic expression. we have therefore voted against the final vote on this resolution.

mr president, commissioner špidla, citizens on the stand and colleagues! like the others, i welcome the debate on roma in the eu. i also welcome all roma as members of the european union. despite the prohibition of discrimination, the first article of the un, 12-15 million roma live in a vulnerable situation. the 7-9 million roma in the eu, as many as the population of sweden, are present in most eu member states but no country gives the group proper recognition as its largest ethnic minority. discrimination is also increasing. since 2000, when sweden ratified the council of europe's framework convention for the protection of national minorities and the european charter for regional or minority languages, the romani language has been recognised as a minority language in sweden, but this is not enough to break the exclusion. the whole of the eu must protect the position of the roma and in particular the vulnerable position of women and children. we must work with roma women to make them part of our gender equality policy, particularly in the areas of protection of reproductive and sexual health and promotion of family planning, education for children and increased participation in political decision-making. i listened to commissioner špidla's report, thank you for it, and i would like to ask commissioner špidla, who is responsible for gender equality in the commission, if there is anything more we can do specifically for women's and children's rights and against exclusion, because the roma cause is the responsibility of the whole of the eu. i call for a vote on the resolution.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for her excellent work. in 2000, when the report on the modernisation of social security systems was discussed, i was the rapporteur. when we put forward the strategy, we discussed for the first time the open method of coordination and health care together with pensions and other issues. in health care, organisation and financing are national issues which in some countries are dealt with at regional level, and should be allowed to do so in the future. the question is whether there is a european added value to cooperation, and the answer is yes, because the challenges we face are common. you have pointed out some of them, and i want to mention two. the technological advances that enable us to do more things are, of course, positive advances that enable more cures. but it also calls for greater priorities in the future. we also live longer, which is fortunate, but which also puts a greater strain on health care systems. here we can have an exchange of experience of best practice, best practices. it is important, as many have pointed out, to focus on preventive care. in this context i myself raised the question of the need for a developed occupational health system, because many illnesses and other problems arise in working life. knowing the environment and being able to intervene early is important for health care. i do not see the person who receives health care services primarily as a customer but as a patient. it has to do with the view that health care is not a commercial service but a service driven by needs which i do not think should be in the services directive, a view which has also been expressed by some others here. the services directive is primarily concerned with commercial services, whereas this is a public service and should be treated in that context.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to make a few comments on the resolution before us. there has been a very high level of legislative activity in recent years, almost too high in my view. i would say that the costs to the financial sector of implementing all these new laws are quite high and growing. just to bring in the whole basel ii directive is costing the banks a lot of money. i therefore want to limit the legislative ambitions in the future and slow down the process somewhat. i welcome, for example, the fact that we did not come forward with legislative proposals in the clearing and settlement area. let competition work, there is no risk of monopoly. technological progress is so rapid that competition from new players is breaking down old monopolies. one area where i do not want to see new legislation is the area of corporate governance. it's a field that deals with corporate governance. let the market regulate itself here. if germany wants its corporate governance rules, they can have them. if england wants its rules, they can have them. in the long run, the financial market will determine which system is most trusted. it will attract the interest of investors. i believe in institutional competition. do not create a common code regulated by any directorate of the european commission. too much regulation will lead to systemic congestion. once a law is made, it's almost impossible to change. the risk is that we will have a financial market filled with legal disputes, litigation, court rulings and even more legislative measures to close the loopholes in the legislation. the words 'harmonisation' and 'level playing field' have become the gateway to a stifling legalist system, but open up the possibility of mutual acceptance of each other's systems. the responsibility for financial market supervision should not be concentrated in the eu, but rather there is a need for better cooperation between local national supervisory bodies. i do not want to see a new super-financial inspectorate in the eu.
in writing. we consider that the legal basis given for the regulation in question is incorrect. the extension of the schengen convention to allow the bodies responsible for issuing vehicle registration certificates in the member states to access data in the schengen information system cannot be based on the common transport legislation. this is a third pillar issue, which concerns judicial and police cooperation. it is for the council, not the parliament, to decide on these matters. the junilista has therefore voted against the amendments.
in writing. pollution from households, agriculture and industry is increasing. it's deteriorating the quality of the groundwater. when groundwater is polluted, the consequences are more long-lasting than in the case of surface water pollution. groundwater is an important resource for drinking water, industrial and agricultural processes. therefore, it should be protected for current and future use. we social democrats therefore welcome the commission's initiative for a new framework for groundwater. however, groundwater is different in the eu. therefore, it is difficult to find a common standard that is suitable for all groundwater resources in the eu and that at the same time provides the most positive result for the environment. we believe that an approach which took greater account of the wide diversity of groundwater resources in different parts of the eu would have been more beneficial for the environment.
in writing. the junilists are elected with the mandate that only cross-border environmental issues are decided at eu level. the council shall play a central role in this decision-making process. groundwater is partly a transboundary issue. we regret that the groundwater directive does not refer to the principle of subsidiarity more. the directive on the protection of groundwater against pollution should therefore be proposed for a period of three years, to be replaced by a directive which takes account of the principle of subsidiarity.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted in favour of the report on the proposal for a council regulation amending the regulation establishing a european fund for the improvement of living and working conditions. we believe that it is important to keep the composition of the fund's board of directors and its working methods under review in order to make the european fund for the improvement of living and working conditions more effective. however, it is problematic if this review is to be carried out by the whole of the european parliament, since few people know how the fund works. we also believe that recruitment should be based on the merits of the applicant and should be gender neutral. it is in the interest of the organisation to have a board of directors with different skills and experience, but there should be no political control over recruitment.
