mr president, i would like to thank you. in the committee we often get half-baked proposals from the council. unfortunately, i sometimes get the feeling that the environment committee then sees it as its task to make half-baked proposals bad. now we have another example of this. the bathing water directive. the name of this directive says it all. the eu must therefore regulate the quality of the water on our beaches. i have to say, i think it's stunning. the question is whether there is any issue which is so small that it should be left to the member states to decide. but in the council's defence, this is not a new stupidity, because the current directive is far worse than the compromise reached by the council. under the current directive, a small country like sweden would have to regularly measure the quality of water in up to 5 000 bathing sites. the fact that sweden does not measure the water quality of more than 800 bathing sites is not a sign that we are some kind of european wild west. it is an example of how common sense, fortunately, has been given priority over unreasonable legislation. and in the council's defence, i will say that at least they have now tried to get rid of some of the worst of the craziness. but as i said, the environment committee's proposal which we are going to vote on is unfortunately an example of the committee's wish to make half of the legislation bad. one example in abundance is that the committee now demands that information on the quality of bathing water, for example in härjarö outside enköping, where i spent many sunny summer days, must now also be disseminated in french. unfortunately, in many places there is now a desire to add to the burden. for example, some people want recreational water to be included in the water quality report and regularly monitored. this includes water quality wherever people windsurf, paddle, kayak or the like, and - the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. president. - the debate is closed. in orwell's animal farm, there's a scene that repeats itself, namely that the sheep as soon as you're about to have a debate start shouting: "four legs good, two legs bad". that is what we are now dealing with - whenever anyone objects to our transferring power from the member states to the eu institutions, we are told that this is tantamount to representing a repetition of the second world war, the holocaust and all that. we cannot even discuss the validity or otherwise of the working time directive without receiving accusations of this kind. the principle of subsidiarity is fundamental, it is celebrated in every conceivable ceremonial context. when we get into the details, it is the opposite, nothing can really be left in the member states. the working time directive is itself a breach of the principle of subsidiarity. the countries are different, they have different economic structures, some live on industry, others have process industries, we have different arrangements in the public sector and rules for this. there is no reason for this, and it is extremely inappropriate to try to regulate working hours for the whole of the eu in this way. those who now advocate this say that we will otherwise be socially dumped. this is a very serious accusation against the countries we have accepted as members of the european union, all of which meet the copenhagen criteria, all of which are states of law with the right to free association. the fact that we could be subjected to any form of slave labor there is a grotesque accusation that must be categorically rejected. here parliament has now tried to make the existing working time directive even worse. we shall say no to that, it is an attempt to further advance the positions of the eu institutions. the proposal should therefore be categorically rejected on the basis of the principle of subsidiarity.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for a very good job. i shall dwell on one thing in this short time, namely, the exception. it has been said by mr de wit that this is contrary to the principle of subsidiarity, but that is not the case. denmark, sweden and france, for example, are free to have higher standards than this minimum directive. this is a minimum directive. so, what, we can do without the minimum directive? no, we can't. we have to. because it also indirectly affects standards in those countries which have much, much higher standards. if we have a situation where countries start competing with each other for long working hours, then that will affect the situation in denmark, sweden and france. it is therefore linked to the principle of subsidiarity, since we have free movement of capital. that's one thing. the second concerns our responsibility as legislators. does that border go along the national border? should we only think about the free movement of companies or should we also take responsibility for the health of employees? i believe that we must also take responsibility for the health of our employees and that is why we need a minimum directive. thirdly, we often talk about reconciling family and work life, but what if we allow long working hours? it means that men will work and not take responsibility for the family and then we will not have an equal society. that is why we need this proposal from the rapporteur.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is good that we now have the opportunity to revise the working time directive. my main focus in this work is to make it easier for member states to create new jobs. it is therefore important to provide flexible solutions and to reduce the level of eu-level regulation and centralisation. working time issues should be primarily the responsibility of the member states and therefore there should be a wide margin for divergent solutions between them. the industry also operates differently and, as individuals, we have different needs and desires for our working hours. working time is an area for national practice and not for eu decision. unfortunately, there are strong forces in this parliament which want to go in the opposite direction. they want to limit the ability of the member states to adapt to their own traditions and needs. only one model will apply to the whole of europe, which means that they want to introduce supranationality in matters of working time. they do not want to allow member states to differ and that all europeans do not have identical preferences for the way in which their working time should be allocated. unlike those people, i believe that the most important thing in europe today is to create good conditions for the creation of new jobs. unlike them, i believe that people themselves, rather than politicians, know better what is best for them. as with so many other issues, it is not true that one solution is best for all. we are different and that is precisely the charm and strength of the eu. we are made up of different states with different cultures and traditions. i want to give europe the opportunity to preserve its own particularities and to give the member states the power to decide on working time. it is not a matter for detailed regulation at eu level.
in writing. we support in principle the accession of croatia, serbia and montenegro, bosnia and herzegovina, macedonia and albania to the eu. when countries fully meet the copenhagen criteria, they should become members. this is a natural consequence of the enlargement process which started in may 2004. the eu's framework agreements, which aim to prepare countries for membership, must focus on improving public administration, strengthening the judiciary and fighting corruption. the countries must not become accustomed to the costly and harmful agricultural policies of the eu. the enlargement of the eu must not be used as an excuse for not reforming and eventually abolishing the common agricultural policy. an enlarged eu of up to 35 countries requires a major reform of agricultural policy.
in writing. if croatia, serbia and montenegro, bosnia and herzegovina, macedonia and albania meet all the criteria for eu membership at some point in the future, they should be welcomed as members. however, the european parliament should not take a position on a framework agreement until there is a clear basis for demonstrating the financial commitments that a framework agreement provides for the other member states. a document should also show the types of support and rights that a framework agreement will entail. experience from previous framework agreements shows that a 'yes' vote creates political pressure to support all possible forms of aid and subsidies in the future. the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to apply the principle of subsidiarity to all aid schemes, including those for the production of agricultural products. the eu and potential member states alike benefit in the long term from adapting to a system that creates dependency on subsidies. i have therefore voted against the report and hope that the commission will come back with an economic calculation and a document clearly defining the aid that will be made available when a framework agreement is signed.
in writing. we have voted in favour of this report's proposal to accede to this international convention. in general, we are sceptical about the eu as such joining international conventions, but since intergovernmental organisations can join this convention and we also believe that this improves the internal market in this area, we are voting in favour of the report.
in writing. the eu's task is to solve crossborder problems. one of these is the spread of infectious animal diseases. that is why we are voting in favour of the report. however, the fight against the spread of infectious animal diseases within the eu framework is no excuse for continuing to pursue a costly and harmful common agricultural policy (cap). we believe that the financial responsibility for the gjp should be nationalized.
in writing. provisions on the rules to be applied to bathing water quality at bathing sites in europe are not a matter for the eu to deal with. this issue is to be dealt with by the member states. the parliament's proposal does not take account of the principle of subsidiarity or the principle of proportionality. in addition to our principled position that bathing water quality management is not a matter for the eu, we consider that the parliament's proposal is more bureaucratic and cumbersome than the council's. we have therefore voted against this recommendation. the situation regarding bathing water varies greatly within the eu and it is therefore not desirable to adopt a directive which would apply to the mediterranean countries as well as to the northern countries of the union, such as sweden. we therefore oppose several formulations, including the establishment of contingency plans for events that may affect the quality of bathing water, such as floods, accidents or infrastructure problems. we are opposed to the detailed regulations which parliament wants to introduce, such as extending the scope of the directive to include additional leisure activities.
in writing. preserving cultural heritage is of course very important. but we believe that this is not a european issue, that it should be dealt with by the member states and that we can have a good deal of confidence in the way they deal with this issue. we have therefore voted against this report, as we believe that this recommendation should not have been dealt with in the european parliament at all.
in writing. i have voted against this report because i believe that the eu should not regulate these cultural issues. the report is way too claw-fingered. it's up to each country to handle how films are regulated.
in writing. we note that it is obvious that there must be high security requirements for europe's ports. but we wonder whether there is a risk that a member state such as the netherlands would really fail to take care of the security of its port of rotterdam, which is so crucial to its business activities? the way this directive has been drafted, there is a risk that it could be a severe blow to the activities of smaller ports, as they have to implement a disproportionate number of costly measures to continue their activities. as such ports are often located in economically weaker regions, this has unfortunate regional consequences. we want to see a new review and reworking of the directive from the perspective of the smaller ports.
in writing. the resolution on the murder of robert mccartney points to the ira as responsible for the murder, even though the issue has not yet been settled in court. it is unreasonable for the european parliament to judge a sensitive issue which could affect the peace process in northern ireland. that is why we choose to abstain on the resolution.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i am grateful for the contributions, especially those of mr schmit and mr dimas. it is very positive in itself that you are exuding a certain optimism on this difficult issue, both on the problem in general and specifically on how you read bush, i wish i could be as optimistic. george w. bush gave an energy and climate speech just this day and unfortunately that speech didn't show any real movement in the right direction. but i agree with you that dialogue with the united states is extremely important, because without their active participation, climate action can never be truly effective. of course, just as important is a dialogue, and a strategic one, with large developing countries such as china and india. i saw the other day that china is planning to build 800 large coal plants in the next 10 years. the emissions from these will completely overshadow what we do in europe. the conclusion, of course, is that the developing countries do not have to go down exactly the same path in energy development as we have. they do not have to make the same mistakes as us and it is in everyone's interest to offer them favourable terms so that they can invest in the best possible technology. i am sorry to say that the clean development mechanism is not enough, but we must come up with something much more radical and ambitious. the situation is serious, as recent research reports indicate that the climate system may be far more fragile than we previously thought. it will be much more difficult to stay within the two degrees of temperature rise set by the eu and to avoid dangerous changes in the climate, what the british call dangerous climate change. that is why the bonn meeting is so important, because you can lay the foundations for the period after 2012. we point out in our resolution the need for short-term and long-term emission targets. we also believe that such objectives must be followed by much more ambitious programmes in terms of both the development of new sources of energy and increased investment in energy efficiency. there are a number of issues to which we must return and work together to develop concrete objectives. i wish the commission and the council well in bonn and hope that you will come back with positive reports so that we can do this work in a constructive way for the future and take our responsibility, as florenz pointed out, for future generations.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the last hundred years, the world has experienced a temperature increase of about 0.6 degrees. the un's intergovernmental panel on climate change (ipcc) has found that these global climate changes are having the greatest impact on the world's poor. i would like to stress this because in this house we too often see global problems from a one-sided european perspective. the reasons why poor people are the main victims of global climate change are many. this is partly because these countries' economies are often dominated by climate-sensitive sectors, especially agriculture. in addition, they have little resources to adapt and protect themselves against various changes, such as rising sea levels or reduced rainfall. resources are also too scarce for preventive healthcare, making it difficult to stop the risk of outbreaks of climate-related diseases, such as malaria. the rich countries of the world need to coordinate their joint efforts on climate change and work on preventive measures. my party believes that the eu should work on cross-border environmental issues. climate change is one of them, and i therefore support constructive and well-thought-out proposals from the union in this area, and in doing so i would point out that the un convention on climate change has been ratified by 189 parties. we cannot therefore work unilaterally through european institutions, but must also coordinate our actions within the framework of other international organisations and be responsive to the competent bodies of the un and the world energy council (wec).
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you to mr schmit and mr dimas for their very encouraging comments. i really hope that your analysis is correct, that it is going in the right direction. i would also like to thank mr anders wijkman for his work on the resolution which we are going to vote on later. i share the view of many others in this chamber that the conference of government experts is very welcome, although i would also like to stress that it cannot replace meetings at a higher political level. sometimes it seems that we have a lot of knowledge but we lack the political will to do more to tackle climate change. i hope that this meeting can help to draw attention to positive examples of how to reduce the impact of climate change, not least in terms of community planning, because sometimes it seems that climate issues are only considered as an environmental issue on the side, for those who are most interested. but it's very important that we integrate this approach into all of our social construction, into all of our social planning, because that's the only way we can achieve results. i also hope that the meeting will discuss how to integrate measures aimed at reducing climate impact and achieving sustainable development into the wto trading system as well as into other international agreements. this is what is needed to get more countries to take the necessary action, but this type of regulation could also help to reduce the transport that is so much of a climate threat today. for example, fish is now being taken up in the baltic and the north sea, transported by air to asia, repackaged into ready-made food which is then transported back and sold in the same place where the fish was taken up. finally, good luck at the meeting and do your best to communicate that the eu is actually doing something in this area. we need to, we need to show our voters and our citizens that we are actually doing something about the biggest challenge we face, which is climate change.
mr president, i would like to thank you. several recent scientific studies show that climate change is likely to be even faster than we thought and that greater reductions in the amount of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere are also needed to prevent these changes. this means that the demands on us are increasing and that the measures we face to reduce emissions by 60-80 per cent by 2050, which is the realistic thing, will require a completely different energy and transport policy than we have today. so we're really just getting started. at the same time, we often say here that we are better than the united states, and we are, but it's not particularly difficult in the current situation. we saw in buenos aires that the united states today is not only refusing to take responsibility for itself but is also actively trying to prevent global cooperation on the world's climate. it is therefore all the more important that the eu has the courage to pursue a consistent policy with far-reaching objectives. i also believe that the ultimate loser in this is the united states itself, both ecologically and economically, because with the kind of 'dinosaur system' we have for energy and transport, we will be forced to make even faster and more comprehensive transitions in the future.
vice-president of the commission. madam president, i would like to thank you. the commission's main communication challenge is to reach 450 million eu citizens with limited resources. according to the latest eurobarometer full report, tv is by far the most popular source of information for citizens in all 25 member states on eu issues. radio is closely followed in third place with a higher percentage in the new member states, and audiovisual media are the most effective channels for disseminating information about the european union. as part of its objective of informing citizens about the eu, its realities and policies, its decisions and actions, the commission has published every year since 2003 a call for proposals for the co-financing of high-quality and accessible audiovisual projects in an appealing popular form, which at the same time aim to promote the ideas and values of the eu. the total budget for 2004 was eur 6.7 million. of the project proposals submitted by broadcasters or broadcasting companies, 79 projects were selected. for 2005, the call for proposals is limited to radio and the budget for that activity is eur 3,9 million. the commission then draws up agreements regulating the forms of this cooperation between broadcasters or producers and the commission. in designing their projects, applicants are free to choose the type of programmes they prefer to produce and broadcast. the format can be drama series, competitions, films, news reports but also debate programmes and so on. so far, no candidates have chosen to develop projects for dramas, competitions or films. this finding is consistent with the results of the commission's latest quality studies and opinion polls carried out in the eu's 25 member states. the aim was to identify the wishes and expectations of europeans in terms of both form and content. in fact, television viewers do not consider films, drama series or competitions to be the most appropriate form of communication of information about the eu, but prefer topical newsreels, reportage, documentaries and, above all, factual and educational news elements in more general news programmes. they may also be interested in historical documentaries, cultural programmes and films, but to a much lesser extent. the applicants are always free to choose the type of programme they wish to produce, provided that it contributes to conveying the european message to the citizens.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i want to thank you for this very informative answer. i have just one more small question, and that is whether the commission can guarantee that the information is neutral. you say it's up to the people who make the programs to decide what's in it. is there no requirement for neutrality and objectivity?
vice-president of the commission. madam president, i would like to thank you. we assume that the partners we work with follow the laws of their respective member countries on neutrality, or rather objectivity. we cannot impose any additional requirements, but we will make an offer and invite tenders to produce programmes, but they must follow the national laws relating to editorial freedom and, of course, objectivity.
vice-president of the commission. it is very difficult to comment on a specific case when i do not know the detailed circumstances, but i do not think we should be afraid of an open debate with those who disagree with us and we should welcome a lively debate on different issues. if we work with civil society, with ngos, we will always also have them criticising us, but that is part of the democratic procedure. i am used to working, for example, with environmental ngos and we give a lot of support to ngos. i was constantly criticised by them, but this is actually their role. they should also keep up the pressure on us and they should keep an eye on what we are doing, and we have to accept that there is a debate. i do not know your specific case and why this is a problem at all. but if we give money or ask for calls for proposals, then we have to be very specific about what it is that we expect in return; and probably in this case there was something wrong in that respect.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the un is the international organisation best placed to bring about a lasting solution to the conflict in darfur. it is important that eu action is carried out within the framework of un cooperation and that eu actions are carried out in cooperation with institutions with a long experience and legitimacy, such as the who or the un world food programme (wfp). i welcome the un's efforts in the sudan conflict, such as the recent creation of the 10,000 strong un mission umis. i also welcome the proposal to increase the presence of the african union in darfur. it has always been important to my party that the un be the first to act in this conflict. the un should act in cooperation with the african union, and any eu action should be focused on humanitarian aid after consultation with the un. the un plans to contribute a total of us$1.5 billion in aid during 2005. this is the single largest un appeal this year, it is important that eu assistance efforts are coordinated and conducted in consultation with the un so that the overall global response in sudan achieves maximum effectiveness.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteurs schmit and almunia for their contributions. there is much to be said about the world bank, its governance and, not least, the way its leadership is appointed. the recent selection of wolfowitz shows that competence for the specific task played less of a role than political merit. it shows that the appearance of high-level executives in the international system still leaves much to be desired. but i have a short time and i shall focus specifically on the relations between the eu and the bank. both schmit and almunia point to the weakness today, namely that we are not acting unitedly and with one voice. on the part of parliament, we recently had a meeting with the european directors of the world bank and they were completely unanimous in demanding and calling for a more united approach on the part of the union on development issues, both in general and specifically as regards the work of the bank. we also had a meeting with jeffrey sachs a few months ago in the committee on development and he asked why - given the enormous flow of aid money compared to the rest of the world - 'why does the eu not act more unitedly, you would get more out of your work, better results and you would also have more influence in all this important context'. both schmit and almunia have stressed in their speeches that we are short of funds today. how can we achieve a more united and unified foreign policy if we continue to behave so fragmentedly in these international institutions? in writing. we oppose the present common agricultural policy and we oppose changes of little consequence in principle to the existing system. we are calling for a comprehensive review and re-examination of the whole of the cap, and it is difficult to single out individual areas such as the matter now before the vote. we believe that the quota system for potato starch, which is now up for vote, should not be extended at all, but that the eu must abolish all types of quota systems in agriculture.
in writing. we support structures and rules that prevent discrimination in the labour market for citizens of other countries. we are strong supporters of a dynamic and open internal market in the eu. we believe, however, that it is for each member state to assess which professional qualifications are to be mutually recognised. unfortunately, the directive does not take sufficient account of national conditions and needs. the training systems vary between member states, making it difficult to mutually recognise virtually all professional qualifications. we support the amendment which proposes that the directive should not apply to notaries.
in writing. the report aims to simplify the eu's organisation of the fruit and vegetable market. but it preserves the existing structures of the cap. we cannot therefore support the report.
in writing. we believe that the eu should not decide on the working hours of the member states. this is a matter for the parliaments and the social partners of each member state to decide. we have chosen to support the amendments which allow for greater national self-determination, but we are voting against the resolution as a whole.
in writing. working time is something that is appropriate for national legislation and should not be regulated by the eu. however, there is already a directive at eu level regulating working time and this directive aims to update the existing directive. the european parliament has therefore voted to make the individual rules in the working time directive as good as possible. we have safeguarded the principle of subsidiarity and a flexible labour market while protecting the health of workers. unfortunately, a lot of fine-tuning was voted through which we think could hurt small businesses in europe. we therefore voted against the whole proposal.
in writing. the question of the regulation of working time is a complex one. my main point in relation to this directive is that this type of issue belongs primarily at the level of the member states. therefore, when the opt-out proposal was voted down - a rule which is not applied in sweden - and the proposal to calculate average working time over a period of more than four months was rejected, i thought it appropriate to vote against the whole proposal.
the replacement of the questioner. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the council for its reply to mr seppänen's question. i have two follow-up questions. the first concerns whether the council considers that the final disposal of radioactive waste is a matter of common interest in that the eu should seek common solutions for different member states and how and where to dispose of its waste. my second question concerns the euratom treaty. one of the points of discussion in the negotiations on the new constitution was whether the euratom treaty should be allowed to survive, as it does in the constitution. the council considers that it would have been more difficult to pursue a supranational policy in this area if the euratom treaty had not been the legal basis.

mr president, i would like to thank you. now the logic of global free trade has hit us too. it is hypocritical to demand protection against textile imports while ignoring the conditions of the oil and precious metals industries, where we do not have the same threat to european companies. what we should do is introduce a general fair tariff, where producers who do not comply with ilo and environmental requirements are made to pay. these tariffs should then be returned to the developing world to improve their environmental and social situation. i think it is strange that we should be investing in protecting the intellectual property rights of big companies in china, when it is mainly human rights that need protection in china and that is where we should be investing our efforts. but the transition from free to fair trade now requires a reform of the whole economic policy, so that trade becomes a tool for man and not the other way around. the un environment programme (unepo) and the un development programme (undp) should have power over the wto and not the other way around.
mr president, i would like to thank you. information is an objective way of communicating what is happening, and it is good for democracy. pr and propaganda, that's when you want to sell something that's not in demand. the commission has managed to maintain a fairly good balance between these, but unfortunately the report is moving in the direction of more propaganda. of course, we always want to be seen in a positive light, but the eu institutions must be equated with public authorities, who must describe rules, laws and their work objectively and accurately. that is why i am against paragraph 5, which says that universities should spread the values of the eu instead of critical thinking, analysis and knowledge. i am opposed to paragraph 9 which deals with pure brand placement, something we normally try to limit. the demand for a television channel of its own is not realistic - it is better to have reasonable conditions for public service television and tougher rules against media concentration.
in writing. we believe that the national parliaments should decide on their own position at the almaty conference on the aarhus convention. member states are free to decide on the attitude to participation in the decision-making process on gmos and the establishment of a financing system to ensure predictable financing (paragraph 4). the countries which have not ratified the aarhus convention will of course have to decide independently whether they wish to do so or not. in our view, the european parliament should not be making any gestures in this respect.
in writing. global climate change is a problem which we believe there are good reasons to address at international level. the eu is one of several international organisations with an important role to play in this context. we question, however, why the european parliament should call on the member states and the incoming presidencies to act in a certain way on climate issues. the resolution contains political views on energy issues, the kyoto protocol and guidelines on how third countries such as china, india and the united states should act. we shall vote against this resolution. the reason for this position is that we believe that national parliaments should determine their respective commitments and positions in international organisations and in political matters on their own. the report contains calls from the european parliament to member states and third countries on how to act on the urgent climate issue. we oppose this, despite the many good intentions in the proposed resolution.
in writing. we have chosen to abstain on this matter. many of the phrases are highly commendable. we are, however, opposed to the eu's desire to play a foreign policy role by making statements about humanitarian disasters in the world. we do not believe that the eu should take a position for its member states in international affairs and we believe that national parliaments should formulate their respective approaches to international affairs. we support the swedish government's position on the sudan question.
in writing. while it is important to improve the situation of democracy and human rights in central asia, we believe that this is not the task of the eu, as this region is not part of the eu's immediate vicinity. these tasks are better handled by a pan-european security organisation such as the organisation for security and cooperation in europe (osce). we welcome the statement's calls for strengthening the role of the osce, but cannot support the statement's basic aim of strengthening the eu's foreign and security policy. that is why we are voting to abstain.
in writing. we are turning to phrases that are tucked into the text to be comments on the way the eu works and the future. there is a belief that the construction of a federal eu will bring peace while reducing the role of nato in europe and the world. there is also much more to add to a resolution dealing with the end of the second world war. canada's important role in the final victory must not be forgotten. the american presence in europe in the post-war period must not be forgotten when it comes to preserving peace. we particularly object to the wording of paragraphs 1, 7 and 8 of the motion for a resolution. however, in order to honour the victims of the war, we have voted in favour of the resolution as a whole.
in writing. the rapporteur speaks in glowing terms about upgrading existing services, creating new services, technical adjustments, expectations of 'fine-tuning' and additional resources for information offices, mainly in romania and bulgaria. the text put to the vote is unclear but can only be interpreted as a request for additional appropriations. we are opposed to increased spending, particularly in the case of proposals which involve increased administrative costs and additional money for a budget line which is often used for pure propaganda. we have therefore voted against the resolution as a whole.
in writing. we support the general objective of the report, which is to liberalize international trade without compromising basic social and environmental requirements. we also support the demands to end export subsidies under the eu's common agricultural policy (cap) and to give less developed countries quota- and duty-free access to the eu market. these requirements are not consistent with the report's statement that the 'european agricultural model' should not be called into question (paragraph 16). the liberalisation of trade policy must go hand in hand with a fundamental reform of the cap.
in writing. we swedish social democrats have voted in favour of the report on the evaluation of the doha round following the wto agreement. the general approach of this report is good, as parliament supports free and fair multilateral trading systems in the context of sustainable development. we cannot, however, support paragraphs 16 and 17. the planned reform of the agricultural policy is absolutely necessary, as it is a first step towards a fair world trade. we oppose any attempt to make it more difficult for third world countries to sell their products within the eu. the accession of developing countries to the eu market must be given priority over the maintenance of agricultural subsidies.
in writing. what is 'true' and what is 'false' information about the eu? what is the information on the 'european tank'? of course, information about political institutions is shaped by political values. facts can be distorted and only a selection of facts can be presented to influence citizens. by an information strategy we mean that the political debate should allow room for all opinions, from those who advocate increased supranational governance in the eu to those who advocate strict intergovernmental cooperation. an institution like the commission should not argue for the draft constitutional treaty or for the development of greater supranationality in the eu. as regards, for example, paragraph 9 of the report, it is absolutely ridiculous when it proposes that the eu institutions should enter into agreements with television companies that can produce drama series, competitions, films and more to promote the ideas and values of the eu. some of the information material produced by the eu institutions crosses the line of what can be considered factual information and some of the eu information that is aimed at children and young people should never have been published. in a democracy, the various actors must act in a free and open debate. there must be no actor who is pushing a political line from above with the help of taxpayers' money.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted today against the report on the european union's information and communication strategy. we believe that a political institution such as the european parliament should not be used to shape the opinion of certain ideas or to influence the media to act in a certain way in relation to the political debate taking place in the member states.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this resolution opens with a long list of the problems facing spain. unfortunately, this is not an isolated phenomenon, as we remember from the heat wave in france last year. in fact, nine of the ten warmest years in the last 500 years have occurred in the last ten years. and it is not enough to change agricultural policy to compensate farmers who are affected. we're going to have plans for how we deal with disasters, but the big plan that future generations are expecting us to have here today is to fight climate change, or else this will just be the first example of a long line of people, animals and nature suffering from climate change. climate research clearly shows that even the ambitious targets we have for a maximum temperature increase of two degrees will not be met by the targets we have set for emission reductions, on the contrary, much stronger policies are needed. we should not adjust policy after disasters, but we should adjust policy to prevent the cause of disasters. in this context, criticism must be directed at the former spanish government, which has for a long time opposed eu initiatives to introduce climate taxes and eu initiatives to change the energy system to protect the climate. if this fight against good climate work continues, spain will be hit more times. i therefore hope that the spanish government will now take the lead in combating climate change on our planet for the future of the spanish people and the rest of the world.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, mr president of the council! since the enlargement of the european union a little over a year ago, our powerful neighbour to the east, russia, has become increasingly important to us. it has long been a strategic partner and together we have many challenges and tasks that require cooperation and common solutions: trade, environment, energy, the fight against terrorism and organised crime, to name just a few. we need good relations if we are to achieve stability, security and prosperity across the european continent. however, our partnership has not been without problems, and it is gratifying that at the summit on 10 may we were able to agree on cooperation in the four common spaces. some 400 concrete points of cooperation or particularly concrete ones are not included in this summary but they are far from solved yet. it is important and gratifying that we have come this far, but it also remains to be seen what lies beyond the rhetoric. the eu's foreign policy is based on the principle of the free movement of persons. what this means for future marriage plans remains to be seen. we are interdependent, but relations have been strained and cooperation is hampered by internal developments in russia, where under putin we have seen a decline in human rights and democracy. more and more power has been tied to the kremlin and putin personally. political parties have been marginalized, freedom of expression and the free media have been curtailed. corruption remains widespread and the abuses of the bloody conflict in chechnya seem to be unending. the murder of mr mashadov, the elected president of chechnya, will not facilitate a political solution. putin and the eu also have different views on developments in neighbouring countries. the situation in our common neighborhood has been gratifying in many ways. in ukraine and georgia, people have turned away from authoritarian regimes and have chosen democracy, openness and renewal. we must realize that we have everything to gain from democratic neighbours and russia and putin must accept that these countries do not belong to the russian sphere of interest. the people there want democracy and see the eu, not russia, as the model for the future. however, the problems of cooperation are not only russia's. relations have been complicated by the differing signals being sent by the eu and the individual member states. the common policy places a very strong emphasis on values such as democracy and human rights, but when individual heads of government meet putin, common values tend to fall very far down the agenda. instead, it will focus on, for example, energy. this does not give a good image of the eu and creates confusion on the russian side. the report we are discussing is critical of some aspects of russia's domestic policy development and says that the eu must be clear. the conflict in chechnya can only be resolved politically. the eu must offer itself as a mediator and eventually also participate in the reconstruction process. there are terrible atrocities against civilians in chechnya. it's a matter of concern to all of us. it's not an internal conflict. no one denies the terrible acts committed in russia - i am thinking here of the beslan massacre - but that does not legitimise the abuses in chechnya and the unwillingness to bring the military responsible to justice. russia, by its size and geographical position, and as our neighbour, is a very important partner. there is no alternative to active cooperation but we need to focus on a number of concrete areas of cooperation where the eu and russia have a mutual benefit from results. quality, not quantity, should be the guiding principle in this work and democratic issues must be constantly integrated. the eu must convince russia that economic development and trade go hand in hand with modernisation, a functioning justice system and democratic reforms. we support russia's ambitions to join the wto and the reforms that will entail. we look forward to deepening cooperation in the environmental field, such as maritime and nuclear safety, and to continuing the commitments under the kyoto protocol. we hope that together we will find a solution to the conflict in transnistria, that russian troops will be withdrawn from moldova and georgia. we must, of course, look mainly to the future, but we have a common history, sometimes dramatic and bloody. to move forward, you have to be able to put the past behind you, but that requires a process of reconciliation. the archives of europe and russia from the second world war must be opened and russia must recognize the occupation of the baltic states and the atrocities committed by the soviet union. it is excellent that the border agreement with estonia has been signed, now latvia also remains. our ability to influence developments is limited. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we never back down from the values on which eu cooperation is based and make this clear to our great neighbour. the eu and russia can never achieve a true partnership unless it is based on the defence of democracy. the russian people are not helped by our silence on important matters of principle. a coherent strategy with a common message instead of a one-man show by individual leaders is the only way to conduct a credible policy towards russia. (applause) i would like to thank you.
on behalf of the alde group .– mr president, let me thank mrs gál for a very good report and very good cooperation on all the amendments. i am very pleased we are voting on this tomorrow. our union is entering a new phase. we are in the process of creating a legal obligation for the union to ensure that fundamental rights are actively promoted across all policy areas. the proposed fundamental rights agency would be the practical tool in this work. its main mandate covers what happens inside the union. at the same time we know that just outside the union’s borders there are literally slave markets, where young women and girls are sold to be trafficked into the sex industry in different parts of our union. for instance, it is estimated that every day about 40 girls are sold from these kinds of slave markets in moldova. this is the worst case of human rights violation occurring in our part of the world and the fundamental rights agency should be able to confront it. therefore i hope that this house will confirm the vote of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs to the effect that the agency should also be able to cover third countries when they are involved in human rights issues affecting the union. i also hope that this house will state clearly that the future gender institute at the very least should work closely with and be situated in the same location as the fundamental rights agency. this is rational and cost-effective, but more importantly it should be the obvious line of thinking for anyone who believes that women’s rights are human rights.
mr president, members and commissioner mccreevy, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to thank the rapporteur for the fact that we now have a report and that the european parliament too is taking a comprehensive approach to money laundering and terrorist financing. in the committee on civil and human rights, on which i sit, we have agreed that it is not enough to deal with terrorism as a crime, but we must also look at how terrorism spreads globally and where the money comes from. for me, as a former member of the swedish parliament, it has long been clear that everything from the income from sex slavery to prostitution, drug trafficking, gambling and gambling to the transfer of funds between banks and countries not only finances terrorism but also a lot of criminal activity in our countries. of course, we members of parliament must be subject to the same checks as all other citizens. i hope that the directive will lead to a broad debate on so-called black money in our home countries. money laundering is not only funding terrorism, it is also corrupting our societies and is therefore a threat to democracy. that is why today's debate and directive are so important for the future and for the european parliament and the eu.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur and also thank him for this report. it was a good sign that the committee voted unanimously in favour of the report. the main reason is that the proposal gives us the continuity that is necessary to promote equality. and continuity is necessary if the measures are to have the intended impact and long-term effect. this is of course of great importance, because there is still so much to be done in the field of gender equality. some claim that at the rate at which gender equality is being promoted today, it will be hundreds of years before we can experience living in an equal society. in addition to the continuity, which is also emphasised in the report, the integration of the gender perspective in all policy areas is necessary. i would also like to make a strong appeal to the commission to ensure that the organisations and projects receiving support are carefully evaluated and monitored in order to maximise their impact.
the rapporteur. madam president and commissioner! i would like to thank all my colleagues on these cases and especially the case for which i am responsible, the front-row case. these two reports are important because they are about making the vehicles in europe better. we can really do something here at european level, because vehicles are sold all over europe and not just nationally. that is why we need european action. as for the front arches, which i was responsible for, they are dangerous today, especially in big cities, if you hit a child, a pedestrian, a cyclist. that's why this case has come up. why do we need legislation? there is an agreement between japanese, european and korean car manufacturers not to install hard front bows. but that deal is not enough. firstly, it does not apply to aftermarket front arches, the kind that are added to the car afterwards. nor does it define what hard frontal arches are. that's why we need tests. the directive procedure thus creates legal certainty for both the car trade and the manufacturers of accessories. everyone will know what is involved when we get this directive. why not ban frontal arches altogether? such discussions are held in some countries. they can be good in the wild -- we don't have that much wildland now, but they can be good there, and they come for those conditions, too -- but they're not good in cities. we do not want to ban them altogether, because they can actually make car fronts softer and thus safer. i am also convinced that we would not have the support of the citizens of europe to ban all frontal arcs altogether at european level. trl, the transport research laboratory, in the united kingdom, has published a report which actually shows that there are so-called smart bars, i.e. game traps or front arches that are flexible and also protect pedestrians in collisions with cars equipped with this type of front arches. in committee we have looked at the commission's proposal, of course, and had a committee discussion and eventually an informal trialogue to reach agreement. we have heard the commissioner comment on this and say that he is satisfied with the informal tripartite discussion. we have said something during the debate which has been controversial but which we have eventually agreed and compromised, namely that we think the four tests which the commission proposed to start with were too far-reaching. this is because we know that there is a rapid technological development that allows us to perhaps find new forms of frontal arcs and frontal protection in the future, and new tests. the tests we propose will therefore initially be somewhat more lenient than the commission's original proposal. but here we are in full agreement with the commission now. and then, after a few years, the commission will review the technical regulations and other tests that may be used. the directive applies to future new front-end bows. both the ones on the car and the ones put in afterwards. they shall be approved in a type-approval procedure. in essence, it means that from a certain date only frontal protection systems which meet the new requirements are type-approved and can be placed on the market. front-end bows placed on the market before that date are the responsibility of the member states. it is precisely on this last issue of old front-end arcs on the market that we have jointly proposed a new article in the legislation, which would allow member states to decide what they want to do with old front-end arcs on the market. we have now reached an agreement on this, and i am very happy about that. this is an important road safety measure and i thank everyone for the work and for the agreement we have reached. finally, the safety belts in buses and other vehicles than cars. the koch and costa reports are also very important to me. i agree with what the commissioner and renate sommer have said. i would like to point out that it is important for everyone that we use seat belts in buses and that we get seat belts in buses. a seat belt on a bus is good for me if i use it, but it's also good if my neighbor uses it. if you have an accident on a bus and you're not wearing your seat belt and you get thrown around on the bus, you hurt other passengers too. we often do not think about this. it is therefore important that we now have this directive on the use of seat belts in buses and lorries. we'll have it in every vehicle and they'll be used.
madam president, i would like to thank you. many citizens are concerned about the increase in obesity and the ill health it causes in many citizens and especially in already vulnerable groups, those with very low incomes. this is a major social problem, but perhaps above all a major problem for the individuals affected. that is why many individuals are trying to shop healthier and choose healthier foods. unfortunately, this is being exploited in a very bad way. many companies try to make products like breakfast cereals, soft drinks, and other products appear more useful than they really are. as a representative of the citizens, i think that we in the european parliament must ensure that no consumer is duped into buying products that he does not really want. we must therefore require that the whole content of the product be taken into account when making health claims. otherwise, i cannot support this report. of course, it is especially important that no one be deceived into buying alcoholic beverages, with the problems we know they cause, in the belief that they are beneficial. therefore, health claims cannot be used for alcohol. this is not a matter of deciding what people should eat, but of giving people the right and opportunity to make informed choices for themselves. that is why i find it difficult to understand that the ppe group, for example, might think that companies should be allowed to mislead consumers. do you really think that companies can't make products that consumers want without having to trick them into buying them?
mr president, i would like to thank you. as regards the economic guidelines which we have discussed today in connection with the goebbels report, i would say that it is important that we never underestimate the growing competition we are seeing from the asian economies as well as the american ones. in ten years' time, we will have 800 million middle-income earners in these countries. it will change the economic relationship and place great demands on our ability to exploit it. sometimes we also overestimate our own ability to meet this transformation. i am struck by the fact that we forget how many people are actually unemployed in europe and in the various countries. even in those countries where we report high employment rates. in sweden, the problem is that 20-25% are unemployed or have no job to go to because we have so many early retirees and because of sick leave and actual unemployment. this is a social problem, but it is also an economic problem in terms of our ability to create welfare because, as was said here before, it is not growth that creates jobs. it's jobs and entrepreneurs that create growth. we have good hope in the simple fact that we are successful in the countries and sectors of the economy where we have implemented reforms. in the telecommunications markets, where we have liberalized, we are most successful in the countries with the most liberalized economies. in countries that have reformed their labour markets, we see better developments in employment and in countries with taxes that facilitate business, we see more business. the problem for europe is not that we have too high interest rates and too little demand stimulus. on the contrary, we have historically low interest rates and a very large stimulus burden due to interest rates and high public spending. what we need is reform. in the guidelines we have agreed in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, we stress the need for a more flexible labour market, better conditions for business and a focus on productivity and competitiveness through more open markets. we will therefore support this report and make sure that the proposals we put forward in it are supported.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the two rapporteurs and in particular ana mato adrover for the good cooperation they have shown in the committee on social affairs and employment in rather difficult circumstances. let me say first of all that i think it is good that we have integrated guidelines, because employment policy and economic policy are integrated. we must also, as we have added in our proposals, ensure sustainable development, both economic, social and environmental. the situation in europe is not bright at the moment, growth is too low, unemployment is too high. i personally think that in terms of economic policy we should concentrate more on growth-enhancing measures throughout europe to get growth going. stabilisation policy is important - we have been more successful than expected - but we should now concentrate more on growth-enhancing measures. we face increased competition in the world. the question is how to meet it. will we face china and india with worse working conditions and lower wages? the answer is no. we will meet it with research and development, with better jobs, with investing in the workforce through skills development. we're going to see restructuring. we have seen restructuring and we will see it again. it is important how we implement the restructuring. the involvement of workers, not exclusion, participation and social responsibility of enterprises. if we don't, we're gonna see more demonstrations in the streets. it also provides better restructuring if the workers are involved in the process. we must create a labour market where we can reconcile work and parenting and then we cannot have too long working hours or rules at european level which allow for any length of working hours, because that disadvantages one sex. we must create conditions in the labour market so that both men and women can participate. finally, disadvantaged groups. we must make greater efforts with regard to people coming to europe as refugees, who are often excluded from the labour market, and with regard to the disabled and others, so that they too can participate to a much greater extent in the labour market.

in writing. the junilista considers that a one-year extension of the five-year programme for enterprise and entrepreneurship is not desirable. the programme does not have the cross-border character required to consider that the principle of subsidiarity has been respected. it should be the responsibility of the member states to facilitate access to finance and risk capital for smes, if they so wish, and to provide information on eu legislation, european standards and partners. for small businesses across europe, it is important that companies can try out different solutions in accordance with the principle of institutional competition. in order to contain the costs of an extended programme, we are voting against the report.
in writing. the report regrets that the european parliament was not consulted or informed about the measures relating to the work and organisation of europol and calls on the council to take measures to increase democratic control by involving parliament. the junilist believes that increased democratic, judicial and budgetary control of europol is desirable, but since the europol convention is an intergovernmental cooperation, it is the national parliaments and not the european parliament that should exercise this control. that is why we have voted against the report.
in writing. we believe that the european union's fisheries policy must be reviewed. over-fishing drives people in developing countries deeper into poverty and robs the marine environment of a vital link in the food chain. we believe that if the union concludes a fisheries agreement with a third country, the total amount of the financial compensation to that country should be financed by the member states of the union whose fishing vessels are taking advantage of the agreement in question. the member states may then decide whether they will finance this expenditure by charging fees to their fishing vessels. this is the junilist's position in principle on eu fisheries agreements with third countries. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. however, these proposals are extremely limited.
in writing. we support the framework agreement in principle in order to ensure the practical functioning of the european parliament and to ensure that the commission's activities, including its expert groups, are monitored. we have chosen to support the report despite the misleading wording in recital a, which claims that the creation of a constitution for europe would deepen democracy in the eu.
in writing. this report deals with the community's strategy for promoting the urgent objective of achieving equality between men and women. we have voted against the report because we have confidence that the national parliaments of the member states will deal with this important matter in an appropriate manner. we are opposed in principle to the general trend of the eu institutions seeking to gain influence and competence in an increasing number of areas.
in writing. the single market requires cooperation and harmonisation of the laws of the member states. however, it is important to take account of the proportionality of legislation and the role and importance of national parliaments. we believe that the member states should be the first to decide on rules on nutrition and health claims for food. we are critical of several of the commission's articles, such as the right of the commission to draw up nutritional profiles to be followed in health claims in connection with advertising (article 4) and regulations on which implied health claims are not to be allowed (article 11). however, the european parliament has tabled a number of positive amendments, which is why we have chosen to support the report as a whole.
in writing. the resolution sets out guidelines for the employment policies of the member states. although several of the proposals are good, we do not think that the eu should intervene in this area. employment policy is best decided in each member state, in accordance with its specific circumstances.
in writing. we do not share the views of the european parliament's committee on constitutional affairs on the establishment of a european external action service. the creation of this section is a step towards a common foreign and security policy for the eu in which the member states have completely relinquished this policy area to the union. we believe that each member state of the european union should continue to pursue its own foreign policy, which is shaped by the election of representatives to the national parliament, or alternatively by the election of the president, or by national referendums.
in writing. the report deals with the extension of the mandate of the european monitoring centre on racism and xenophobia to become a human rights agency. without respecting the outcome of the referenda in the member states, parliament proposes guidelines for the role and tasks of the agency as if the constitution had already entered into force. moreover, there is no reason and it is an unnecessary duplication of work to create another eu body to deliver opinions, recommendations and raise awareness in the field of human rights, an area where the council of europe, the osce and various un bodies are already doing excellent work. that is why the junilista has voted against the report.
in writing. the goebbels owninitiative report is another flagrant example of how the european parliament is trying to turn national policy into a common eu policy. we see many fairly sound views being expressed on how growth should be stimulated, the importance of relevant investment, how environmental initiatives can contribute to economic growth and how unemployment creates social injustice. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competitive' in the definition of the term 'competitive' in the definition of the term 'competitive'. institutional competition between member states will show which system is the most successful. such established best practice will then be followed by other member states in their own self-evident interest.
in writing. we support the report on the social agenda for the period 2006-2010. as for the text on a legislative initiative on a 'guaranteed minimum income', we interpret it as referring to what we in sweden call social contributions and not the introduction of a minimum wage system.
mr president, i would like to thank you. on wednesday, the global campaign drugs for neglected diseases initiative is launched. aids, malaria, and tuberculosis are well-known problems. less well-known are chagas' disease, sleeping sickness, and leishmaniasis. together with aids, tuberculosis, and malaria, these diseases kill 14 million people each year, mostly in poor countries. the disease causes more deaths globally than road accidents, famine and terrorism combined. three billion dollars would be enough to research better treatments for these diseases. the solutions are within reach if solidarity and humanity are mobilized. tsunami was a word unknown to many six months ago, but when the devastating power of that word became widely known, we were able to quickly mobilize support for those affected. now attention is being given to other lesser-known concepts, such as chagas' disease and leishmaniasis, which afflict millions of people. let us show the way again, to learn new concepts and take new steps against this scourge.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. last week's referendums in france and the netherlands were a dramatic reminder of how low the legitimacy of the european union has fallen in the eyes of the european people. the union is rightly perceived as an increasingly elite project. a political elite without a real mandate from the people is overriding citizens and their national institutions in more and more areas of political life. in sweden, a scientific survey of public confidence in some 20 different institutions is carried out each year. the commission and this house, the european parliament, are at the bottom of that list. we are many politically committed people who want to save the european integration project from attempts to create a european superstate. we want to return it to a confidential cooperation between independent and equal countries and limit it to areas such as the internal market and cross-border environmental problems. the attempts to push the union towards a united states of europe without popular support are now threatening the survival of the integration project. however, it is not only the attempts of the political elite to push for a supranational eu against the will of the citizens that undermine the union's legitimacy. it is common for european people to associate the eu with top management, bureaucracy and corruption. and in this particular debate in the house, it is corruption and fraud that we are dealing with. i would like to commend mr bösch for his report. it is carefully crafted and contains good proposals which i wholeheartedly support. at the same time, our work in the committee on budgetary control shows how difficult it is to tackle fraud. there's some fundamental systemic flaw that we can't get to. the money disappears year after year through agricultural policy and regional policy. there is little evidence of any marked improvement. officials who expose scandals in the eu bureaucracy are routinely sacked, fired, retired early. the history of the eu is littered with the corpses of all these whistle-blowers. some scandals are revealed by other means, but in normal cases there is never a legal process. this cannot continue and it is now commissioner kallas's responsibility to ensure that this development does not continue into the future and undermine the little trust that the eu population still has in the union.
mr president, mr frattini and mr de vries, you have been very helpful. first of all, i congratulate the rapporteurs on their improvements to the council's anti-terrorism proposal, which is trying to make the fight against terrorism more effective in several excellent ways. however, the balance between rights and freedoms must be maintained and our democratic european system improved. today, a car has almost better rights than a human being, because the car can have economic legislation removed by the european parliament and the ec court of justice. these issues highlight the need for a new constitution to better combat crime and terrorism. the question of data storage is a prime example of the end of the road for the pillar system. we need a new treaty to protect privacy and against disproportionate measures and impositions. data protection is needed in all legislation and not, as now, only in some. angelika niebler has well developed the criticism of the european parliament in this area, and i agree with her. we have not seen any evidence of the need for the proposed measures on the data storage side. the council has acted provocatively and decided to implement legislation to which the european parliament opposes. such actions weaken our democracy. that's not what we need right now. we need the other way. we need to strengthen democracy and strengthen action by the council, and i hope that in future the council will listen to the european parliament to a much greater extent than it has done so far.
in writing. we advocate that eu countries cooperate on cross-border issues where eu cooperation brings added value to what can be achieved within existing international cooperation structures. we have chosen to vote in favour of the report on transeuropean energy networks as a whole because this is an important cross-border european issue. we support the amendments that allow for limited top-down management. we welcome the idea of using the existing eu structural funds to finance the costs of this type of cooperation.
in writing. increasing energy efficiency is an important part of the strategy to achieve the kyoto protocol objective. however, we do not believe that the eu should set detailed energy saving targets or how to achieve them. the eu can and should intervene in genuinely transnational environmental issues, but it should not dictate energy policy objectives. our rejection of the european parliament's report does not mean that we support the commission's original proposal.
in writing. we are open to the idea of eu countries creating a web-based portal where member states provide relevant environmental indicators. we are critical of the formulation which allows the eu to implement similar measures in other policy areas. the rapporteur has not clearly specified which areas are relevant and to what extent. we also believe that the report is too detailed and that the member states are asked to provide data in too many areas. the geographical conditions vary between member states. thus, the same types of data and indicators are not relevant for all countries. for the reasons stated above, we have voted against parliament's report. we believe that the commission's proposal is less far-reaching and better formulated than the one presented by the rapporteur parliament.
in writing. junilistan supports institutional competition and therefore regrets that the commission has considered it necessary to regulate this market as well. today, the legislation in which the reinsurance is taken out is the applicable law, which means that market participants themselves choose the most appropriate legislation. the directive is not primarily a protection for citizens but concerns regulations for financial institutions and business-to-business (b2b) trade. these operators carry out their own risk assessments on a daily basis and do not need additional eu legislation. we are therefore voting against the commission's proposal, since we cannot vote against it.

in writing. the proposal on the storage of traffic data presented by sweden, ireland, france and the united kingdom aims to improve judicial cooperation in criminal matters. we fully support this objective, as it is important that the member states and the community provide the law enforcement authorities with adequate tools for their work. sweden has had great success in its law enforcement and the detection of serious crime by using preserved traffic data. the same applies to certain other member states. however, the four member states have not sufficiently explained the necessity and the reasonableness of the draft framework decision. it is therefore necessary that the four member states and/or the commission provide good examples of cases where the storage of traffic data has been of major importance for the prevention, investigation, detection and prosecution of criminal offences and offences, including terrorism, and clearly demonstrate the reasonableness and the need for legislation in this area in relation to the possible privacy implications that this would entail.
in writing. the junilists are in favour of increased cooperation between member states to combat terrorism and other serious crossborder crime. however, these are issues relating to intergovernmental police and judicial cooperation, the so-called third pillar issues. they are decided by the member states in the council and not by the supranational eu institutions. the evaluation of the results of the negotiations and the assertion of responsibility are thus the responsibility of the national parliaments - not the european parliament. the junilista can therefore not support parliament's amendments, although we would have supported the content of several of them if we were in the riksdag. as regards report a6-0162/2005, we support sweden's initiative in the council for a framework decision on simplified data and intelligence exchange between law enforcement authorities in the eu.
in writing. we support the majority of the aspects of the bosch report on fraud. the fact that different tax rates on tobacco in the eu member states lead to problems of cigarette smuggling is a known fact, as mentioned in paragraphs 33 and 36. we agree with these statements, but we are opposed to further reductions in tobacco taxes. lower tobacco prices increase consumption and have a negative impact on public health. we believe there are better ways to address the problem of smuggling. we cannot support the provision of mobile forces for customs authorities or the european external borders agency's involvement in customs espionage.
