mr president, i would like to thank you. now we are again faced with proposals to increase eu expenditure and thus revenue. the argument is that the eu is growing and therefore must cost more. the eu is growing both in numbers and in the constant transfer of tasks from the member states. is this a reasonable development? the obvious answer is no. the european countries which meet the requirements for membership must of course be welcomed. however, there is neither rational reason nor popular support for moving further political tasks from the member states to brussels. on the contrary, the widely-acclaimed but never respected principle of subsidiarity suggests that a number of powers should be transferred back from the eu institutions to the national parliaments. that this is the will of the people is shown by the referendums in france and the netherlands and by what we know of the opinion of the people in many other member states. we are witnessing the end of an era in the history of the european union, the end of an era in which the political elite could pursue its ambitions and plans without regard for the will of the people. the reason this has been possible in the past is that the party structure in western europe reflects 20th century political issues, ranked along a right-left scale. the established parties, with few exceptions, are in favour of a continuous shift of political power from the parliaments of the member states to brussels. the citizens cannot therefore express their opposition to this development in general elections to the european parliament. referendums on eu issues and sometimes elections to the european parliament open up the possibility for the european people to express their views on the role of the eu. then you take that opportunity and say no to the transfer of power. it is important that we keep this in mind now as we discuss the eu budget for 2007-13. there is no popular support for increased spending in a union where over 70% of the budget goes to agricultural policy and the regional funds. we reserve the right to reject the temporary committee's proposal for a budget line of 1.07 per cent of gnp in appropriations for payments and 1.18 per cent in commitments. we believe that the total payment appropriations should be within 1% of the eu countries' combined gnp, as proposed by the g6 countries.
madam president, i would like to thank you. what is at stake is a priority of union spending. not only must you be able to prioritize what is important, but you must also be able to give less priority to what is less important. we must also be able to prioritise between what are the common tasks of the union - the most important tasks - and what are the important tasks of the member states. we have not reached that stage in this work on the budgetary plan. if the commission's proposal had been implemented, it would have meant an increase in the swedish membership fee by almost a third. it had reduced the scope for the investments that had to be made in growth and new jobs in sweden, and it had created bigger budgetary problems. if we want europe to become the most competitive knowledge-based economy in the world, it is not reasonable to give priority to the contributions of the rich member states at the expense of developing a common infrastructure in our weakest regions. there has not yet been sufficient priority given to this. it is also not right that we should give priority to the marketing of european agriculture in the third world while neglecting the most important initiatives in the european knowledge and research community. if you, the council and the commission, are not able to ensure that in the negotiations we can now achieve the objective of 3% of the european economy to support knowledge and research, so that european research can be at the forefront of the european knowledge society, then you are failing to make one of the most important priorities that europe needs. the rapporteur has done a great job, with clear priorities. the commission did not do so, and the council now seems to be reducing the investment that should be made in research. don't do that to me! europe needs support for research.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i thank commissioner reding for his answer. i'm not sure whether to be reassured or worried, though. there is a conflict here between the rules of the internal market and the possibility of the public service to operate. i would urge the commission to be clearer than you are now that the public service really has full freedom to shape its activities, without this being seen as a restriction on the internal market. it is very important that the commission takes the opportunity to be clear also externally on this issue. there is a great concern because the public service has a great deal of commitment among our citizens. it is particularly important these days to show that we do not believe that the market should take over everything, but that culture and public service should continue to play their role.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the minimum tax level for alcoholic beverages may be as low as zero today, but others have opted for a higher tax level, mainly for health reasons. unlimited mobility imposes, through unfair tax competition, on virtually all countries the policy of the lowest taxed member state. this would be the effect if we use the term personal use without time limit. since many alcoholic beverages can be stored for a lifetime, a person may argue that a truckload of alcohol is for personal use for the next 40 years, leaving smugglers free to go. a 120-day principle allows for differences in consumption levels between countries and, for example, birthday celebrations, but still has a legal reference to what can be considered reasonable for personal use. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the word 'smoking' in the wording of the directive. that is hardly the view of the house, since it would be contrary to article 30 of the treaty.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to thank john bowis for this excellent report. there are many sick and health-care-demanding people across europe who have much to gain from the vision of increased opportunities to move across borders that we are promoting here. unfortunately, there are politicians and bureaucrats in europe who do not take kindly to the increased mobility and freedom of choice. they see freedom of choice as a threat to their own ability to plan and regulate, a threat to their own power. that is why in so many member states, including sweden, it may be impossible for a sick person to even seek treatment in another part of the country. in this report we make it clear that we are not on the side of the bureaucrats in this conflict, but on the side of the patients. i would like to stress, however, that i too believe that health care is a national concern. our job is to break down barriers and make sure that patients can seek care where they need it. free movement provides both competition between health systems that drive development and a more efficient use of scarce health resources. in sweden, patients may have to wait in queues for years for treatment that is out of reach in other countries. by breaking down barriers, we create the conditions for both development and efficiency. this will improve health care in our member states and provide more people with enough. i would like to ask my colleagues to vote in favour of amendment no 5, tabled by myself, john bowis and ria oomen-ruijten for the ppe-de group. the amendment adds a new paragraph 4a to the text, which does not stipulate that we should remove health care services from the services directive. if there are any service companies operating in an environment of regulation and monopoly that impedes mobility and competition, it is the healthcare companies. it is not even part of this debate, where we are discussing the mobility of patients, not of carers. it is important to emphasise the knowledge of health care in different countries. we need to educate patients so they can benefit from the opportunities we're opening up for them. i would therefore like to conclude by inviting you all to a seminar on 15 june in brussels. for the first time, we will present a real ranking of european health systems, with facts from the patient's perspective. welcome to the club, everybody.
mr president, i would like to thank you. "never have so many europeans had so few things to be thankful for", i say to the french people. they showed, along with the dutch people, that they are tired of the establishment continuing to push through proposals that move power away from the people to the closed rooms of government offices and the boardrooms of big corporations. the problem is not that the eu is moving too fast, but in the wrong direction. the eu is stuck in yesterday's problems. the eu integrates states and maximises production instead of integrating people and maximising social and environmental protection. stop torturing the constitution. let it die in peace and call a new process where each municipality has its own convention. then, use their proposals for european cooperation with a local dimension as the basis for a new draft.
in writing. a firm prioritisation of the european union's resources is crucial to bringing about the changes europe needs. the enlargement and integration of the union's countries must be given priority, as must the fight against crime and the task of making the eu the most competitive economy in the world. the support for the weakest regions must be given priority, as must the ambition to increase the eu's research budget substantially. the eu's role in combating poverty, fighting infectious diseases, climate change, etc. also needs to be given priority. we cannot accept an increase in administrative costs of 3% per annum. low taxes on labour and business are a prerequisite for making europe a successful economy. this requires room in the fiscal policies of the member states. we therefore consider that the levels of commitments and payments adopted by the european parliament are too high. in order to reduce the eu budget, we want to review the 2002 agreement on agricultural expenditure and review regional aid with a re-prioritization of the poorest regions. for these reasons, we abstained in the final vote - and did not vote against - because we can support substantial parts of parliament's proposals. but we cannot give our support to the excessive level of expenditure, especially since the distribution is not in line with our priorities.
in writing. the written reservation by lars wohlin to the böge report and the speech by hélène goudin in parliament on 7 june 2005 summarise well why junilistan chose to vote against this report. we support the demand of the swedish government and the other five countries that the eu budget should not exceed 1.00% of gnp, either in payments or commitments. the eu must meet its commitments to the 10 new member states, but this expenditure is contained in the current spending framework. the difference between our demand for 1.00% and the european parliament (and commission) proposal is that the eu-15 (except greece and portugal) must first of all refrain from support from the cohesion fund. the junilists welcome the co-financing initiative and hope that it will open the way to the future renationalisation of, among other things, agricultural policy. we also support the idea of a correction mechanism to compensate those countries which under the current system account for a disproportionate share of eu revenue. the correction mechanism will hopefully also be an alternative to the english rebate. both france and the netherlands have shown by their rejection of the constitution that they prefer less sovereignty to more. a swedish veto in the council against a bad compromise is a better alternative than a new longterm budget. a 'no' to this report is a 'yes' to greater subsidiarity.
in writing. the current rules on excise duties are sometimes unclear and difficult to apply. this leads to unnecessary additional work for individuals and businesses. we therefore welcome the commission's review of the rules on excise goods. at the same time, we are critical of several of the commission's proposals for solutions. that is why we have voted against the european parliament's legislative resolution today, which largely supported even the weaker parts of the commission's proposal. we believe that only goods transported by a private individual should be considered as a private transport and thus be exempt from taxation in the country of origin. both tobacco and alcohol may be exempted from the general requirement that excise duty be paid in the member state where the consumption of goods acquired for private use takes place. the reason for our position is that tobacco and alcohol are detrimental to public health. these products should therefore not be treated as any other product. we are also moving towards allowing private individuals to transport large volumes of fuel oil themselves. we believe that special excise duties should be allowed on goods which are harmful to our environment. in this way, you can control people's choices so that they become more environmentally friendly. in addition, traffic and transportation are safer if handled by professionals.

in writing. today, for reasons of public health, i have decided not to support the report by mr rosati on the amendment of the directive on the general rules for excise goods such as alcohol and tobacco. neither the commission's proposal nor the european parliament's report protects public health to the extent that i consider desirable. when used correctly and in conjunction with other measures, indicative reference levels can play an important role in determining whether goods are held for commercial purposes or for the personal use of individuals. i therefore believe that the reference levels should be maintained in order to limit the harmful effects of alcohol and to reduce overall alcohol consumption. in addition, special efforts should be made to harmonise excise duty levels with the stated objective of reducing tobacco and alcohol related harm.
in writing. both the commission and the european parliament want to eliminate in practice the possibility of limited import quotas for alcohol and tobacco. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of certain substances in the production of certain products. the swedish, finnish and danish governments instead want the indicative levels to be halved and to be regarded as maximum limits for private imports. the junilist therefore supports the position of the swedish, finnish and danish governments in the council. fortunately, this is an area where sweden still has a veto in the council, which is why this proposal should not be a threat to existing legislation.
in writing. the junilists believe that police and judicial cooperation should be intergovernmental, that criminal law should not be harmonised at eu level and that a european public prosecutor's office should not be set up. immigration and asylum policy should remain national in order to avoid the creation of a fortress europe. in addition, member states should determine the design of their democratic institutions, taking into account the copenhagen criteria and the european convention on human rights and fundamental freedoms. we cannot therefore support the resolution.
in writing. an effective anti-discrimination policy is important, as is a well-functioning minority protection system. however, these are issues which, in accordance with the copenhagen criteria, are for each member state to consider and should not be decided at eu level. the junilist believes that the council of europe and the european court of justice should be responsible for the political and legal controls.
mr president, i would like to thank you. eu member states have different views on how to reform the un system. let the member states discuss this with the other un members. this discussion should not be conducted within the framework of eu cooperation but in a broader international context. it is clear that an eu strategy for reforming the un system is largely aimed at safeguarding european rather than global interests. the resolution stresses that the aim is that eu countries will in the long term speak with a single voice in the security council. we are against this proposal. it is clear that the european countries have different foreign policy positions. this was particularly evident in the context of the iraq war. france and the united kingdom are examples of countries that often have different views on foreign policy issues. would it be possible at all to speak with a single european voice in the security council? would the voices of small countries like sweden be heard at all?
replacement of the questioner. thank you for your answer. i must ask, however, whether the judgment of the turkish supreme court on 25 may is not alarming. this sentence deprives children of their natural right to be taught in their mother tongue and to develop in their own cultural background. the judgment of the supreme court is a violation of human and democratic rights. in addition to violating these rights, the judgment also dissolves the teachers' union, thus depriving it of the right to represent its 200,000 members. it's a violation of the ilo convention. the council should inform turkey that the door which has been opened can be closed again if human rights are not respected.
madam president, i would like to thank you. when that judgment was given, i put a question to the commission on this subject. it is a matter of civil and human rights, and we are all appalled by what has happened. my question to the council is: will this be coordinated, given that this is a matter for the council and the commission as well as for the european parliament? what happened is very serious. i would like to know whether you are coordinating this or whether we will resume the discussion when i receive my reply from the commission.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i question whether the eu is really interested in achieving a sustainable exploitation of fisheries resources. it is contradictory that the eu is advocating this in the report, while the union has fisheries agreements with countries in, for example, africa. these third country agreements contribute to the depletion of the world's oceans and hamper the ability of many developing countries to build a profitable fishing industry of their own. a recent report by the swedish fisheries agency criticises eu agreements with third countries. the criticism is that none of these agreements promote sustainable fishing and are not implemented in a way that complies with the fao code of conduct. several ngos share this view and have pointed out that the agreements have a predominantly negative impact on coastal communities. if sustainable development of fisheries resources is indeed a priority for the eu, the union should not only promote action in the mediterranean, as in this report, but also advocate and implement a radical review of the union's fisheries agreements with third countries. in this report parliament has made changes to the commission's proposal in favour of the fishing industry. this does not promote sustainable development. the eu should not be working on fisheries issues. this type of issue should be addressed at regional and subregional level as far as possible. fisheries issues requiring international cooperation can be handled with advantage in the framework of international bodies where national special interests are not as evident as in the eu.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank duarte freitas for a good report which the verts/ale group will support. industrial tuna fishing is not only threatening tuna stocks but also other species. the most famous is the dolphin. the agreement on the international dolphin conservation programme was reached because many of our citizens wanted to protect dolphins from dying as a result of poor fishing practices. it is important that these objectives are met as we now ensure that the agreement is implemented in our legislation. the work has been quite successful so far, as my colleagues have described. amendments nos 9 and 11, which the verts/ale group tabled in committee and which were supported there, are important to ensure that consumers are not deceived by the labelling. a reference to article 3 of the 2003 regulation is important to also give all those who meet the conditions the right labelling requirements and to ensure that no one can profit from it in an unscrupulous way. we cannot but mention it, because this is parliament's opportunity to express this will to you as you negotiate further. we also need to evaluate the dolphin-safe methods. they can get better, so they don't harm other species. so-called log sets kill sea turtles and many other species. i know that the aidcp has started work to improve these methods. it is important that we keep abreast of developments and use the best possible methods.
in writing. we believe that it should be up to each member state to decide whether to accede to the convention on the conservation of dolphins. this is not a matter for the european parliament.
in writing. we are against the eu paying financial aid to the fishing industry in connection with biological recovery periods. this position does not mean that we support the commission's proposal. we welcome certain phrases in the parliament's report, such as that measures relating to migratory species should be handled within regional fisheries organisations. we are voting against the report as a whole because we do not think that the eu should be working on fisheries issues. we are in favour of regional fisheries issues being handled through cooperation between the countries concerned.
in writing. to avoid the formation of a fortress europe, it is important that we do not create a single european asylum and refugee policy. it should be left to each member state to pursue a more generous policy in this area if it so wishes, which is prevented if the member states are bound by common eu standards. the junilist cannot therefore support the report.
in writing. we chose to vote in favour of the european parliament's own-initiative report on patient mobility in the final vote. we did so because we believe that the text of the resolution as a whole expresses our views on health care. however, there are parts of the european parliament's resolution which express certain supranational ambitions in the field of health care which we cannot accept. each country is responsible for the health and medical care of its citizens. when residents are temporarily in another eea country, their european health insurance card entitles them to unplanned treatment there. we think this is a good system and it seems to be working well. citizens have the right to influence how their tax money is spent. if patients are to be able to choose health care in another member state and have it paid for by the taxpayers of their home country, that principle is threatened. we also fear that such a system would be very costly and favour patients with more resources at the expense of the most vulnerable patients. we also believe that healthcare issues should not be dealt with in the proposal for a service directive.
in writing. eu citizens are currently entitled to healthcare in other member states and to receive reimbursement for this care in their home country. this is considered to be a natural part of the internal market. we welcome a uniform framework clarifying the right to seek medical care in another eu country. we therefore welcome the commission communication on patient mobility (com (2004) 0301). the report by parliament deals with areas which are outside the measures necessary to achieve satisfactory rules on access to health care in another eu country. the report covers everything from telemedicine to eu research on patient mobility. we have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. several of the proposals in the resolution are good on how to reform the united nations, including an enlarged and more representative security council, strengthened environmental work and an emphasis on the link between development and the environment. nevertheless, we cannot support the report because it also seeks to strengthen the eu's common foreign policy. the eu is also committed to the principle of subsidiarity, which is enshrined in the treaty on european union. the latter proposal clearly shows that the eu has the ambition to act as a state in international contexts, which we clearly oppose. in our opinion, the swedish people should make their voice heard directly in the un, not through the eu.
in writing. the resolution can be seen as the european parliament's wish list for the forthcoming eu-us summit in washington on 20 june 2005. it contains proposals for eu-us cooperation in a range of areas, including security policy, the environment, energy and the economy. several of the demands are good, but we do not believe that the eu should represent 25 member states vis-à-vis the united states, including in the field of security policy. that is why we cannot support the resolution.
in writing. the resolution on the situation in uzbekistan is another example of the european parliament's ambition to strengthen the union's common foreign and security policy. we oppose this development. uzbekistan is not, in our view, in the eu's immediate vicinity. we believe that other organisations - such as the un, the osce and the council of europe - have a mandate in this case to act in conflict prevention and to strengthen human rights. we therefore support the call for an independent inquiry into the situation in the country initiated by the un, with the support of the osce, among others.
in writing. on employment and production and the contribution of these factors to economic growth and social integration in the new member states both reports contain general recommendations on how eu member states should conduct their economic policies. although we can support several of the proposals in substance, it is not the eu's task to define this policy. the countries themselves should find the appropriate solutions to the design of economic policy through institutional competition. the countries which introduced transitional labour mobility rules for the ten new member states should be entitled to retain them. but it is a good idea to review the rules because the problem of free labour immigration from the new eu countries has been dramatically exaggerated. for the reasons stated above, we vote against both reports.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the eu is clearly in crisis. the clear 'no' vote in the two referendums has left the whole of the eu in a kind of vacuum of great uncertainty about the future. i am of course sorry that the french and the dutch rejected the constitutional treaty, but such a clear 'no' must be respected. it is therefore wise to pause and take some time now to analyse, listen and reflect on how we can move forward to implement the reforms that the eu so badly needs. the crisis was of course aggravated by the 'budget fiasco', but in many ways no budget is better than a bad budget. i don't think this budget was very modern. but we cannot put the whole of the eu in a freezer and wait for the mistrust that many feel towards the eu to suddenly disappear. more than ever, we need leadership, someone who dares to stand up for visions, for european cooperation and to defend the decisions taken in brussels. in this context, i would like to pay tribute to the president-in-office for his outspoken speech and for his attempt to reconcile very different views. we cannot allow the wet wettex cloth that has spread all over europe to paralyse cooperation. we have a lot of work to do. we have the lisbon process and the mythical services directive that is really needed. we must fight cross-border crime. we have the ambitious environmental work of reach, and there's a lot of work to do in foreign policy. the list of foreign policy measures 33 pages of council conclusions was indeed written before the council even began, but it nevertheless gives the appearance of great unity and high ambitions, which is very good. the eu has a major role to play in the world and must continue to do so. however, i am concerned about enlargement, madam president. the council's conclusions are almost ignored. we cannot take the referendums as a means of putting enlargement on hold in any way. the enlargement of the union to ten new member states just over a year ago is a glorious success for the whole of the european union. bringing east and west together is the best thing we've ever done. the european home, which at the moment may not feel so cosy and inhabited, must be open to our neighbours when they meet the necessary requirements. we cannot pretend that the enlargement process is also being put on hold and that they are being left to wait. in many ways, europe is the answer to the questions in these countries, even though it may not seem like the answer to their questions to our citizens. soon all the institutions will have summer vacation. perhaps more than ever, we need to take a few weeks of rest, a pause for reflection, and to consider how we are to move forward. i think in the long run it will be seen that something good will come out of this crisis. europe is worth it, but then we must really make an effort to ensure that the pause in the ratification process does not also become a pause for european cooperation as a whole. that would be disastrous.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the french and dutch referendums and last week's summit really exposed the european project as an elite project with no popular support. to call the people's vote a crisis is to demonstrate clearly that one does not really believe in a democratic europe. we must now state that the draft constitution has been defeated under the union's own rules. two of the founding countries have already said no. it's settled. so the question is: what are we gonna do now? in my view, we should convene a new convention that is democratically elected so that it reflects the opinion of the people on this issue and not the opinion of the elite. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. it was an attempt to stuff the people of europe with something they didn't want. firstly, there is a need to revise existing treaties in such a way that they are easy to understand and to understand, which is not a major political issue, but simply a matter of simple revision. secondly, we must review the rules for decision-making in the areas where we have already said that we will take joint decisions. thirdly, there were all sorts of plans for a common defence, a common foreign policy and a whole host of other things which are absolutely not to be discussed in this context. it should not be in something that looks like a constitution, but it is a matter for the people of europe to discuss in the future. we shall start from scratch, show respect for the popular vote and then look at it from a democratic point of view with democratically elected citizens in a convention.
mr president, council, commissioner wallström and members of the public! when you took over the presidency in luxembourg, you had to deal with the tsunami, a natural disaster. you're going to end up with a summit without a budget and a new treaty. the excellent foreign policy decisions are unfortunately not widely known. thank you for the many results! in the meantime, luxembourg has ensured that bulgaria and romania have become members and that turkey can start negotiations in october. you have made history. thank you for that! europe is not in crisis, because the debate is not about countries wanting to leave the eu. the referendums have said more with the right things, the council the right things better for the future. the citizens want us to clarify the objectives, i.e. more jobs, order and clarity in the labour market, social responsibility, international solidarity, the fight against crime, sustainable development and a europe of equality. finally, we are having a debate in our member states about the future that the citizens of the eu now want to be part of. it is not enough that we here in brussels do a lot of good things if no one knows about it or cares. i am hopeful and look forward to a lively debate, but this time it is with all those who wish to participate. so let us not turn to ashes, yellow cards or deprecation. a pause for thought, but not for inaction! let us challenge citizens and demand engagement and answers to questions that guide how we want the eu to develop. we need to listen and talk less. it is difficult for us politicians, but we will come out of this period of reflection stronger. finally, the debate is about politics, about the future and the aims of the european union, and i welcome it.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the swedish junilist believes that members of the european parliament should receive a salary from their respective national parliaments and receive a uniform remuneration from the european parliament. the principle of subsidiarity shall be the guiding principle. it is for each member state to determine the remuneration of its members. the national remuneration of meps is adjusted to the actual salaries and the cost situation in each member state. members should receive remuneration from their home country and pay tax in their respective home countries. in principle, we believe that the eu should not set the salary of the members of parliament elected by the citizens of each of the eu countries. it would be unreasonable for an mep in a low-wage country to be paid several times more than people in similar or higher posts in their home country. a common statute for meps could, for example, have the effect of making the meps of one country receive several times more than the head of state or government of that country. it is important that meps are not regarded as a privileged elite by the voters in their home country, and that the conditions are more or less in line with the national pay situation for corresponding positions. the current system of travel allowances without regard to actual costs, which is based on flat-rate allowances, is in our view completely unreasonable. members shall be reimbursed only for the actual costs of their travel. the travel allowance system can be reformed without the adoption of a common statute. for the reasons stated, the junilista has chosen to vote against the proposal. in conclusion, i would point out that the members of parliament are called members, 'député', in the statutes. it seems strange, then, that in some lands people refer to themselves as honorable, honourable. such expressions are obsolete in a democratic eu.
for the pse group. mr president, commissioner frattini, you are here. i would like to begin by thanking michael cashman and others for a very good report. as asylum, refugee policy and immigration are issues which we in the committee devote a lot of time to, it was necessary first to have clear rules on the community code and the border crossings for persons. the fact that we can now re-establish controls and jointly tighten up the external borders makes the eu's borders better. the opportunities for traffickers, importers of women and children for prostitution, criminals and drug traffickers to move freely across borders are being hampered, while people, despite their foreign appearance, and groups can now feel welcome. when we vote yes tomorrow, we can tackle one of the committee's major tasks, asylum and immigration policy. we have so many common questions about how we in the eu should safeguard these very values. we want a more open europe, but that requires common solutions and respect for countries, respect between countries and between people and groups. this is the first step. thank you again for a very good report!
mr president, the lisbon strategy is a very important tool to enhance european economic growth and when we negotiated this in the parliament we were very clear that we wanted sustainable economic growth. despite this, in your speech, mr blair, you hardly mentioned environmental questions at all. environmentally driven industry has two and a half times the growth rate that is normal in european industry. environmentally driven questions are also key to economic growth, so i want a message on this from you, mr blair. secondly, i am negotiating the chemical directive as rapporteur. right now that is one of the very few things that can deliver a result to the european citizen during your presidency. therefore it would be very nice to also have a clear result on this to ensure that the future is not blurred but blaired.
mr. speaker, this is prime minister blair. europe is better off than ever. i say this because a large part of the current debate is about the view on enlargement. i am one of those who believe that enlargement with the ten new member states has made europe safer, more stable and has created better conditions for peace and prosperity than ever before. for me, this is an argument for further enlargement, not an argument for trying to close europe's borders now. one of the main tasks of the new leadership in europe will be to anchor and develop the vision of how europe can create greater security and greater prosperity by linking the countries now facing enlargement. prime minister blair, i believe that the problems we face today in the eu are linked to the fact that europe is better than ever. at the same time, this makes the european union a greater challenge than before. it is not, as is sometimes said, a conflict between the old and the new europe or a conflict between a free-trade europe and political union. the european union is a political union and must face its task in that light. it is the break from the old eu, the old european union of six, nine, twelve or fifteen members, to the european union of 25 member states of today. this requires respect for variety and diversity and also an ability to take advantage of the dynamics that exist throughout europe. this requires openness towards the new member states. it requires the ability to find common denominators, to invest in the knowledge society instead of the subsidy society, to invest in new enterprises instead of making agricultural aid an obstacle to the research and development we need. i hope, on behalf of the swedish moderates, that you will lay the foundations for the new leadership that the new europe and the new european union need.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank prime minister blair for an inspiring speech. i share the view that europe needs to change. i share the view that the eu needs to be more active in the world. i share the view that the eu is not just a free trade area but also a political union. i also share the views on the budget. the british rebate is old, but just as old is agricultural policy and what we are investing in in agricultural policy today. this too needs to change. we need to invest in research and development, skills development and more. what we talk about during and after important meetings must be translated into action. i also share the view of the social europe. it is true that you were involved in introducing the social protocol, the employment strategy and the active labour market policy into eu cooperation. all of this was good, but we have to move on and find a balance. we must therefore have a proposal for a services directive which balances trade in services, because that is what we need. at the same time, we need good labour market conditions, high environmental standards and consumer rights. we also need a working time directive which takes account of what you said about combining family and working life, so that neither of us is working to death and so that we do not compete with long working hours. we need a staffing directive, not to break the legs of the staffing companies, but to create a framework. if you manage to open up trade and open up borders, but at the same time develop social europe, then we in the socialist group will support you.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i just want to point out a mistake in the swedish version. what is article 23 (2) in other language versions is article 12 (7) in the english version.
in writing. today parliament voted on the long-debated statute for members. there are some dubious aspects, but for the most part it is an improvement on the existing rules on travel allowances, taxes and pensions. reimbursement will be for actual travel costs instead of a high standard fee. the statute also provides that all members will receive a common salary from 2009. this is particularly important in order to ensure that our colleagues from the new member states receive a fair level of pay and to reduce the pay gap between those who earn the least and those who earn the most. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover the whole of the european union. we have long supported the move towards a statute for assistants and reimbursement of actual costs. we will continue to do so in the future. however, in order to ensure that the statute agreed with the council is adopted, we have today voted against all the amendments tabled in plenary and voted in favour of the committee's report. a change in the compromise compromises the unity of the council and thus the possibility of agreeing on the statute of members at all.
in writing. we want to emphasize once again our principled position on this matter. we believe that members of the european parliament should receive their salaries from the respective national parliaments. the principle of subsidiarity shall be the guiding principle. it is for each member state to determine the remuneration of its members. the national allowances of members of the european parliament are adjusted to take account of the actual wage and cost situation in each member state. members should receive remuneration from their home country and pay tax in their respective home countries. it is important that meps are not regarded as a privileged elite by the voters in their home country, but that the conditions are more or less in line with the national pay situation for corresponding positions. the current system of travel allowances, without regard to actual costs, and based on flat-rate allowances, is in our view totally unreasonable. members shall be reimbursed only for the actual costs of their travel. the travel allowance system can be reformed without adopting a common statute. we have voted against the proposal for a common statute.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. the voluntary pension fund should also have been abolished with immediate effect. instead, the conference of presidents should implement the plenary's decision on travel reimbursement for actual costs instead of blocking this reform as hitherto in order to use the issue of travel reimbursement in the negotiations with the council.'
in writing. in principle, the left party believes that it is wrong to vote on one's own salaries and allowances. the left party supports the proposal to finally abolish the standard allowance for members' travel. it is positive that compensation should henceforth be paid on the basis of actual costs. the left party, however, does not support the proposal on wages, as the level of wages is too high. political appointees must not become an economic elite through high salaries. the tax is of course to be paid nationally, not eu-wide. the criticism also applies to an overly favourable pension system. for the reasons i have given, i shall abstain from voting on the final vote.
in writing. the junilist broadly supports the compromise agreed between the council, the commission and parliament. the code clarifies many points of previously unclear rules, which are scattered among several sources of law. in particular, we welcome the strengthening of protection against discrimination in the conduct of border checks and the possibility for third-country nationals who have been refused entry to appeal against decisions. however, we do not share the rapporteur's view that the ecj should be given jurisdiction to rule on matters relating to the reintroduction of internal border controls and therefore welcome the fact that the council has not accepted these proposals. the junilist group believes that each member state should reserve the right to re-establish its own border controls if it considers this necessary to combat drug smuggling, illegal trafficking in human beings and human trafficking, and if it is necessary to withdraw from schengen.
in writing. the report aims to create a legal basis for cooperation and trade relations with the following industrialized countries: the united states, canada, japan, south korea, australia and new zealand. the cooperation will include support for the centre for european affairs. trade cooperation includes exchanges, conferences and leadership training. we support the eu's common commercial policy, but have difficulty justifying the budgeted cost of the measures of eur 17 million until 2007. the results of the french and dutch referendums show that the eu's bureaucracy must be reduced. that is why unnecessary expenditure must also be eliminated. in view of this, we are voting against the report.
in writing. junilista supports both un resolutions on darfur and the congo, which imply that persons obstructing the peace process, violating humanitarian law and human rights or breaking arms embargoes should be prevented from travelling within the territory of the member states and that the funds, financial assets and economic resources that may be associated with them should be blocked. we do not, however, want to contribute to the development of a common foreign and security policy for the eu. all member states are obliged to implement the measures decided by the un security council under chapter vii of the un charter, and therefore a joint decision at eu level is not necessary. the junilista has therefore voted against the reports.
in writing. we are opposed to the eu's common fisheries policy. however, we have chosen to support this report because parliament's position is closer to the recovery plan adopted by the northwest atlantic fisheries organisation (nafo) than the commission's proposal.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted today in favour of the report on security research. we believe that it is important to develop a structured and effective security research programme in order to guarantee, among other things, the credibility of the eu and a qualified intelligence capability in the context of the european security and defence policy. however, we object to certain points in the report, on the one hand those relating to the budgetary allocation for security research, which the rapporteur relates to a percentage of gdp, and on the other hand those relating to worldwide observation and data collection.
in writing. the report aims to develop a european security research programme (esrp) to be launched in 2007. we are not opposed in principle to joint research efforts at european level, but we are opposed to the esdp's objective of contributing to an integrated european defence market. we therefore vote against the report as a whole.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the second reading recommendation before us is a success for consumers. it is a success for the health of children and a very good example of how the precautionary principle should be applied. when we have concrete warnings from the scientific experts that public health is threatened, it is our duty as politicians to act. one important reason why we have achieved this success in parliament is that the rapporteur, mr trakatellis, has been so firm and decisive in the negotiations. i would like to thank him for the way in which he has carried out this work which has enabled us to reach this agreement, so that even the last three controversial phthalates can be banned in products which are children's toys and are used for children between three and six years of age. there has been a great deal of discussion in the committee about the amount of scientific evidence needed to make such a decision. i think we know enough when several of the recognised committees say that this is dangerous and involves risks. then we have an obligation to act. this is the precautionary principle. there is another aspect of this decision which i would also like to highlight, namely that it is now more comprehensive by including products relating to child hygiene, which is a fairly large market for these products. it is important that we have got this through the negotiations and that these products are covered by the decision. of course, our whole group will support the proposal for compromise with the council of ministers.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. we've learned that a monopoly situation doesn't work well. we have also learned that a deregulated market requires quality control, both in terms of the diversity of production to guarantee the supply of electricity and to meet demand, and in terms of the control of the network itself and its structure. we have also learned that we have to check that these investments are actually being made. in sweden we have had a very long power cut after the big storm last winter. it became very clear that none of these quality controls had been fully implemented. this means not only that industrial production is halted, but that other vital functions of society are affected and that the quality of life of people is dramatically reduced. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, but also the commission, on an excellent and pioneering work on value for voters.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this is a very complex issue that is the subject of enormous lobbying by various groups. i think it's great that for once the citizens care about what we do in this house. we're not spoiled for choice. a well-functioning patent system in europe is important. individual inventors and companies must of course be able to patent their inventions, including those related to computers. the trend today, however, is that pure software is also patented, which is not good. we need researchers, innovators and individual software developers who can come up with new ideas without risking infringing trivial patents and endless lawsuits. firstly, we should reject the whole proposal, calling on the commission to come back with a comprehensive proposal for a community patent covering all inventions. it is wrong to break out software-related inventions as a separate sector. secondly, we must change the common position, which is not a good legal text. it contains too many ambiguities. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, i would like to thank you. today, we can choose whether we want an old-fashioned patent law with an old-fashioned view of big business and an old-fashioned view of development or a modern, small-scale, creative development with a modern law. everyone says they don't want software patents. we've seen the disaster in the united states when you do that. the only way to prove that today is to vote for the 21 compromise amendments that i and many others have signed. we will then have intelligent, balanced legislation that supports creativity and a sound patent system. if you vote against it, you're putting yourself in the hands of the big corporations. it leads to legislation that makes software that controls a device patentable. it's like patenting the manuals of appliances. such a world is not modern.
in writing. we welcome the european parliament's resolution on an information and communication strategy for the euro and economic and monetary union (emu). at the same time, we voted against the european parliament's call to turn swedish opinion in favour of swedish entry into emu and a transition to the euro. we believe that the results of the swedish referendum should be respected. the question of swedish membership of emu and the euro is not relevant for the foreseeable future.
in writing. i have voted against the own-initiative report on an information and communication strategy for the euro and for economic and monetary union for two main reasons. firstly, i believe that it is up to each member state to decide how it wishes to conduct the debate on the euro. it should not be handled from the commission's side. secondly, the experience of central european campaigns is generally poor and represents a waste of resources which should not be promoted. discussions on monetary policy and currency should instead take place through a dialogue between citizens and policy makers. that is why i voted against this report today.
in writing. i cannot support the european parliament's resolution on an information and communication strategy for the euro and economic and monetary union (emu). i think it is up to each member state to decide whether it wants an information and communication strategy or campaigns for the euro and emu. i also believe that the swedish referendum result of 2003, when the swedes voted against the euro, should be respected. the question of swedish membership of emu and the introduction of the euro is not an issue for the foreseeable future.
in writing. i believe that sweden should join emu and i share the rapporteur's positive attitude towards the euro. i have, however, chosen to abstain on this report. one of the main conclusions of the report is that the commission should have an information campaign on the excellence of the euro. i believe that information campaigns by the commission are neither helpful nor appropriate if we want to get more citizens to be sympathetic to the euro. this is not what we're going to use taxpayers' money for.
in writing. today i have chosen to cast my vote on the report by jules maatens on a communication strategy for the euro and emu. i share the rapporteur's positive attitude towards the euro and believe that sweden should join emu. what i do not support, however, is the commission's information campaign on the excellence of the euro. information campaigns by the commission are not the way to get more citizens to be in favour of the euro. this is not what we should be using taxpayers' money for.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted today in favour of a report supporting the fight against harmful child labour. it is essential to combat vigorously child labour harmful to children's physical or mental health and in accordance with the ilo conventions on child labour. companies have an important role to play and a responsibility to combat this type of child labour. the establishment of appropriate community safeguard clauses and mechanisms to detect and prosecute eu-based importers of products manufactured by children is a well-intentioned proposal. however, we moderates believe that this creates considerable legal uncertainty, which risks, rather than protecting the interests of children, the withdrawal of companies from the regions where there is a risk of child labour, rather than engaging in trade and investment.
in writing. the european parliament voted today in favour of a proposal for measures to combat child labour. child labor is something most people think is part of history, and so do i. unfortunately, not all countries of the world have followed suit. that is why i voted today in favour of a number of additional proposals which divide child labour into several categories, since child labourers, for example, may be the only alternative to prostitution.
in writing. today the european parliament adopted a report proposing various measures to combat child labour. the report states, inter alia, that education for all is essential to combat child poverty and poverty reduction. it is my firm belief that all children must be protected from harmful work. it is important to remember, however, that blanket bans are not a realistic option as long as poverty is widespread and educational opportunities are limited. some forms of work can contribute positively to this, for example by providing training at the workplace. the report also addresses corporate liability and proposes, inter alia, prosecution of companies importing products manufactured in violation of the ilo core conventions. i believe that the potential of industry to strengthen respect for human rights and international conventions should be fully exploited. i therefore strongly support initiatives such as the global compact and global responsibility (swedish government). however, legislation and the possibility of prosecution at eu level is well advanced. there is a risk that this would rather be contrary to its purpose, which is to improve the situation of children and to combat poverty.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the commission, it seems to me that the proposal which is beginning to emerge from parliament is well in line with the longterm budget proposal we presented earlier, which is logical. i shall concentrate on the european social fund. the social fund has two general tasks. the first is to strengthen the employment strategy, and the second is to promote social inclusion and combat discrimination. this has become increasingly important. there are two major challenges facing the eu in the new society, in the globalised society, with the demographic changes. that is why these objectives are more important than ever. lifelong learning is essential to boost employment. europe will not be able to compete with india and china, with their social model, with their wages and conditions on the labour market. instead, we must compete, among other things, through research and development and the skills of the eu workforce. this is of course the responsibility of the member states, companies and social partners, but the eu can add value by strengthening skills across our territory. we have a number of disadvantaged groups who must be more involved in the development of society, but also in the labour market. we need all of them, and especially the women, in the labour market of the future to be able to cope with external competition. women are still underrepresented and discriminated against in the labour market. this also applies to people with disabilities, who are extremely excluded from the labour market today, as well as people from countries outside europe. let me make a few points. it is good that the transnational element is increasing. we're keeping the innovative element that has been in equal and other things. it is important that the social fund can interact with other funds to some extent at local and regional level. finally, i agree with mr špidla that it is important that the social partners and the partnership continue to play a prominent role in the work of the social fund.
mr president, i would like to thank you. europe needs a regional policy of solidarity. we have very large differences between regions in the eu, especially between new and old member states. this is not acceptable on a continent of prosperity. however, we cannot rely on the single economic market to bridge the gaps, and that is why we need the structural funds. the new regions should therefore receive a larger share of the structural funds, and the richer countries must have their own regional equalization policy. at the same time, however, eu countries must also emphasise that growth and research provide scope for regional development. the european parliament is now making a reasonable proposal, including a financial one. we also stress the importance of giving priority to environmental issues in the structural funds. finally, i would like to emphasize that the commission and the european parliament believe that there are regions with permanent geographical disadvantages, including rural and mountainous regions, which must also be compensated through the structural funds, since the geographical conditions do not change over time. i assume that the european council will also take this into account in the future budgetary plan.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the whole report is about the very foundation of all equality work, namely the right and the ability to be self-sufficient. i would like to highlight three aspects of this directive. the first point concerns equal pay for equal work. this is not new, but has been included in previous directives. however, the necessary reinforcement is also provided by the inclusion of the call for the social partners to take both initiative and responsibility for the principle of equal pay. despite the directive on equal pay, there are still large wage differences and discrimination is still taking place. the other part i want to point out is parental leave. parenting is no longer seen as a one-gender issue, but parents should be given the opportunity to share responsibility for their children. the third aspect i would like to highlight is equal treatment in working life. it must not only concern equal treatment of women established in the labour market, but also discrimination in recruitment and in terms of employment.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating mr dimitrakopoulos on the enormous work he has put into this complex but extremely important issue. after the prosecution of saddam hussein's regime, the iraqi people have now been given the opportunity to rebuild their country freely and democratically. the parliamentary elections, held under heavy pressure from forces that were not interested in democracy or free elections, were the first step in the democratization process. it was also a chance for politics to win over terror and violence, but it didn't. although a transitional government has been formed and the drafting of a new constitution has begun, the security situation in iraq has not stabilised as it should have. on the contrary, the situation is such that efforts and a strong international presence will be required for a long time to come. it is obviously better the sooner iraq itself ensures the security of the country. however, as we have seen in other conflicts in our neighbourhood, such as the wars in the balkans, we cannot expect quick results. building peace and democracy takes time, even if all the forces in the country are positive. even more time is needed in countries such as iraq or bosnia where there are various groups opposed to the democratization process. however, we must have time and patience. we cannot afford to fail to support iraq's political democratization efforts or to withdraw the international forces from iraq too hastily, which would endanger the peace not only in iraq but also in its neighbourhood. therefore, the only option for us is to continue to support iraq, both politically and through a strong international presence, for as long as it is necessary. ferrero-waldner rightly pointed out that europe is united today in its efforts to rebuild iraq. in my view, it is precisely this unity that would lead to the stabilization of the situation in iraq and the overcoming of the problems we have today.
in writing. the report seeks to bring the eu into line with unece regulations nos 94 and 95. the aim is to remove technical barriers to trade in the approval of vehicles with regard to the protection of occupants in the event of a head-on collision and a side-on collision. we choose to support the proposal because we support the eu's common commercial policy and consider it necessary to take measures to remove trade barriers. we also welcome the fact that this is happening within the framework of the un.
in writing. we have voted to withdraw the entire computer-related inventions bill. we are opposed to software patents and want to protect classical patent law and have had the ambition to improve the common position of the council of ministers. unfortunately, this has proved difficult and we risk having an unclear legal text that does not meet our requirements for legal certainty. this does not benefit large companies, small companies or software developers. the only people who benefit from a messy law are the patent attorneys. the consequences of some writings have also been difficult to overlook. it is desirable to harmonize this area, but we would rather do without the directive than contribute to bad legislation. it is therefore good that parliament wishes to reject the proposal. we believe that the commission should once again take up the issue of the community patent, which should apply to the whole of the eu. a major problem in this context is that the european patent office (epo) has issued a number of trivial patents and patents for pure software. however, the epo is not under the eu's jurisdiction but is governed by a larger specialised convention. if the eu is to take the lead in the matter of a legally secure and transparent patent system where patents are granted only for real inventions, we should introduce a community patent covering all areas, not just computer-related inventions.
in writing. many of the report's objectives on sustainable integration, social security and gender equality are worth pursuing. but these issues are best handled at lower decision-making levels, not at eu level. to control the eu's increasing centralisation and regulation, the union's tasks must be limited and its budget limited. against this background, we cannot support the proposal to allocate eur 336.2 billion to the european social fund from 2007 to 2013.
in writing. we moderates have today rejected the european parliament's proposal for a european fisheries fund. we believe that the european fisheries fund needs a proper restructuring, as the commission proposes. however, we do not think that fisheries policy needs more money.
in writing. we fully support equal treatment for women and men in the world of work. this is a key area which is being dealt with with merit by international organisations such as the ilo. we do not believe that the eu should regulate these kinds of issues in long-term reports, which have the character of political programmes. the eu should not control working time, parental leave and other important national issues. this is a matter for the member states to deal with independently, in accordance with the international agreements already adopted.
in writing. we are in principle in favour of the proposal to create uniform conflict-of-law rules for non-contractual obligations. the proposal would complement the brussels regulation on jurisdiction and the recognition and enforcement of judgments in civil and commercial matters and the rome convention on the law applicable to contractual obligations. this contributes to legal certainty and the proper functioning of the internal market. however, we believe it is important to ensure that the regulation does not conflict with freedom of the press and freedom of expression. sweden's constitutional protection of freedom of the press and of expression must remain unchanged. nor is it appropriate to apply foreign law in the assessment of the admissibility of trade union action taken under swedish law on swedish territory. in both these areas, it is also doubtful whether there is a legal basis in the ec treaty for such a regulation. we therefore support the amendments which apply the law of the country where the actions are carried out to trade union action and reject the amendments which involve unacceptable interference with national rules on freedom of the press and of expression.
in writing. we voted in favour of the report on the mandate for the conciliation procedure for the 2006 budget before the council's first reading. it provides a good basis for the continuation of the budgetary process. we do, however, believe that the structure of the eu budget must be more modern. this requires, among other things, a reform of the common agricultural policy, with a reduction in intervention prices on agricultural markets, a reduction in direct payments and a gradual transition to national co-financing. a reduction in the amounts for the international fisheries agreements is also justified. we also believe that cohesion policy should focus on the needs of the new member states.
in writing. we believe that the eu is a union of values and that turkey is part of its neighbourhood. that is why we also support the report which seeks to strengthen the role of women in turkey. we support turkey's accession to the eu, but we believe that neither turkey nor the eu are ready for unification today. turkey does not meet the human rights requirements for membership. the eu must also change. the rigid bureaucratic structure and costly agricultural policy must be fundamentally reformed before turkey can become a member.
in writing. the un has an important role to play in the reconstruction of iraq and in ensuring the establishment of democratic institutions. we support the parts of the report which stress the role of the un in building a democratic and stable iraq. at the same time, it is clear that the report seeks to strengthen the eu's foreign and security policy mandate. the strengthening of the eu in this area risks weakening the un. we therefore vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. we wholeheartedly support the fight against global poverty. we believe that the eu can make a valuable contribution to this urgent battle by pushing for a radical reform of the union's protectionist trade policy, by ending the eu's destructive fisheries agreements with third countries and by ending the payment of export refunds to european producers of products such as milk and sugar. however, the resolution contains extremely limited wording on this type of urgent reform. we believe that the eu should not be involved in aid matters, but that this type of important matter should be dealt with by the member states. international coordination of aid operations shall be carried out within the framework of organisations with long experience and broad legitimacy, such as the united nations and its bodies.
in writing. the junilist supports the swedish government's demand that cohesion aid be reduced from the proposed €336.1 billion to just under €200 billion in the 2007-13 budget plan. the reports should address the most appropriate way of reducing expenditure in order to approximate to the g6 requirements of a budget not exceeding 1.00% of member states' gni. the basis of all cohesion policy must be that aid should go to the poorest countries. today, far too much money is going to poor regions in relatively rich countries. the current system is not only unfair to the poorest countries, it can also appear cynical, because rich countries are not allowed to help less prosperous regions in the same way as they could if most of cohesion policy were internationalised.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the resolution we are voting on tomorrow is important, not only because it comes at a time when, ten years after the massacre, there are still people who do not believe that it happened, but also because neither the serbian parliament nor the parliament of bosnia-herzegovina has been able to agree on a similar resolution. the serb members of the bosnian parliament could not accept either the text of the resolution or the compromise proposals. someone has just today placed a bomb at the monument erected in srebrenica in memory of all the victims. as someone who was born in bosnia and who has experienced the war that we are talking about today, i feel a responsibility to convey to you at least a small part of all the emotions that we had to deal with on a daily basis during the war. the world was shocked by the video images of executions of young men shown in the hague recently. we lived in these pictures. so i want to describe to you what it's like to be on the other side. i will now read part of a defendant's story to the hague tribunal, written by a journalist and author who has followed the trial. "the defendant caught sight of a young boy getting off the bus. the boy could have been 15 years old or younger. the boy looked at the soldiers and then at the rows of dead bodies in the field. his eyes got bigger, like he was having trouble absorbing what he was seeing. as the prisoners knelt down, just before the soldiers were ordered to fire, the accused heard the boy's voice: 'mommy,' he whispered, 'mommy.' that day, the defendant heard people praying for their lives, grown men crying. he heard them promising the soldiers money, cars, even houses. but that boy called out to his mother, like children do when they've had a bad dream. "if we are to have any chance of moving forward after the balkan wars, we must first ensure that all those who have committed crimes are brought to justice, not only those who are indicted in the hague, but also those who are free and who should be brought to justice as soon as possible in national courts. we owe it to those who were killed, and to those who survived.
