the rapporteur. i am pleased to see that the commission has taken this step. we all know the serious background to the proposed directive on waste from the extractive industries. it is still only a few years since we saw gigantic accidents of collapsing mine dams in spain and romania. in the case of romania, the accident resulted in the poisoning of important river systems with cyanide. these accidents illustrate the enormous environmental risks and international repercussions of improper management of mining waste. to this must be added a series of minor accidents and a kind of ongoing environmental poisoning from old historical waste around our nature that has not been properly managed, and from which, among other things, heavy metals are released into the water systems. the european union today has no good rules to deal with these environmental problems. the waste regulations are not adapted to this type of waste. the proposed directive can remedy this. the cyanide method used in romania is prohibited by the proposed directive. we will make a very strict demand on the safety and sustainability of dams. we will require mining companies to be serious, to restore nature and to be able to guarantee economically that nature will be restored after mining. many of the amendments adopted by parliament at first reading have been taken up by the council of ministers in the common position, which is of course excellent. other important proposals have been rejected. it is therefore important that we adopt the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, which now mean that we are trying to restore what was adopted at first reading. i would like to give you some examples of such changes. we want not only an inventory of the old mine waste, but we also want something to be done about it, which in parliament's proposal means obligations. we also believe that the environmental problems arising from cavities after mining should be covered by the directive. the council's common position excludes large waste groups from essential parts of the directive, such as the financial guarantee. to avoid this, we must adopt amendment no 13. it is important to remember that even waste that is not chemically hazardous can cause very serious environmental problems. this also applies to inert waste. we also propose that the exceptions be tightened up. no long-term derogations can be granted, since waste can be put aside and a proper waste facility must be built. such long periods of derogation are now included in the council's position. these are some examples of amendments where we in parliament want a directive which is much more far-reaching, effective and progressive than the council's common position. voting against these amendments would mean lowering environmental ambitions. a strict and good directive in this area will not only benefit the environment, but it will also benefit the best, the most serious and the most modern and environmentally conscious mining companies. it will accelerate the improvement of the industry. at the same time, it will disadvantage those who do not take their environmental responsibility, which is good. it will benefit environmental engineering and research. it can make the eu a world leader in the field of mining environmental technology, which is also a positive thing and can mean that we can help other regions of the world. with the proposals put forward by the environment committee in my report, we have the opportunity to prevent future serious accidents, such as those in spain and romania. we can make sure we never see another accident like this. we have the ability to reduce the release of toxins into our water systems. we also have the opportunity to consistently address past environmental sins and improve water quality. let us seize these opportunities! last but not least, i'd like to thank my colleagues for a great collaboration. a number of colleagues from various political groups have tabled amendments which have improved this report. i would also like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and i would like to thank the commission for the very good cooperation so far.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. like the other eu institutions, this chamber is always ready to go into areas where the eu is not supposed to be involved. the eu is depriving the citizens of europe of their national self-determination in one area after another. the various debates this evening are revealing: no one in this house seems to be asking whether what we are discussing is a task for the eu. the eu's political legitimacy is therefore steadily declining. this is seen in opinion polls but rarely in democratic elections. the established party structure does not allow the citizen to express his opposition to the transfer of power to the eu. it is therefore only in referendums that this popular opposition finds expression in the democratic process. we have seen this in denmark, ireland and sweden, and more recently in france and the netherlands. i have a political mandate from my constituents to oppose this concentration of power in the eu, but not in all areas. there are cases where i and my colleagues welcome joint action at eu level. the galileo project is one such area. the question is to build a european infrastructure in a very important high-technology area. the individual countries cannot cope with this task. with galileo we are making europe independent of an american system which, in times of unrest, could be disrupted and shut down unilaterally by the united states for military reasons. i therefore welcome the galileo project and recommend that the house support the proposal for the implementation of the installation and operation phases. for once, the eu is on the right track.

in writing. i believe that it is of the utmost importance that the eu's agricultural policy, including the organisation of the raw tobacco market, be reformed. the current systems cost a lot of money and, in addition, the high level of agricultural subsidies today means that eu products are dumped on the world market, with serious consequences for developing countries. today the european parliament voted on an amendment to the regulation on the common organisation of the market in raw tobacco. i voted in favour of the amendment because it means that the reform of the raw tobacco market which has been started can continue. i do not think that the eu should support tobacco farming. i welcome the continuation of the reforms that have been agreed, which means that from 2010 onwards half of the total aid to the tobacco sector (eur 484 million) will be allocated to restructuring programmes in tobacco-producing regions, in the context of rural development.
in writing. we have voted against this report. we are strongly critical of the eu's common fisheries policy. however, it is important to be able to establish that eu vessels are complying with the rules in place and that this is done in a reliable manner. we therefore support the principle of electronic registration and reporting of fishing activities. however, we do not believe that the costs of computerizing ships should be borne by the eu. the financing is left to the discretion of the member states. the natural solution is to put the entire cost on the fishing industry.
in writing. we have chosen to support this report because the management of waste from the mining industry is partly a transnational environmental issue. however, we are critical of the use of structural fund funds for the inventory of closed waste facilities. there is normally no reason for taxpayers in other countries to pay for such measures. the structural funds should be used only in the case of poor member states with unusually high inventory needs. we have therefore voted against amendment no 8. the principle should be that the undertakings concerned should bear the costs incurred in implementing the proposed legislation.
in writing. the report on the eu programme for employment and social solidarity (progress) is indeed arbitrary, but it is based on the mistaken notion that the commission can and should develop overall strategies and proposals on how to solve these issues for 25 very different countries. instead of the eu regulating from above, member states should learn from each other through healthy institutional competition. no decision has been taken on the financial framework for the programme, as the eu's long-term budget has been rejected by the european council. the fact that we are still trying to implement the progress programme, which will cost more than a billion euros from 2007 to 2013, shows that we are not respecting the council's decision.
in writing. we voted in favour of the question of the future of the textile and clothing industry after 2005. we do not fully share the proposal, especially paragraph 11, which seeks an agreement covering more textile categories than those contained in the eu-china agreement of june. we believe that the quota system should be abolished in the long term and that the eu should open up trade with the rest of the world.
in writing. we moderates voted against the above report today. the abolition of quotas for trade in textiles and the entry of china into the wto have made the issue of high priority. we are opposed to both the protectionist measures introduced in the spring and those proposed in this report. the price of the kind of protectionism advocated in the report is paid by consumers through higher prices, by other sectors through more expensive inputs, and by entire regions through lost structural change and thus lost competitiveness. the consequences of this have become clear to more and more europeans over the summer. in northern europe, the textile trade was already liberalised in the 1970s and 1980s, which brought major benefits to several countries in southern europe. this was a painful process for many regions in northern europe but it has brought new competitiveness. it is regrettable that the countries which were the big winners of this deregulation are not themselves prepared to go through the same process. we have known for ten years that in 2005 we would have a new world trade regime for textiles, without quotas. the textile industry has had ample opportunity to prepare. a few more years of trade barriers will not help the european textile industry.
in writing. the saïfi report is aimed at countering the increase in imports of textiles from china into europe. this has increased significantly since the abolition of quotas on 1 january 2005 when the world trade agreement on textiles expired. eu leaders should have anticipated the situation and adapted in time, but protectionist flaws have long clouded the eu's trade policy vision. increased protectionism cannot in the long run protect european industry and jobs from increasingly fierce international competition. the best way to meet this competition is to invest in education, research and innovation and to make it easier for companies to act on the global market. however, after the incompetent management of textile quotas so far, there may be a need to draw up a clear plan for the transition to free trade in textile products in order to alleviate the pressure on the employees of the textile industry in southern europe. the junilist supports in general terms the further liberalisation of world trade and cannot therefore vote for the report as a whole. we cannot support the proposal for a 'ethics tax' on international companies either, because we are opposed to giving the eu the right to tax.
in writing. we voted in favour of the report on the application of articles 4 and 5 of the 'television without frontiers' directive. it is important that we show our support for the possibilities for the public service to develop. public services are of great importance for democracy and diversity and it is important that the eu safeguards this. we are also concerned about the increasing concentration of media in the eu. in principle, however, we are against rules which determine the proportion of broadcasting time to be made up of european productions.
in writing. we consider that the broadcasting quotas for european productions and programmes by independent broadcasters is something very questionable. in particular, these quotas should not be decided at union level. we want to promote products that highlight cultural distinctiveness, but we doubt that broadcasting quotas are an appropriate means to this end. we have voted against this report for the reasons stated above.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i was a member of the temporary committee on political challenges and budgetary means and i argued that we should stick to 1% of gdp. in view of this, i do not think we should decide to use the surplus from the 2004 budget for the expenditure in the 2005 budget. i believe that the structural funds, which are already growing in size through this expenditure, do not need to be increased. it is already more than eight billion. i therefore do not agree with my complaint that no money is being spent. there are of course reasons why money is not being spent, and so there is no harm in examining matters very carefully when it comes to new expenditure. i am not prepared to return these surpluses to the member states, but i believe that these funds should be used for a margin of flexibility which, in my view, is too small. otherwise we will often find ourselves in situations where we have to admit that we are not able to take the necessary action. we can't predict the future. i therefore believe that these funds should be used to expand the flexibility margin and not to build up the structural funds.
the committee on development and cooperation has been asked to submit an opinion. president. - the debate is closed. this question has been discussed for quite some time. it is, of course, good that we have now reached a decision and an agreement. at the same time, on behalf of the committee on development, i am disappointed with the result. we have consistently opposed the fact that funds already earmarked for genuine development purposes in the asian budget are now partly reprogrammed for this disaster. we have also always maintained that we should protect the emergency reserve as much as possible and use the flexibility instrument instead. we now have only 53 million euro left in the emergency reserve, with just over four months left in the year. what if there's another big disaster in the world? our arguments have not been successful. that doesn't mean our arguments lack relevance or weight, quite the opposite. we will support this proposal but will continue to work towards providing new resources and the flexibility instrument for major disaster response such as this. we also hope to safeguard the emergency reserve as much as possible so that we can respond in the event of major emergencies. finally, i hope and believe that the funds now allocated for reconstruction and rehabilitation are being used in the wisest possible way and that, above all, as much as possible, disaster preparedness aspects are being incorporated into this, so that we do not reinforce a vulnerability which already exists in the people living in this region.
vice-president of the commission. madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. the commission will convey the facts on eu issues and give citizens the opportunity to make their own judgments and choices not spread political propaganda. informing citizens is part of our public service mission. it's about transparency and accountability. this is an area where the commission has had a long and fruitful cooperation with the european parliament. on 20 july, the commission adopted an action plan for better communication. it is intended to show in a clear and pragmatic way how we can improve the dialogue with citizens throughout the eu. it is also a testament to our commitment to more dialogue, consultation and debate on the role of the union. our actions are based on three principles: listening better, describing how eu policies affect the daily lives of citizens and making the message relevant at local level. the first principle, in particular, of listening, is an expression of our readiness and willingness to pursue further democratization. it is about more actively asking for citizens' views and taking them into account from the outset when new policies and legislation are being drawn up. nils lundgren asks me whether i think the eu institutions have already been given too much power at the expense of the member states, and would be given even more under the proposal for a constitution for europe. my answer is no! first, any decision to confer powers on the eu institutions is an independent decision of the member states. moreover, the aim is not to weaken the role of national governments but to increase their ability to achieve objectives of common interest.
it sounds very nice when commissioner wallström says that we should listen, and when she emphasizes that democracy is about will and that the opinions of the people are conveyed to those who have the power to govern. but the whole debate we are hearing in this house, not just today but also in the future, shows that the opposite is true. we have to remember what happened. we have put forward a proposal for the creation of a constitution for europe. this has been the subject of two referendums in the constituent countries where the people said no. it is not a problem that can be solved by more propaganda offices, more money for information, etc., is it? should we not respect this as a decision from below, i.e. that the constitution is ignored?
i welcome the initiative on the plan for debate, dialogue and democracy. it is obvious that the various institutions must provide information on their activities. it is clear that we politicians must go home and inform about what is happening here in parliament and have a dialogue about the decisions we take. in addition to the local perspective, which i think is very important, i would like to mention one aspect which i have not heard mentioned here today, namely that the policy at eu level must be integrated not only into national policy but also into local and regional policy. environmental and labour market issues, for example, are of course topical at all levels. are you working to integrate policies at different levels so that citizens can recognise more and see the added value of the european dimension?
i would like to thank commissioner frattini for this welcome decision. i come from sweden, which is one of the countries that has been very active in this area. in the general debate on refugees that we have had so far, we have not talked about this as an important solution. i therefore look forward to reading the proposals that will come and thank you again for your reply.
mr president, i would like to thank you. children should have safe medicines tailored to their needs. it is a self-evident legal requirement. however, with a six-month extension of the patent, we are paying an unnecessarily high price for the pharmaceutical companies to meet the obvious demands. the pharmaceutical companies already have years of protection and 100 billion euros in their cash registers for acquisitions. they can withstand a one percent increase in costs much better than already impoverished public budgets. we're not going to transfer any more money to the companies. and then it will end up that we finally have safe medicines for both children and adults, but no one can afford to buy them. we in the green group have tabled a compromise amendment for a maximum of four months with the additional restrictions described by mrs breyer. if that is not acceptable, parliament is showing that it listens more to the lobbyists of the pharmaceutical companies than to the needs of children.
mr president, i would like to thank you. a perfectly safe society is not a police state. it's a suspicious and insecure society that no one wants to live in. the continuing increase in police powers will never stop all terror and organized crime but will only erode democracy. the best weapon is reducing recruitment through tolerance, social cohesion, reducing poverty and allowing legal immigration. when people do not feel oppressed or excluded, they are also less likely to be recruited into crime syndicates or terrorist groups. biometric passports are not as effective as you, charles clarke, claim. have you read the audit report from the us court of auditors? not all suspects are identified. innocent miners are at risk of being stopped because of bad fingerprints. the cost is enormous. for the eu alone, biometric passports would cost around eur 10 billion. it's not worth the money.
mr president, mr president of the council, mr commissioner! the terrorist attacks in london this summer have once again demonstrated the vulnerability of our open democracies. once again, innocent people are being killed on their way to work. how do we protect ourselves when our own citizens, respected neighbors and beloved family men are suddenly turned into ruthless killing machines? more than ever, cooperation is needed to help prevent, detect, detect and arrest those planning terrorist acts or other cross-border crime. the criminals are far ahead of both the police and politicians in terms of cross-border cooperation. we need to be much better at coping with the same degree of cooperation. we talk a lot, but as my colleagues have already pointed out, very little has happened. our counter-terrorism coordinator gijs de vries has repeatedly pointed out the problem that too much is said and too little is done. in swedish, you say "lots of talk, but a little hockey". the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. europol and eurojust must be given much more resources and perhaps develop into a kind of european fbi that can coordinate, lead reconnaissance operations, preliminary investigations and perhaps even raids. information and intelligence from the various member states could be coordinated. the fight against crime is a vital balance at all times between the need for security and tough measures and the integrity of the individual. the developments of recent years have forced all of us to make some compromises on our privacy. for example, cameras in public places are increasingly accepted. they also played a vital role in the work of the london police in identifying the terrorist bombings. every action must be carefully weighed at all times. the argument for building large systems to store data traffic, phone calls, e-mails and text messages has so far not convinced me. it's a very large invasion of privacy with many risks of abuse. after all, most of us are not criminals.
in writing. past experience shows that the eu is not capable of managing disaster aid effectively. instead, each member state should set up, for example, a solidarity fund to help countries affected by natural disasters. coordination should be adapted to the specific circumstances of each disaster and take into account the priorities of the affected countries. the junilista therefore chooses to vote against the reports, despite the fact that the objectives in these cases are both justified and heartwarming.
in writing. the report concerns protection at workplaces against exposure to optical radiation. we support minimum health and safety protection at work, but we believe that this can be dealt with by the member states themselves. we support the amendments which reduce bureaucracy for companies.
in writing. we have voted against the de sarnez report today because we believe that it conflicts with the swedish legislation on freedom of the press and expression and its clear rules on the independence of the media. the report contains proposals on how best to protect minors from harmful and inappropriate content on the internet, which we do not object to but consider to be an urgent matter. however, the report also recommends that member states introduce the right of reply in all audiovisual and online information services and introduce rules on media ownership. for these reasons, we have voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. ensuring that minors do not have access to programmes or services for adults in audiovisual services is of course a very important issue. but we believe that the development of legislative measures at eu level to protect the physical, mental and moral development of minors in connection with these services is the wrong way to go. there will always be web hosts around the world outside the eu. as noted, much of this is based on good parental controls and there is now the possibility for parents to install filters on their computers. there are positive aspects to the draft report, but it goes too far. this includes the proposal to set up a secondary-level domain name such as .kid. we believe that it is up to the democratic institutions in each member state to solve these daunting problems. the eu should provide a framework of common standards in this area to which the member states can subscribe, analogous to the schengen agreement. we have voted definitively against all amendments which refer to the treaty establishing a constitution for europe. this treaty has fallen.
in writing. this report goes against a very important principle of european cooperation, namely the principle of the country of origin. the commission is proposing the creation of an eu paediatric authority to centrally assess, approve and monitor paediatric medicines and stimulate production. but there is no good reason to centralize this activity in a new eu authority. the report is also based on the notion that the pharmaceutical industry does not consider it profitable to develop specific medicines for children. this is hardly convincing. where there's demand, there's supply. if there is one thing that companies in a market economy are good at, it is identifying niches and developing products for them. if it is true that there is a great need for specific paediatric medicines, the solution should be sought on the demand side through the authorities of the individual countries. the whole report is steeped in a desire to extend the eu's influence and build up new bureaucracies. we have therefore voted against the report.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. ever since i was a kid, the middle east has dominated the news. sadly, it has been mostly about misery, violence, war, oppression, and stock conservative rulers. nowhere in the world is the concentration of untouchability as high as in the middle east. half a billion people live there in dictatorships with the only exception being the democracy of israel. this is of course a problem for the people who live there. they have the right to democracy and human rights just like us. it's also a global problem. if you see no future, frustration, fanaticism and fundamentalism will flourish. there are signs, of course, that the situation is changing. an example of this is the presidential election in egypt today. it's a step forward even though mubarak will of course win. at least there were more candidates. the developments in lebanon, with the expulsion of the syrian occupying forces, are also very positive. the decision to allow women in kuwait to vote next year is also an important step forward. as has already been mentioned here, the evacuation of gaza, which went so smoothly after all, is a very important step for the continuation of the peace process and the establishment of two states. the forthcoming palestinian elections will be an important test of the progress of the reforms in the palestinian government and how the process can proceed. in iraq there is a government, there is a draft constitution, there are strong forces who want democracy and stability, but of course the situation is extremely painful with the daily violence in which the people are forced to live. however, there are still a great many problems in the middle east. the presidency mentioned iran and the mullahs in iran who have been playing games with the world community for several months now with their nuclear weapons programme. this is a matter for the united nations to deal with. we cannot continue to "pretend to negotiate" with iran in this way. the oppression of its own people continues on an unabated scale, which is completely unacceptable. the oppression of the people of the region is unacceptable. the eu should therefore join the united states, international organisations, foundations, the opposition in these countries and others in taking a joint initiative for an international plan for peace and democracy in the middle east as a whole. the resources of the barcelona process should be used much more focused on democracy itself. nothing would be better for peace, stability and the fight against terrorism throughout the world than a victory for democracy in the middle east. in this area we have a great responsibility.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is not the small democracy of israel that is the great threat to peace in the middle east, but the great dictatorial regimes. we are witnessing today a hopeful development in the form of a withdrawal from gaza and small signs of democracy in the arab world. this makes the requirements of european middle east policy much clearer. iran is a country whose stated political objective is to destroy one of the member states of the united nations, israel. iran is breaking the non-proliferation treaty and developing intermediate-range missiles with the aim of reaching targets in the countries we are talking about. the country expresses the same anti-semitism that too many regimes and groups in this part of the world express. it cannot be acceptable to provide support for terrorism as iran does. if the european union is to be a force in the peace process, it must now support the forces of democracy and peace that are at work. the eu must react against those who do not accept international agreements and who deliberately choose the path of violence. we must create a framework and a support for democratic development throughout the middle east. there is no better basis for peaceful and stable development in this part of the world than a breakthrough for democracy. we see the many different signs of democratic development. the eu must then vigorously support those who keep the lamp of democracy and freedom burning. we must also ensure that the support we give to the palestinian authority and to other countries is accompanied by clear demands for democratic development. we have the opportunity to play a role in peace, but then we must also show that we can strengthen democracy.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking you for the opportunity to make this important report on gender discrimination in health care. i would like to thank all the colleagues who have shown great interest and commitment and have made valuable contributions to the report. the sex we happen to be born into is as important a factor in health as social, economic, and ethnic background. the un conferences in cairo in 1994 and beijing in 1995 put gender gaps in health care on the agenda. unfortunately, little action has been taken since then, either in the member states or at eu level. women's health is still often seen as synonymous with sexual and reproductive health. of course, women's health is more complex than that. i would like to mention a few examples of gender discrimination. women still have to wait longer than men in the ambulance for a heart attack. still, the mortality rate is higher among women than men for the same type of heart disease. still, more men than women with visual impairments are given the opportunity to undergo cataract surgery. violence against women, a major public health problem, is still a phenomenon almost invisible in medical education and practice. men continue to be the norm in the research and development of new medicines. this report focuses in particular on two different areas. the first area is to propose measures to prevent gender discrimination between men and women suffering from the same or similar diseases. the second focus area is women's health from the perspective of so-called women's diseases, i.e. diseases that almost exclusively affect women, such as breast cancer, osteoporosis, eating disorders in young girls and violence against women in all its various forms physical and psychological abuse, genital mutilation, sex trafficking, prostitution, etc. gender discrimination becomes very clear when one looks at the few research reports that exist. for example, an analysis of a skin clinic in sweden shows that men and women are treated very differently: the men get the more expensive light treatment while the women are sent home with a prescription for skin ointment. the cost conclusion is that if women were treated equally to men, the resources spent on women would increase by 61 percent. on the other hand, if women's treatment intensity were the norm, the cost of treatment would be reduced by 33 percent. i can also mention that all the doctors who differentiated between women and men when prescribing in the example i mention were women. it shows that gender differences permeate the thinking of both men and women. to combat gender discrimination, researchers and others need access to gender-disaggregated statistics in healthcare. without data and without knowledge, we cannot prove what gender discrimination really is. without such knowledge, we cannot solve the problems either. it is high time that action was taken to ensure that we can speak of fair and equal care. gender differences in health care are just as important a determining factor as ethnic background, social and economic factors. we must take this into account in future work to ensure fair and equal treatment. i would also like to stress that access to sexual and reproductive health counselling and family planning are important instruments for women's health, as well as for women's opportunities to participate on equal terms in society and to combat poverty and the ongoing feminization of poverty.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. although i am, as some - if not many - call the "weaker sex" and hundreds of miles away from my children tonight, i would like to join in this important debate. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, my swedish colleague eva-britt svensson, for bringing up many important issues which are worth highlighting in this context. i am glad that the pse group has supported my amendments on women and smoking, which are now in paragraph 34, and i hope, of course, that i will have support for them tomorrow. sweden became the first country in the world where the proportion of women smokers exceeded that of men, something of which i am not particularly proud. women are more vulnerable to the damage caused by smoking than men. one explanation is that women generally have smaller body sizes, smaller hearts, and narrower coronary vessels than men. studies also show that it is mainly women with low education in low-paid occupations, such as health and care, who smoke the most. today, a major new study by the european society of cardiology (esc) was presented, which shows that women with symptoms of cardiovascular disease generally receive poorer care than men. this is despite the fact that women's chances of surviving a heart attack are lower than men's. discrimination of any kind, in this case gender discrimination in the health care system, is an international issue. that is why we in the eu should take a clear line and also take advantage of each other's experience.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. unfortunately, there is a constant drive to enter virtually all areas. we shall be voting on many such rejection proposals later today. as regards the northern dimension, i think it is therefore very important that we concentrate on results-oriented activities and not on everything between heaven and earth that is north of the 15th parallel. so what is important in the northern dimension for the next ten, fifteen years? i agree with almost all the speakers before me that the answer is the baltic sea. i would also add that the nuclear power stations outside st petersburg are among the most pressing issues of the day. in both cases, it should be noted that russia is absolutely central. it is therefore important to nurture and develop our relations with russia in both these areas. the baltic sea is a completely unique inland sea. there is no analogy with the mediterranean or any other sea in the world. the baltic sea is brackish water, which means that flora and fauna that are adapted to salt water or fresh water are constantly in danger. the salt content rises or falls depending on how the water flows through the sound. this makes the baltic sea extremely sensitive, as several speakers have pointed out, because it is a shallow sea. the baltic sea cannot tolerate pollution, but around the baltic sea there is now intensive economic activity and intensive agriculture with a lot of emissions. this means that we should work on things like saving flora and fauna, clean up emissions, controlling shipping and shutting down the nuclear power stations at st petersburg.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the resounding 'no' of the french and dutch on the eu constitution made it clear that the broad layers of citizens we represent want a narrower rather than a wider eu cooperation. the report proposes, inter alia, the creation of an independent directorate-general for tourism within the commission. this means more power for the eu, which junilista is against. it is true that a well-organized tourism sector has a good potential to contribute to the betterment of people in many developing countries. the eu should not, however, pursue a common tourism policy, as the report suggests. we believe that the eu should only deal with genuinely crossborder issues, where the eu brings a unique added value to existing international organisations. the eu should not therefore be involved in development policy and tourism policy. paragraph 10 requires developing countries to introduce a non-restrictive visa policy. this is a completely unreasonable proposal. the developing countries must of course decide for themselves what visa policy they wish to pursue. otherwise, developing countries should also be able to demand that the eu introduce a non-restrictive visa policy. paragraph 24 calls for the fight against crime in developing countries to be conducted in a way that does not damage the reputation of these countries as tourist destinations. should the eu dictate to other countries how to fight crime? paragraph 25 requires that tourism be promoted, protected and safeguarded through initiatives aimed at combating crime against tourism, including the establishment of specialised police units. i repeat: this is not the eu's job. we are critical of the fact that paragraph 30 refers to the eu constitution which has already been rejected by the french and dutch people. we are in favour of a european union with limited influence and will therefore vote against this report.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted for the resolution on natural disasters because we can support the general line. we share the view that the disasters are largely due to climate change, which brings extreme weather conditions. to address climate change, global work on the kyoto protocol must continue and be intensified. we also believe that the eu member states can be helped collectively to cope with the acute crises that arise in the wake of natural disasters. however, we think that some of the proposals in the resolution are too detailed and too far-reaching. we are not in favour of agricultural aid being used to build fire-damaged roads and we do not want an eu-funded agricultural insurance system.
in writing. we welcome international solidarity when a country is hit by natural disasters or other events with devastating consequences. however, we do not believe that the eu should use these types of events to increase its influence in different policy areas. the commission shall not carry out detailed analyses of the causes of forest fires. the eu will not develop a common policy on fighting forest fires. no eu bodies responsible for monitoring various climate factors will be established. it is regrettable that the european parliament is trying to take every opportunity to advance the eu's positions.
in writing. tourism is a very important industry for a number of eu countries, but it is not part of the community's policy area. the report nevertheless calls on the commission to take a number of measures in the field of tourism. the report also proposes to create a specific heading for tourism-related measures in the eu budget. we believe that tourism is an example of something that the member states should manage themselves and not an area in which the eu should intervene. that is why we have voted against the report.
in writing. the report also calls for the eu to take action to ensure that the eu's citizens are able to access the internet. as it is not a community area, we also oppose the various steps and tendencies in the report to go in that direction, and therefore we see no reason for the commission to draw up a tourism package which would include a review of existing directives on consumer rights in tourism. these actions should be developed in the member states with stakeholders.
in writing. we shall vote against this report. this is not a matter for the eu to deal with. the report states in point e that tourism is not a community policy area today but, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, falls within the competence of the member states. that is a perfectly correct assessment. nevertheless, the report contains a series of proposals for a tourism policy at eu level: - tourism services are classified at eu level; - a specific training network for tourism-related competence bodies is set up; - bodies representing tourists as consumers are set up; - a contact group is set up with the participation of the member states and tourism companies; - a network is set up for the exchange of good practice between organisations involved in risky sports and leisure activities. we oppose all such proposals. despite the failure of the draft eu constitution, it is cited as the basis for the eu's position on tourism. it is proposed that a specific heading for tourism be created in the eu budget. the june summit made it clear that the member states are not willing to pay for all the new policies. the report's rationale, that tourism contributes to growth and employment and is therefore central to the lisbon process, can be applied to virtually all areas. we believe that the principle of subsidiarity is superior to such thinking.
in writing. the moderates have voted against the own-initiative report on gender discrimination in the health care system. gender equality, health and social care, like many other issues where there are different solutions and traditions in the different member states, should be dealt with at national level. the eu should only deal with matters where individual member states cannot do better on their own. the report above addresses many important aspects of women's health and access to care. unlike the rapporteur, eva-britt svensson, we believe that many of these aspects can best be addressed by the individual member state, the individual hospital or the individual. one way of developing and improving health care, without, for example, the eu interfering in national health strategies or the commission being required to investigate certain trends in health care, is through best practice and the exchange of information between member states, hospitals, research institutes and other interested parties, which we warmly welcome.
in writing. the own-initiative report on gender discrimination in healthcare systems addresses many pressing issues concerning women's health. not least because the physiognomy of the male is the starting point of modern medicine, even though there are obviously significant biological differences between the sexes. this is an abuse which my party, the liberal people's party, has denounced on several occasions. despite the report's good intentions to promote women's health in the union, i am voting against it. i choose to do so because i believe that the report is in too many respects contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. many of the proposals presented infringe the sovereignty of the member states and also infringe the right of individuals to make their own decisions affecting their own lives. it is not the job of the european parliament to decide whether women should have the right to a caesarean section or whether healthcare workers should have shorter working hours and higher salaries. at the same time, the report is too broad in its wording in other areas, and i am referring in particular to the sections on ovarian overstimulation and the removal of eggs.
in writing. the european schools system is beginning to take on a life of its own with the debate that 'the european school system promotes the idea of european citizenship' (quoted in paragraph c of the report). we can understand the need for european schools to provide education for the children of eu officials in their mother tongue. but the system has become strange when, for example, only 1.6% of the pupils at the european school in culham are children of staff of the eu institutions and bodies. with the enlargement of the eu, the number of official languages has increased to such an extent that the whole situation of the european schools has to be reviewed. the deployment of decentralised eu bodies would now also mean that european schools would have to be set up in all the member states. we believe that the education of the children of eu officials must be solved more flexibly. the eu institutions and bodies are simply allowed to pay the tuition fees (up to a certain level) for the children of their staff as employers. if parents then choose to place their children in a state, municipal or private free school, they can solve it locally. as a rule, there is a choice between a local or an international school in the places where the eu institutions and bodies are located. the european schools system seems to have outlived itself. we have therefore voted against this report.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank john bowis for a very good job, particularly for going beyond the well-known diseases such as aids, malaria and tuberculosis to tackle sleeping sickness, leishmaniasis and chagas disease. they're forgotten diseases here in the west, but they're making a brutal comeback in the south with millions of victims every year. the tsunami brought the suffering of the people to light, and aid flowed in. if we can bring these diseases to the forefront and mobilize the same resources, we can reduce the suffering of millions of people. yesterday we voted on the need for paediatric medicine, and unfortunately parliament gave extra patent extensions worth many billions to the companies as compensation. we must then be able to reasonably find the almost three billion euros needed to find treatments for these diseases affecting children and adults in the developing world. tb and aids are here in the western world, and so are treatments. however, they mainly affect adults, and we have no treatments adapted to children. public funds must be invested in this area. the children of the developing world have no means of financing their own medical needs. for example, the money could come from a 1% profit tax on pharmaceutical companies that are paid when they find a cure. there are many other solutions. it is our collective responsibility to create resources immediately to combat these diseases. the progress of a civilization can be judged by how it treats its most vulnerable members. the most vulnerable group on earth are these poor and sick children. if we can't even help them, humanity is gone, and we should move back to the caves.
in writing. the moderate delegation voted today in favour of a report on serious and neglected diseases in the developing countries. we believe that disease and ill health are a dominant problem in these countries, both a cause and a result of underdevelopment. however, we doubt that the eu will comment on the pricing of various products by the pharmaceutical industry. we also hesitate to force the pharmaceutical industry to reinvest part of its profits in r & d for neglected diseases. we would rather encourage the pharmaceutical industry to decide, on ethical and moral grounds, to reinvest part of its profits in research into diseases affecting poor countries. this is in line with taking responsibility for global development. further attention should be paid to african governments that impose a sales or import tax on antiretroviral and other drugs. this results in medicines becoming unaffordable for many poor groups. it must forcefully call on the governments concerned to abolish such taxes.
in writing. the global fight against infectious diseases is an urgent issue that requires a comprehensive international commitment. junilista believes that international cooperation should be carried out within already established organisations with expertise and experience in the field, such as the world health organisation (who). the junilista is in favour of limited eu cooperation. we are critical of the union's pursuit of influence and competence in matters already dealt with by existing international organisations.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the autumn of 1980, when i was 15 years old, i went to poland for the first time in my life. we were a group of swedish youths who were cooperating with the polish independent student movement. it was a youth movement that was, in practice, part of solidarity and the enormous positive mobilization that solidarity meant in polish society at the time. for me, it was a journey of upheaval. the freedoms and rights that were a given to me as a swedish teenager were something that my polish peers had to fight hard for. poland was a society of scarce goods, clear political oppression and a hard life for most poles. at that time, solidarity stood for the hope of a better future with its demands for freedom, democracy and the right to national self-determination. solidarity was a progressive movement, not only with its demands for the democratization of polish society: as a trade union it worked for higher wages, better pension conditions, influence for workers in the workplace, better health care and childcare. it was a truly progressive movement that was suppressed by the military takeover, which was supported by the soviet union. but despite the military rule, it would not be possible to stop the demands and the hopes that solidarity symbolized. in fact, demands for free trade unions and spontaneous workers' organization have on a number of occasions been decisive in the opposition to dictatorship in the countries dominated by the soviet union after the war. solidarity and opposition in poland was a perhaps the most important but one in the series of revolts against oppression. east berlin in 1953, budapest in 1956, pozna in the same year, prague in 1968, where the democratic opposition was violently suppressed. most of the time, workers, with their organization and their demands for justice and democracy, were at the forefront of the protests. today poland is a political democracy among other political democracies. there has been tremendous progress in key areas since the fall of the wall. an important part of the reason for that, perhaps the most decisive, is the work of solidarity against the old regime. this development has led to a better future, not only for poland, but for the whole of europe. at the same time, much remains to be done in terms of the social demands made by solidarity in terms of influence over the working people and a better standard of living. poland is today a society where many people live on the dole and where unemployment is very high. at the same time, trade union organisation in the country is very low and employees are often repressed. to do something about it, we need a strong, well-organized trade union movement.
mr president, i would like to thank you. today we commemorate the 25th anniversary of polish solidarity and discuss its message to europe. we remember the days and months when the polish people had had enough, the days and months when polish workers went on strike in gdansk. the struggle that started so much more than just the struggle for the freedom of poland, it started the struggle for the freedom of all of eastern europe. the first stone of the berlin wall fell not in berlin but in gdansk. but we often forget that the strike and the unity in gdansk were preceded by years of polish revolt against communism. many fought for freedom in different ways and separately, but there was no unified force that united all poles around the same idea. only after a polish pope was installed in the vatican did the poles realize that their spiritual heritage united them and that there were forces that would carry them to independence and self-government, and so it did. i myself lived in communist yugoslavia at the time and remember the images from gdansk vividly. i could not believe that anyone actually believed that communism could be brought down. when the people of serbia protested against milosevic's regime almost two decades later, they had learned some of solidarno's lessons. they had learned that totalitarian regimes are not permanent but can actually fall. they had also learned that any democracy must come from within, and that it is a united people fighting for their freedom that must win. solidarno had already sent the most important message to europe. my colleague sjöstedt from sweden, who belongs to a party whose leader still calls himself a communist, has just given a speech in which he praised solidarno, a movement that fought against communism. this is also the message that solidarno sent to europe. the message that europe will not tolerate communism or any other dictatorship or totalitarian or enslaved system. the future of europe lies in freedom, and we all defend freedom, today in a very special way, by expressing our respect and gratitude to the people of poland and eastern europe for the struggle for a free europe that they fought in the 1980s and 1990s.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the capital subscription directive brings to the fore a recurring conflict of objectives in eu cooperation. a conflict of objectives which we all have reason to analyse and debate in principle before taking a position. on the one hand, there are often good reasons for introducing common rules at eu level in order to ensure the effective functioning of the internal market. different rules between member states in key areas lead to high costs and thus lower prosperity. this also applies to the financial market. on the other hand, there are strong reasons not to impose common rules on the member states which then become part of our acquis communautaire and thus prevent individual countries from taking the lead and developing the regulatory systems. much of the dynamism of our economies rests on the institutional competition that we have between countries in developing effective and growth-enhancing institutions. the terms best practice and benchmarking are often used to reflect insights into this important process. unfortunately, in this house, little or no attention is paid to institutional competition. however, in the case of basel ii, we are talking about a highly global market with an extremely well-informed group of actors who can act on the fly. there is little room for special positions at eu level in this market. as regards basel ii, individual countries should act independently within the framework of this central bank cooperation. there is really no need to involve the eu and this parliament in that process. we're not cut out for it. finally, let me agree with other speakers that it is unfortunate that parliament is trying to use this report in the constant institutional power struggle between the eu institutions. the junilista does not want to contribute to strengthening the power of this parliament.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to thank alexander radwan and also congratulate him on the work he has done, which is large, complex and important. if there is any area where we see the importance of european cooperation, it is in this area where we are creating a large financial market. i would like to say to my swedish colleague who spoke earlier that without european cooperation we would not be able to have democratic and parliamentary control over the regulatory framework that is being drawn up here. we are now getting stability and efficiency and predictability in the common financial markets. the directive will also introduce more flexibility in risk assessment, which is basically - and it is important to remember - good for consumers and for businesses in europe. the important thing, and as mentioned here earlier, is that it also means a level playing field in the global market, as opposed to the american market. but it is also important that it means a level playing field in another sense, that is, that in the dynamic financial markets we will see the emergence of new financial institutions. just as we have seen a convergence between banks and insurance companies in recent decades, we will see new products and new structures. it is therefore important that this directive does not in practice impede the dynamic development of the market, but rather allows new forms of enterprise to emerge and compete on equal terms. it will be an important task for the commission, as well as for the debate in this parliament, to follow developments in this particular respect. i am grateful that we have been able to get transitional rules which allow the national authorities to reflect this development, but once the directive is in place and its rules apply it will be the task of this parliament and the commission to ensure that there is an openness to change which reflects the volatility of the financial markets. otherwise we will lose our competitiveness in europe. that is why this is an important task.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the comoros is far away and what we do there is done in silence, it doesn't reach the debate here at home. but if the television images from there reached our living rooms, parliament would never have approved this agreement. we buy the fishing rights from the government, but the people we really take the fish from, the fishermen in the villages, they have no influence and they rarely get adequate compensation. neither national parliaments nor the european parliament have any significant influence, but are faced with a fait accompli. it's not until ten months after the deal starts that we get a chance to speak. this parliament has called for amendment many times. it is time to take back democratic control of these agreements, which are more a relic of old-fashioned colonialism than modern trade and democracy. i therefore welcome the proposal for more information. i thank the commission for the increased co-financing from those who use the agreement so that the burden on our taxpayers is reduced, but i regret that the agreement is being extended to several tonnes per year. i believe that the small-scale fishing communities of the partner country must have veto rights over the agreements. my group will vote against this agreement which favours eu fishermen at the expense of poorer colleagues in the south.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the eu's fisheries agreements with third countries have been widely criticised by several organisations working on development issues, including swedish sida. the agreements have certainly been reformed, but criticism of the current partnership agreements is highly justified. the rapporteur also stressed the need to ensure that the eu's fisheries policy was consistent with the principles of the treaty of rome and the treaty of rome. in other words, it is the narrow interests of the eu that must be protected, not the interests of the poor comorian fishermen. the eu is using its power to secure short-term economic interests. the tuna fisheries agreement with the comoros is not very extensive, but the question of fisheries agreements is of fundamental importance. it would be natural for the costs of fisheries agreements to be borne entirely by the parties who choose to use them. why should europe's taxpayers pay for fisheries agreements that benefit the fleets of a few eu countries? the rapporteur believes that ship owners are paying too much for these contracts. the junilists believe that this should not be an eu issue and that the agreements should not be funded by eu funds at all. the british presidency and the commission have put forward the priority of development issues in africa as a policy area. however, the will to seriously reform the eu's counterproductive agricultural, trade and fisheries policies is limited. the ambition seems to be to increase aid without addressing the structural problems that make it difficult for developing countries to escape poverty.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs kreissl-dörfler, for a very good report. it is important that we all work towards the same goal: that the eu in the long term should pursue a policy of asylum and refugees 'equal for everyone everywhere'. safe or very safe country rules create a worrying and unclear legal picture and provide different treatment in different countries for people fleeing. sweden, where i come from, does not apply general rules or the concept of safe countries of origin or safe third countries, but we see all refugees as individuals and give each one an individual assessment. then a member state can assess the individual refugee's history and need to have his or her reasons considered. a country may be safe for a group collectively, but it may be impossible for an individual or a family to return to it, precisely because that person may have been engaged in political union work or belongs to an ngo that is engaged in activities that are not to their liking. i am of course also concerned about the increasingly restrictive approach to the age of consent in both the international and european context. you're not an adult when you're 16, in these cases or any other. i hope that this report will help us to move forward in the process which will eventually lead to a common policy on asylum and refugees in the eu. that is what the citizens of the european union expect us to do, because they want to see the european union as a unit.
in writing. the financial impact of the proposal to increase the fees payable to the european medicines agency (emea) has not yet been determined. in any event, the proposal will have implications for the budget of heading 3 and 2 of the new financial perspective for 2007-13. we are in principle opposed to the emea being strengthened as an authority and given an increased budget. that is why we have voted against this report.
in writing. the eu's fisheries policy should aim to ensure the abundance of fish stocks, quality food products for consumers and good conditions for the european fishing industry. unfortunately, the eu's fisheries policy has become a permanent policy of subsidies for a non-profitable fishing industry which cannot survive on its own without support, without the support policy safeguarding the fish stocks. it prevents structural rationalisation but not exhaustion. at the same time, the icelandic example of developed fisheries ownership shows that there are other methods that both protect fish stocks and help adapt the industry to new conditions. fisheries policy does not have to be aid policy. the eu's fisheries policy should seek new ways of protecting fish stocks from overfishing and restructuring the fishing industry.
in writing. we think that the council's initiative to store traffic data to give law enforcement authorities better tools in the fight against serious crime and terrorism is a good one. the experience of the swedish side shows that in many serious crime investigations traffic data are important. that is why we support the council's proposal in principle. the proposal presented by the commission on 21 september 2005 on the storage of traffic data opens up a debate on law enforcement. the commission's proposal gives the european parliament a say. that is why we have decided to abstain from voting today, pending a new position.
in writing. we believe that the initiative of the swedish government, among others, to preserve data processed and stored in connection with the provision of services such as telephone calls, sms, e-mail, internet, etc. for the purpose of combating terrorism and other cross-border crimes goes far beyond the expected benefits. member states should facilitate the exchange of information among themselves in order to combat these crimes more effectively. however, this does not require harmonisation with uniform rules for the collection of this huge amount of data. the proposal raises serious concerns about the legal certainty of citizens and risks imposing significant costs on service providers. despite its criticism, the report states that harmonisation is necessary in this area - but that legislation must be made on the initiative of the commission and by means of a codecision procedure for parliament instead of the intergovernmental cooperation that is now the case. the junilista does not share this view.
in writing. the junilist believes that member states should be free to decide for themselves what their immigration and asylum policies should be - as long as they are in line with applicable international law. we do not want to contribute to the creation of a fortress europe, nor should it be a matter for the european parliament to determine which laws apply in this area. we cannot therefore support the council's proposal, with or without the amendments proposed in the report, and have therefore abstained, since the directive is part of the creation of a common policy in this area. however, we have voted in favour of many of the committee's amendments to show our support for a more humane refugee policy than that expressed in the council directive and which meets the standards laid down in international conventions.
in writing. the amendments by parliament are moving in the right direction as they do not force countries to refuse to process an application from a person from a 'safe country', which requires that asylum seekers are not treated as criminals and shorter processing times. the european parliament has voted against the green group's proposal to abandon the concept of safe countries altogether and instead follow international mr rules on individual examination. the council will probably not take account of the improvements voted for by parliament, but when we vote on this directive today, we are actually voting on the council's proposals which are contrary to human rights, which is why i am voting against. the environment party is against a harmonised asylum policy because it risks becoming undemocratic and a race to the bottom. in this case, both bad legislation and the threat of parliament going to court over illegal procedures.

for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to comment on the guellec report on territorial cohesion and would like to begin by thanking you for the very, very good cooperation we have had during the time that the report has been drawn up. the major difference between now and 15-25 years ago is that the regions are very active in their own development. you no longer wait for someone else to make sure that you can develop your business and society and create a good life for yourself, you want to do it yourself. it is therefore not surprising that territorial cohesion has become an important concept. it sums up the feeling and the will that exists throughout europe, even in the suburbs of the big cities, that everyone is needed on the european continent, that all citizens are important, that all regions have something to contribute. this must be clear to all of us in the union. that is why the concept of territorial cohesion - that we are holding our europe together, that everyone feels involved - is so important. there is a huge amount of knowledge throughout europe, and we must take advantage of it if we are to develop our continent. if we don't, we're making a mistake. europe as a whole is rich in resources, and they are often found in the regions or in the suburbs of large cities. we have a lot of natural resources which are good not only for the regions when they are processed, but for the whole of europe. it's also about energy. it's what gives us export earnings. we also have very extensive natural resources that we can use in tourism, for recreation. we have a lot of people employed here, we can develop this, it is good for all of europe. we also have diversity, we have minorities and we have immigrants from all over the world in europe whose strength and knowledge must also be harnessed. i would therefore like to highlight some points in the report which i consider important. firstly, the lisbon and gothenburg strategies must be inclusive. we must ensure that they cover all regions, otherwise we cannot develop europe as a whole. the second is that we cannot accept sectoral policies which have the opposite effect, i.e. which only strengthen the centre. thirdly, i would say that it is important that the different policy areas in europe are valued, so that we can really achieve territorial cohesion. an example is the transport sector: we must ensure that we have transport routes that enable the whole of europe to develop. i think this is a good report and i really hope that the commission will listen to what parliament has to say.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to thank our rapporteurs. the railways are needed in europe. we must offer rail transport as an alternative to road transport for both freight and passengers. it's good for the environment and good for our productivity. but then the goods and people must be able to travel by rail quickly and smoothly. it's not like that today. it is not always easy to make a journey from a to b, and goods are often left at the borders. in the second railway package, we are opening up the market for national and international freight transport. it was a good decision, but its full realization has yet to come. it is still slow and faces a number of problems, according to consensus. but should we then open up the railway markets for passenger transport as jarzembowski proposes when the opening up of the market for freight transport is not yet working? some people are concerned that it will be impossible to make it work together in practice. i come from a country where we have opened up passenger traffic in our own way. it is not pure deregulation, but we have done it in the swedish way, so to speak. and we've certainly seen the opportunities, but we've also seen the difficulties when some lines are profitable and some lines are not. this is a difficulty if you have an ambition to have passenger traffic throughout the country. it is important to open up markets to passengers too, and i am convinced that we will eventually have a national opening up of passenger traffic too. but now i would like to propose that we start with international passenger traffic. the commission has also proposed safeguard clauses for local and regional public transport. the decision to open up national passenger services now would be nothing more than an idealistic position taken by the european parliament, without us knowing the consequences.
