mr president, i would like to thank you. the european commission has written to the swedish government asking it to intervene in the work of the supreme court. the basic principle of democracy is the separation of powers. it is constitutionally impossible for the swedish government to intervene in the work of the supreme court. i think this is a serious violation of our constitutional principle. it would therefore be good if parliament could explain to the commission that it too must respect the rules of the game in each country. the supreme court is the highest independent body in sweden; we have no constitutional court or other means of controlling the government. if the commission now calls on our government to influence the supreme court, it would damage our constitution, the way our country works and the way our democracy works. it would be unfortunate if it happened again.
madam president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to thank my colleague mr brejc for his work on this report and for his efforts to concentrate it on the really important issues. i must say, however, that it will be difficult to support the report as it has been drawn up after the committee's consideration if there are no changes to it. the current version of the report states that globalisation is creating gaps and imbalances between countries and within societies. this is not true! if there is one thing that globalization has brought, it is prosperity and well-being to the poorest parts of the world. today we see poverty eradicated in large parts of asia. we see people in taiwan, china, india and more countries gradually gaining dignity and prosperity. globalization has helped to break the cycle of poverty that has plagued large parts of the world for centuries. in these areas we see today growing wealth and growing prosperity and growing respect for the individual. what is important to say is that the opposite is true, because it is in those parts of the world where globalization has not reached that we have stagnation and poverty, for example in those parts of africa and asia where free and open trade is not practiced and where people are excluded from globalization. madam president, it is the lack of globalisation and of participation in the global economy that creates poor people, poor living conditions and undignified working environments. it is also easy to see, for where do we find the worst working environments and the worst social conditions? it's not in the developing countries, but in the countries of the lost world, countries that are excluded from the global economy. it may also be worth asking whether we would be better off if the poor world did not develop. would we be better off if the chinese or indian economies had not developed? the answer to that question is of course no for a number of reasons, partly because it is good that these countries are getting an increase in prosperity, and partly because these are our growing markets. europe is a continent that is very much alive to globalisation. our welfare, our prosperity and our high wages are based on having companies that can operate and grow worldwide with qualified production and qualified services. if we try to shut out globalization, we'll get hit. if we try to impose our rules on other countries, they suffer. it is free trade that historically and today creates growing prosperity and respect for people. we must make that a policy.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by protesting against the fact that we are discussing the court of auditors' report without having had access to it beforehand, which is a major handicap for us. fortunately, we recognise almost everything from previous years; perhaps one could have read last year's report and made the same comments. these are the same problems and the same accounting shortcomings. we're told that the problems will continue. the court of auditors 'cannot guarantee' and so on, they say. it is a question of risky transactions, of deficiencies in the control systems and of amounts with errors, etc., etc. there is a road map for an internal control system, which i welcome, but most of it is as before, i.e. bad. i would point out, however, that the european institutions are audited by the european court of auditors, whereas national systems are of course audited by different national audit bodies. as in so many other areas, an 80/20 rule applies here too, i.e. one fifth of the cash flows can be controlled here and the remaining part nationally. it is extremely urgent that we come up with a proposal on how we can develop structured cooperation in this area, otherwise the court's task will continue to be practically impossible. the main problems we see are from the cap, the structural funds and the committee of the regions. there are grounds for abolishing the agricultural policy, abolishing the committee of the regions and abolishing the european economic and social committee. all this belongs to another period and has no function in today's eu. instead, the structural funds should start to go directly to the poorer member states of the eu. then all the problems we are talking about would largely disappear.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission said that the lisbon strategy was reflected in this work programme. it does in part, but not fully. you may recall that we had a discussion on the balance of the lisbon strategy. we in parliament said that social policy, social justice and social cohesion should not be seen as a residual item after growth and employment. i thought we had agreed that we would see social policy as an integral part of the lisbon process. this is not reflected in this work programme. the social dimension is very small. we have talked about how better regulation does not by definition mean less regulation. i can say that there is no legislation at all in the social field. there are three communications and a green paper, which i welcome, but there is no legislation at all. it is not that parliament has not made proposals. let me bring up a couple of them. the first concerns the new forms of employment, the so-called atypical forms of employment, which are growing like an avalanche, and which bring less security, less influence and probably more stress in the workplace. we have called for a directive to deal with these new forms of employment. secondly, mr barroso, you and i have been to a conference on restructuring. i understood it as if we agreed that in order to carry out the restructuring we had to do it in a way that involved the employees in the process. we have instruments at european level. we have the european works councils and we have asked parliament to audit the european works councils. we haven't seen any of this. in conclusion, i would just like to raise the question of a programme for the integration of the disabled, a non-discrimination programme, not only in the field of the labour market but everywhere. it also shines with its absence.
in writing. we have voted against the three daul proposals. they do, however, concern minor amendments to existing regulations. but we want to make clear our opposition in principle to a common organization of the market in seeds, hops and wine. the whole cap is an absurd creation and must be abolished.
in writing. the report on the social dimension of globalisation was voted down by the moderates today because it has the wrong basic view of globalisation. globalisation is an ongoing process of change that contributes to freedom and prosperity for more and more people. globalisation is a major driver of economic growth through free trade, increased international investment and the transfer of knowledge. together with democratic development, opportunities are created for developing countries to get out of poverty. the eu is a positive example of globalisation with the free movement of goods, services, people and capital. dictatorships perceive the transformative power of globalization as a threat to their own closed society. we moderates welcome globalisation and its role as a catalyst for democracy.
in writing. the resolution on the 'social dimension of globalisation' contains proposals which, in the resolution, include a policy to abolish eu export subsidies under the common agricultural policy and their family members, leading in most cases to greater eu power. we are opposed to this kind of three-pronged centralisation of the eu, which is being carried out without proper analysis or justification. a common eu policy on the 'social dimension of globalisation' cannot be formulated because the member states of the union are so different. this applies, among other things, to tax levels and social protection. instead of being forced to implement a top-down policy, eu countries should learn from each other through institutional competition. we believe that reforms must have popular support in every country. we also believe that the less developed countries should be given the right to regulate their imports of agricultural products for the time being. in view of the above, we choose to vote against the resolution in the final vote. we support the amendments, however, which call for a radical reform of the cap and the abolition of export subsidies. we also support the proposal that the eu must open up its market to goods from less developed countries.
the committee on international trade has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. chemicals are important, they're vital. they are part of our daily lives and a prerequisite for much of modern human activity. at the same time, there is a general concern about chemicals. what are they doing to us and our environment? some concern is justified. that is why i am glad that we are here to vote on a new european chemicals legislation, legislation which will enable us to know which chemicals pose a high risk to us and which do not, to ban chemicals we need to get rid of and to keep those we need. we have helped to make reach a priority, to give greater attention to the substances that pose a major threat. we have exemptions for things we know are harmless, such as cellulose and iron ore. this avoids red tape and unnecessary costs, but above all it means using our limited resources to achieve the greatest possible security. many goals are set in europe that we are not actually living up to. decisions in this chamber often promise a lot but sometimes hold back. the old chemical legislation was a prime example of this. it should have given us security but has accomplished almost nothing. sometimes failures are just embarrassing, but in chemical policy they can be life-threatening. that is why we need reach but a reach that can deliver on its promises. the great challenge for reach is to design a policy that does not create barriers to trade, that does not restrict the ability of other countries to sell their products to the european market. that would be wrong for the world around us but also for european consumers. it is unreasonable for us to be drawing up legislation here which is then declared illegal by the wto. i therefore hope that the house will take up the proposals to reduce trade barriers put forward by the committee on international trade. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. the next breath they take is to accuse the whole of parliament of not caring about the environment. the main newspaper of the swedish social democrats even calls the leader of its own group in this parliament, martin schulz, a defector. i think it's cowardly, and it doesn't help the environment. instead, they should ask themselves why they're standing by. the overwhelming majority, however, from the bourgeois groups of the ppe-de and alde to the socialist group and now also the commission, are in agreement, and it is we who will be putting forward the proposal. i am pleased to have been part of the vote on a new, strong european chemicals legislation.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. chemicals in europe are regulated by very poor legislation for older chemicals, about 30 000, and relatively good legislation for new ones, about 3 000. the new proposal replaces 43 directives and national legislation in 25 countries, legislation which varies widely in control and quality, from quite poor to quite good. at the same time, it is 100 years since we closed national markets. for example, if you want to buy a toy for your child, you will find that some of the items are produced in your country, but most of them are imported. for all these reasons, new chemicals legislation represents a great opportunity for the environment, people and businesses. the committee on industry was the first committee to take decisions. we were able to lift the proposal for a chemicals directive from a dead and highly controversial product to an opportunity to actually reach a decision. i would like to thank the members of the committee for a very constructive and good cooperation. the committee on industry, which is responsible for industry, small businesses and research, has therefore, in accordance with its mandate, focused its work on changes paving the way for new, modern technologies, innovations and modern environmental technologies. in europe, environmentally-driven industries are growing at twice the rate of average industry and are an incredibly important tool for creating jobs and sustainable growth in a knowledge-based economy. however, for reach to be this engine for sustainable growth, the proposal needs to be simplified, strengthened and clarified. it needs to be simplified for the survival of small businesses, strengthened for a better environment and clarified because it is very vague on some points. for example, it may not be intended that each mineral load be analysed separately. ladies and gentlemen, we have four major issues in the eu: the budgetary plan is a mess, the proposal for a service directive is a mess, the draft constitutional treaty has a 'period of reflection'. after seven years of debate, we actually need a decision on the fourth important proposal, namely the one on european chemical legislation. ladies and gentlemen, uncertainty is very expensive. therefore, let us vote "yes" on thursday for the compromises that are on the table.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the reasons why we need a new chemicals legislation in europe have been well described. i shall therefore not go into that. within the alde group we have identified ten difficult political points. i would therefore like to describe some of them. the credible evaluations of the draft law have shown that there are particular problems for small enterprises in the volumes 1-10 tonnes. therefore, a simplified registration for small enterprises is needed for non-hazardous chemicals. at the same time, higher standards are needed for chemicals suspected of being dangerous. the compromise means that additional information is required for about 30% of substances, while a simplified registration is sufficient for the remaining substances. i think that's a good balance, maybe not perfect, but acceptable. the best must not be the enemy of the good when it comes to matters of such importance as this. it is often said that a good compromise is characterized by everyone being equally dissatisfied with the result. i think that in this case the opposite is true, that is to say that most people are reasonably satisfied with the result. the proposal also needs reinforcement. every individual consumer should have the right to know whether there is any hazardous chemical in the product he or she is buying. we therefore have in the compromise also rules on liability, or duty of care, which clearly belong to the companies. the alde group also proposes an amendment which clearly states that the burden of proof should be on the companies. an important issue concerns the authorization, i.e. the decision itself on the chemicals. a strong substitution principle for hazardous chemicals that can be replaced by less hazardous alternatives is important. the chemicals that need to be authorised are not just any chemicals, but those that can cause cancer, are harmful to reproduction and are enriched in the human body - in short, 'the worst of the worst'. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. so i'm glad this is included. furthermore, reach needs to be clarified. when the mining industry thinks that it has to test every truckload of ore that is taken out of the mine, something is wrong. the proposal contains similar grey areas and oddities that all three major political groups have been very much in agreement to remove. i would like to thank commissioners verheugen and dimas. i was a little worried when you brought this room paper, but i am very glad that the commission has now decided to support the main line of the three major groups in this parliament. i would also like to congratulate the united kingdom on the meritorious work it has done and is doing, as well as luxembourg on the work that this presidency has done before. the resolution which i hope we will vote on thursday in this house is very similar to the proposal put forward by the presidency. this means that we now have the opportunity both to stabilise the whole issue and to reach a decision which the alde group considers to be extremely important. we will support the compromise proposals on the table.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank mr sacconi for his work on reach and for the compromises he has made with us on authorization and so on. the attacks by nassauer, schulz, poettering and ek on reach are, on the other hand, a concessionary policy of the german chemical industry, which is destroying the environment and health and sabotaging workers and all small businesses who want to know what the chemicals they buy do and how they affect us. four years ago, the european parliament called for a stronger reach in the schörling report. for a year the environment committee has been working in an efficient and balanced way to protect public interests, health and the environment. now the whole parliament seems hypnotized by the lobbying tricks of the german chemical industry with nassauer as the first leader of the magic stick. how can you in the ppe-de group defend the attack on small businesses in the nassauer compromise, which means that there is no full data sharing and five years more time for them to pay for data that the large companies have? how can you defend the fact that the registration fee was removed from the volume and that there is a lack of clarity about the costs? wake up from the power of the magic wand and vote for the alternative compromise. how can you in the pse group defend the massive erosion of low-volume chemical testing? how can you defend vague criteria when even high-volume chemicals can be excluded from tests that detect cancer risks? how can we protect workers without information and clear requirements on the working environment? wake up from your enchantment and vote no to nassauer and yes to the alternative compromise. you liberals, break the spell and stand up for liberal politics. give consumers the opportunity and the knowledge to opt out of hazardous chemicals. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of consumers. it risks turning us all into guinea pigs by removing the explicit protection against consumer exposure to research chemicals. moreover, the compromise bases consumer protection on the need for a risk assessment on available data, but it was precisely this lack of data that reach was intended to address. that doesn't make your compromise. i therefore ask you to break the spell of the german chemical industry's false lure of growth. the only thing that will grow with a gutted reach is the cancer tumors in our citizens. it would take me an hour to list all the organizations that want a stronger reach. listen to them! you will only have the chance to vote for a stronger reach if you vote against the nassauer compromise and for the alternative proposal.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i speak for the overwhelming majority of my group. the left in the eu wants a strong reach. we want a reach that means we get to know the effects of chemicals even if the chemicals are made in lower volumes. we want a chemical policy which means that it is mandatory to phase out and ban the most dangerous chemicals. we want to see a clear responsibility for the products of the companies. this is beneficial both for public health and for the safety of employees. every serious analysis shows that the benefits of a functioning chemicals policy far outweigh the often greatly exaggerated costs of the same policy. reach should be taken for granted; companies should know what they are doing and take responsibility for what they are doing. in the committee on the environment, we have reached a generally good compromise. i deeply regret that the socialists and liberals have run away from that compromise and have chosen instead to make a deal with the right. the nassauer/sacconi compromise dramatically weakens what we wanted to achieve for reach to work. that means we won't know the effect of the chemicals. perhaps as much as 90 percent of the low-volume chemicals are excluded, and even chemicals in the higher volume range are excluded from real tests. this means that we will not be able to obtain the knowledge needed for a functioning chemicals policy. we consider this to be totally unacceptable. our group will never accept such a weakening of the chemical policy. we have therefore chosen to put forward the alternative proposal together with the green group. many members of this parliament have in practice acted as the lobbyists' extension arms for the chemical industry. i think that's been sad to see at times. to lena ek, i would like to say the following: you talk about the environment, but you have consistently worked at every opportunity to weaken this bill and make it worse. this is the most important environmental proposal we have dealt with in the eu system for many years. what you're representing is not environmental policy. i would like to say to the european commission: you've lost credibility on environmental issues. you're running away from your own proposal. you do not even stand up and defend what you yourself proposed as recently as a few years ago. i consider that weak. you have no credibility in environmental policy anymore. and finally, the apple of sacconis. if you got this apple from nassauer, i'd be careful. it's probably crammed full of dangerous chemicals and pesticides and probably rotted inside.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is regrettable that strong forces in this parliament are working hard to make chemical legislation as watered down as possible. reach is a cross-border issue, affecting both the environment and the internal market. the eu action is therefore justified. i believe that the information requirements for chemicals in lower volumes need to be strengthened. if this is not done, a large part of the purpose of reach is lost. i also support a strong substitution principle. sweden has already introduced this, and it is working very well. i believe that consumers should have the right to be informed about chemicals in goods. this is a prerequisite for them to make an active and informed choice. it is also important to clarify that it is the industry, not the authorities, that should be responsible for the evaluation and risk assessment of the registered chemicals. there is no contradiction between a strong reach and a well-functioning market. being at the forefront of this issue will provide a competitive advantage in the long term. i shall therefore vote against the compromise proposal drawn up by the pse, ppe and alde groups.
mr president, i would like to thank you. like other speakers, i welcome the fact that we are getting stricter legislation in this area. it is strange - to put it mildly - that this area has been so relatively free of regulations for so long. we know the risks of chemicals. every time we have encountered a serious problem, such as pcbs, ddt or freons, we have been taken completely by surprise. the damage has been extensive. so we have to be much more cautious. of course, companies must take responsibility for both information and the replacement of dangerous substances by less dangerous ones, where they exist. i would like to stress that the substitution principle has worked well in my own country for almost 15 years. i believe that this principle must be incorporated into the new legislation. i hope that as many colleagues as possible, including my own group, will agree with me on this point. the debate on reach has been complex. they say the devil is in the details. there are few places in the world where that's more true than right here. there are many difficult details which many of us members do not understand, and this has made it particularly difficult to find constructive solutions. the compromise reached in the 11th hour regarding registration is not perfect. it deviates in several respects from the line i would prefer to take. in view of the circumstances and what the alternative might be, namely a line where the primary responsibility for collecting information would be placed on the agency, i must nevertheless be in favour of the compromise. i repeat that i am not satisfied, but the most important thing is that we have a workable policy in this area so that we can gradually clean up the chemical jungle. finally, i would like to congratulate mr sacconi, who has done an excellent job for a very long time in complex circumstances.

for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the operation and decommissioning of nuclear power are activities that can have cross-border effects in europe. unlike most other activities, they must therefore be controlled at eu level. however, nuclear power is like other process industries; technological developments often lead to life extension. even reactor tanks may be replaced in the future. we therefore do not know for sure when a reactor must be decommissioned. the swedish reactors, which are now on average a quarter of a century old, are now being powered up considerably. the remaining lifespan is therefore assumed to be significant. it is clear that a winding-up entails costs and that it requires the provision of funds to meet these costs. in my home country of sweden, where about half of all electricity comes from nuclear reactors, this financing has been addressed from the outset. i think we can assume that all democratic countries have done the same. detailed and bureaucratic instructions for managing this process are unnecessary. the need for technocrats in brussels to be guardians of experienced nuclear experts in the member states seems almost ridiculous. the governments and authorities of the member states are under democratic control and cannot expose their citizens to risks. it is clear, however, that the member states which have been subject to undemocratic, communist regimes are in a substantially different situation. the commission notes that the commission has not provided any evidence that the measures in question are imputable to the state. it is a clear act of solidarity on the part of the richer member states to provide funds for this. but how much? the junilist considers that the amount of funding for bohunice v1 in slovakia should be set provisionally at the amount of eur 237 million proposed by the commission for the period 2007-2013, as the budgetary plan has not been adopted. we should therefore not commit ourselves to support from the community budget for the period after this.
the rapporteur. madam president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank minister beckett in particular for coming here today. climate change is one of the most serious threats we face. i want to emphasize that it can no longer be seen as an environmental problem. a warmer climate is a more unstable climate, and it poses a threat to virtually every sector of our society, a threat that must be considered part of our security problem. it is a security threat to the eu and its member states but also, of course, to many poor countries in the tropics. while i say this, i would like to stress the importance of not seeing the climate issue as a problem and a burden. if we do the right things, we can reduce the risks to society in the future. if we do the right things, we can also open up opportunities for business and technology in a range of areas. there's a collaborative group called the climate group in the world, which is a bunch of big companies that are showing that in a fairly short period of time, it's possible to both reduce emissions and make a lot of money. i believe that the necessary transformation of energy and transport systems in our part of the world should be a major lever in the lisbon process and should be able to create new jobs and export opportunities. the parliament's report proposes a number of measures for the post-2012 strategy. we believe that the eu should continue to play a leading role in climate action at international level. the recent comments by mr beckett confirmed that this view is shared by the council. we need to do more in the short term to really live up to the requirements of the kyoto protocol. but we're only responsible for a fraction of the emissions, about 14 percent, today. so we need to bring in other players. it is very urgent that we should have constructive cooperation with the united states in the long term. we need to set long-term goals. we are demanding, or wanting, reductions of 30% by 2020 and 60% to 80% by 2050. it is, moreover, a constant demand from the business community that it needs long-term rules of the game. the situation of the developing countries is of course of the utmost importance. for them, energy is first and foremost a matter of growth and development, but they do not need to repeat our mistakes. we need strategic partnerships, not least with the major developing countries, to stimulate technological leaps and investments in the best possible technology. what has been started in this area by the commission's agreement with china is of course very good, but it must be scaled up and made much, much bigger. consider that china is starting two new coal-fired power plants every month! we need to invest more in research and development. it is a fact today that governments around the world are spending less as a percentage of gdp on energy research than they did 30 years ago. it's completely incomprehensible to me. i often compare this to the apollo project. if the americans could put a man on the moon within ten years, we should be able to do something similar with massive research and development efforts in terms of a breakthrough for new technologies. we must take strong action at home to gain credibility in politics. we must stimulate energy conservation, as has already been stressed on several occasions. this means that the building directive, for example, should be extended and made more ambitious. the transport sector is a very critical area. we welcome the inclusion of shipping and aviation in climate policy. we are in favour of rapid action to make rail transport more efficient. we believe that we must draw the right conclusions from the difficulties of reducing emissions from road transport. we therefore need and propose ambitious and binding rules on co2 emissions from new vehicles. we need to help new technologies - and they exist - to come to market. there are, however, rows of obstacles. one of them, of course, is that we are still subsidizing conventional, i.e., fossil-based, technology. we need to expand emissions trading, but we must of course make sure that allocation is not as ambitious as it was last year. instead, we must lower the ceiling step by step. we must invite other players to participate in this, so that we do not become the only market in europe. the majority of the measures proposed in the report are aimed at firms and producers. we also believe that we must involve citizens more actively. we all have lifestyles that affect our development. we have an idea to consider in the long term some form of emissions trading on an individual level. perhaps this is not possible in practice, but a first step should be to increase the knowledge of the carbon content of everything we buy and transport. we in parliament and in the european institutions should of course live as we teach. we should make sure that our buildings and our transport are as low-carbon as possible. there is much to be done in this area. in conclusion, i would like to wish both the commission and the council good luck in montreal. ensure that the eu can continue to play a leading role and drive international climate action towards positive results.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it was pleasing to hear commissioner dimas at the international energy conference in beijing the other day expressing his support for parliament's ambition of 25 per cent renewable energy by 2020, and commissioner dimas also saying that renewable energy is a cornerstone for overcoming problems such as climate change, poverty and general economic development. however, if we are to create a market for renewable energy, the costs of today's unsustainable reliance on fossil fuels must be clearly demonstrated. it is not enough to show the effects of these enormous emissions on the environment and public health, they must also be expressed in terms of euros and cents. europe has already embarked on a market-driven system of emission allowances. the air transport part of the system is certainly being discussed, but i would also like to see maritime transport and, above all, land transport. it is only when we see clearly the costs of carbon dioxide emissions from this sector that we will really boost both the demand and the supply of renewable energy sources. my first question is therefore whether and when the commission intends to include the transport sector in the european emissions trading scheme. my second question concerns the share of renewable energy sources. we want to increase them, but they must also be used. when will the commission propose to replace the so-called 'auto-oil' directive with a minimum 10% blend of ethanol in motor fuel?
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr anders wijkman for a balanced report on an important issue. i would like to stress that it is difficult to see today that europe will achieve the kyoto targets. they are very demanding and the policies currently being pursued do not seem to be able to achieve them in the first place. one reason for this is that the requirements of modern environmental policy are largely ignored in energy policy. given the conditions we have, we will not be able to meet the kyoto targets by relying on renewable fuels. they will grow up and play a bigger role, but they will not solve the problems. we cannot achieve the kyoto targets by regulating the economy or by cutting costs, because that would be contrary to the aim of creating the conditions for a better economy that takes better account of environmental requirements. we cannot solve the problems by slowing down economic development, but we must have an environmental policy which is compatible with a consistent and credible energy policy. then we must also look at the basis of our energy supply. oil and gas is not the way of the future. nevertheless, we see that it is primarily the use of oil and gas that increases as nuclear power is phased out, with increasing carbon dioxide emissions as a consequence. nuclear power generation in europe represents 50% of co2 emissions in the current energy mix. nuclear power generation in europe reduces co2 emissions equivalent to those from passenger cars. they show the size of the numbers we're talking about. nuclear power cannot be ignored in this case. nuclear power will not solve the environmental problems, but you cannot solve the problems of our environment and the kyoto targets by ignoring nuclear power. it is therefore an important task for this parliament and also for the member states to pursue a credible energy policy which does not aim at phasing out nuclear power and replacing it with oil and gas, but at ensuring that nuclear power can play a significant role in the balance of energy policy that is needed.
rapporteur. – mr president, i wish to add the following text to the end of paragraph 24 after the words ‘blending ratios’: ‘examining the environmental effectiveness of requiring 10% biofuel blends in transport fuels’.
in writing. we have voted against the report as a whole, because we believe that the commission's proposal is better. it is appropriate to inform air passengers of the air carrier operating a particular flight in order to enable them to make well-informed decisions from, inter alia, a safety perspective. we support the commission's proposal that member states collect security information to share with the commission and the other member states, but we oppose the parliament's proposal to introduce a community list instead and thus centralise the administration.
in writing. we voted against amendments nos 5 and 12 on an increase in the amount of funding for the decommissioning of the bohunice v1 nuclear power plant, as we believe that the budgetary framework for 2007-2013 should not be exceeded. of course, the decommissioning of nuclear power plants must be carried out in an environmentally and safely manner.
in writing. we support the eu's nordic cooperation, the so-called northern dimension, but believe that cooperation must be based on intergovernmental cooperation, strictly limited and focused on concrete results. environmental issues, nuclear safety and the fight against international crime should be given priority. we support the proposal that the eu should develop cooperation with regional organisations, including the nordic council. nordic cooperation can serve as a model for the eu, as it shows how free movement can be promoted and encouraged without a large-scale supranational bureaucracy. but for the reasons stated above, we vote in favour of the report.
in writing. global climate change is a major problem which we believe there is good reason to address at international level. the wijkman report has many merits. the junilists, however, reiterate their position that the european parliament should not take over the foreign policy of the member states by inviting third countries which have not yet ratified the kyoto protocol to do so. it is also regrettable that the report contains a number of calls to member states which should be dealt with at national level. these include proposals for common eu-wide speed limits, traffic charges, tax incentives, the development of rail transport and public transport in general. we are also critical of eu initiatives to make citizens aware of the climate impact of excessive consumption and production. this can of course be dealt with at national level. we have voted against the report as a whole, but have supported individual amendments, which we believe will improve the report.
in writing. we are in favour of a minimum level of carbon tax within the eu. but we believe that such a decision must be taken by the council unanimously, not by majority vote. taxation will continue to be levied at national level and not by the union.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission's proposal on trap traps is extremely flawed. the proposal includes testing traps to strict standards, special training for trappers and the exceptional use of homemade traps. if this proposal is adopted by the european parliament, it will make trapping practically impossible in the nordic countries. it is gratifying that the committee on the environment rejected the commission's proposal. hunting matters should be decided at national or local level, not in brussels. i would draw the attention of members to the fact that the committee on legal affairs has delivered an opinion on the legal basis of the proposal. this opinion has been incorporated into the report of the committee on the environment and recommends that article 175 should be the legal basis of the proposal. this article concerns the union's environmental policy. however, animal welfare for wildlife is not part of eu environmental policy and is not even within eu competence. if article 175 is the legal basis, there is a risk that more and more cases like this will be considered as eu matters and handled at eu level. i agree with the rapporteur, mr scheele, that the commission's proposal should be rejected. however, mr scheele's view is that the proposal does not go far enough. i'm of the opposite opinion. of course, rules are needed, including on trapping, to ensure, among other things, the protection of animals. however, these rules are to be established by the member states and not by brussels.

member of the commission. mr president, the rapporteur mr romeva i rueda should again be commended for putting together a report that challenges member states to attain better, more stringent, harmonised controls on arms exports. whilst the report's primary focus is on stimulating improvement in european practice, it also recognises the eu's potential to stimulate best practice in the european neighbourhood and in the wider world. it advocates an international arms trade treaty. the commission strongly supports opening negotiations on a legally binding arms trade treaty. we believe this would be of great value, not least in reducing the spread of illicit small arms and light weapons, which help fuel human insecurity, crime and conflict around the world. this is an area where the commission does not take the lead but where we are fully associated under the cfsp. the commission is regularly involved, with other international organisations and ngos, in dealing with the consequences of inappropriate or illegal arms sales. we are also contributing, through implementing specific projects, to reducing destabilising accumulations of weapons around the world. as the report points out, around half a million people die each year as a result of small arms violence. in the face of these sobering statistics we cannot be complacent. turning to the second part – the non-proliferation of weapons of mass destruction – i would first like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr kristovskis, on this very important work. it is now two years since the adoption of the eu strategy against the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. the non-proliferation regime is seriously challenged. the risk of terrorists acquiring weapons of mass destruction is considered by many a very serious threat. the european union is defining its budgetary priorities for the coming years. all of this makes the report not only important but also very timely. the area is not new to the commission. as mentioned in the report, the commission has long been engaged in cooperative assistance programmes to reduce the risks associated with wmd-related materials, equipment and technologies, particularly in the area of the former soviet union. the commission has also gained experience by implementing eu joint actions on non-proliferation in russia under the cfsp framework. these actions constitute the core of the commission's contribution so far to the g8 global partnership against the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. i, along with the rapporteur and the honourable members of parliament, very much regret that the negotiations on non-proliferation in new york were not very successful. this does not send a good signal to countries like iran. the eu strategy calls for the creation of a specific community budget line in this domain. the commission has responded to this call by inserting important non-proliferation objectives in its october 2004 proposal for the stability instrument. this and subsequent proposals are under consideration here and in the council. a positive outcome to these negotiations is essential. while the overall financial issues are being discussed, i would like to express my appreciation to parliament for enabling, in agreement with the council, the commission to implement a series of pilot projects in this area. they help to define how community instruments might best contribute to strengthening eu non-proliferation assistance. in early december a high-level interparliamentary conference will take place in brussels, where a series of independent studies on the way ahead will be presented and debated. i am grateful for the excellent cooperation with parliament in organising this event. we hope to see many parliamentarians present on that occasion. as requested in the report, the commission stands ready to provide further information to parliament on past and ongoing community programmes supporting wmd non-proliferation and disarmament goals. it goes without saying that the commission looks forward to continued good cooperation with the council and parliament in support of these overall objectives. let me now turn to defence procurement. the role of the european parliament in such a sensitive debate is crucial, and i am glad that the commission will now be able to take parliament's contribution into account. as you know, in the next few weeks the commission will adopt a communication on the results of the public consultation that has taken place over the last few months. it is very important that this communication adequately reflects the position taken by parliament. i warmly thank, on behalf of commissioner mccreevy, all those who made this possible, in particular mr wuermeling, mrs beer and mr hökmark, who did a remarkable job in their respective committees. the commission, which was urged back in 2002 by the european parliament to take initiatives in this field, is particularly happy with the content of this house's contribution. the ultimate objective of the commission in this field is to open up defence markets, which are today, as the rapporteur said, highly fragmented, and to increase the efficiency of public spending by encouraging competition and transparency in these markets. this should benefit both buyers and taxpayers but also the european defence industry, which is suffering from a market structure which prevents it from competing in the global market place. the public debate carried out over the last few months and the various contributions received show that the majority of the stakeholders share the same objective. even member states now seem convinced that something has to be done quickly in order to move things forward. the current negotiations within the european defence agency on a code of conduct clearly show the political will to put an end to a situation that is unanimously considered unsatisfactory. the commission is, therefore, ready to play its part in this process. in particular, it could clear up those issues where the consultation has shown that different interpretations of the existing rules have led to differences in implementation. this could be done easily and quickly, by means of an interpretative communication, under the community's and the commission's sole responsibility. but also – and perhaps more importantly – it could propose new, more flexible rules for the procurement of defence material, rules which better suit the specific nature of this material. this would be neither quick nor easy. it would require a new legislative proposal. at this stage, a specific directive on defence procurement seems to be, therefore, the most appropriate legal instrument, which could address all the important issues that national authorities need to take into account when they procure defence material, such as security of supply, confidentiality or particular urgency. the commission will carry out the necessary preliminary assessments and consultations and the european parliament will be closely involved in this process. i thank you very much for your support and for your three very important reports.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, i have to say that i am very pleased. i shall speak about defence procurement and i would like to thank mr wuermeling for his report on this subject. i think it is important to stress that defence procurement plays a crucial role in enabling europe to be part of the technological and military leadership. however, it is also crucial for us to play a role in research and development in this area. that means we need to have large companies that can operate internationally and produce the best products. it is about getting better value for money, but it is also about being involved in developing military technology that enables europe to assert itself in security policy and in cooperation with other parts of the world. a fundamental question is how we can have the industrial capacity to be at the forefront of the development of modern technology. europe's problem is that we have a fragmented market because of historically different security interests. with declining purchasing, lower defence spending and, it is important to remember, a sharp increase in the need for research and development, europe is in a delicate position if we are to be able to keep up with developments in the united states and elsewhere. one of the more crucial tasks will therefore be to ensure that, in the type of defence procurement covered by article 296 of the treaty, we can have increased cooperation in the area of procurement but also in the area of open competition among producers. if we do not succeed in this, we will not be able to compete and develop the most advanced defence equipment. the most advanced defence equipment is covered by article 296, which allows member states to derogate. in order to change this situation, the first step is to have a common code of conduct and, in the longer term, a directive. however, it is important that this work be started, otherwise the foundations of the european defence industry will be eroded.
mr president, i would like to thank you. wuermeling argues in his report that it is not a question of foreign, security or defence policy in general but only of the procurement of goods and services, but of course there is a link between defence procurement and defence policy. a common market for defence procurement is a further step in the military build-up, which is being eagerly advocated by the european defence industry, alongside the establishment of the european defence agency. the rapporteur argues for harmonisation and standardisation in line with accepted nato standards, under the pretext of greater economic efficiency and a reduction in the burden on european taxpayers. if this is not a contribution to the defence policy debate, i wonder what it is. it is clear to me that this is an extended, intensified militarization. i could say, in the words of george orwell, that war has become peace. instead of talking about increasing resources for research and development of new weapons, the eu - which i believe the eu must do - and the member states should invest in research on peace and conflict resolution. it is through disarmament and demilitarisation that we build peace, not through further militarisation and weapons.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would just like to draw the bureau's attention to one thing. at the beginning of this week i discovered my name on 18 amendments which i had not seen and had certainly not signed. i also did not share the view expressed in those amendments. i hope this will not happen again. it was a very difficult task for me to explain that i did not support these amendments at all.
