in writing. junilista is committed to limiting eu cooperation to genuinely crossborder issues. these include the internal market and many environmental issues. we welcome a strong chemicals directive which clarifies the responsibilities of industry for the chemicals it imports. we are convinced that in the long term it is a competitive advantage for swedish and european industry to be at the forefront of this issue. we have therefore chosen to oppose the compromises supported by the majority of the european parliament. we shall vote in favour of the whole of the proposal, because otherwise the commission may withdraw the proposal for a directive. we believe that even low-volume substances should be subject to basic control requirements. both the swedish chemicals agency and the swedish government have stressed the need to include these in reach. we support a strong substitution principle and would like to emphasise that the responsibility for collecting and analysing information on imported chemicals lies primarily with industry, not with public authorities. we are of course in favour of the exemption of natural substances from the forestry and mining industries from reach. the backbone of sweden's economy, forestry and mining, will not be affected by the legislative proposal. we are also critical of the amendments which advocate the creation of a giant chemical authority within the eu. we have relied on the national authorities to be able to carry out the relevant checks.
in writing. junilista is committed to limiting eu cooperation to genuinely crossborder issues. these include the internal market and many environmental issues. i welcome the chemicals directive, which clarifies the responsibilities of industry for the chemicals it imports. i have voted in favour of the motion as a whole. i support a strong substitution principle. i also support the idea of exempting natural substances from the forestry and mining industries from reach. the backbone of sweden's economy, forestry and mining, will not be affected by the legislative proposal. priority should be given to the collection of information on chemicals. the first priority is to collect and analyse data on high-volume chemicals. the chemical legislation must be effective, not just a paper trail. i am critical of the amendments which advocate the creation of a giant chemical authority within the eu. i have full confidence that the national authorities are essentially capable of carrying out the relevant checks.
in writing. the report is about creating a binding code of conduct for the export of war material within the eu framework. it is proposed that a new eu body be set up to control member states' exports. whatever the view on principle, there is reason to question whether this leads to a more restrictive policy, which is the aim. at the same time, the european defence agency is working to further coordinate and liberalise the export policies of the member states. we oppose the creation of an eu body to control the export of war material. we also do not think that the european defence agency is justified. the size of a country's exports of war material should be decided by each country's national parliament. sweden should therefore be entitled to continue to pursue a restrictive export policy. in view of the above, we shall vote against the report in the final vote.
in writing. we believe that the united nations is best placed - given its experience, knowledge and global reach - to drive global disarmament and ensure compliance with the non-proliferation treaty. on this basis, we are voting against the creation of an eu agency for conversion and disarmament. we shall also vote against the report in the final vote. but we support the call for nuclear disarmament and the objective of a nuclear-free zone in europe. the requirements to limit the proliferation of nuclear weapons should lead in the long term to the elimination of all nuclear weapons.
in writing. we swedish social democrats have voted in favour of the report because we think it is important to have clear and unambiguous rules for defence procurement. however, there are some words in the report which, in their broadest interpretation, could be taken to mean a future common defence. we are against a common european military defence and do not support proposals to create one. we want to see the eu strengthen its capacity to detect and prevent conflicts and manage crises early, primarily through political, diplomatic and economic means.
in writing. article 296 of the ec treaty provides for exemptions from the rules on the free movement of goods for trade in and the manufacture of military equipment. part of this report calls for tightening and clarifying the types of equipment and services to which article 296 should not apply. the junilista considers the completion of the internal market to be a very important issue which should be given priority. however, the report shows that the changes to the eu acquis proposed are largely aimed at deepening military cooperation within the union, taking another step towards a common foreign and security policy. it is the junilist's view that foreign and security policy is a national issue, which should not be decided at eu level. that is why we are voting against this report.
, in writing. this report contains a wide range of proposals for eu action to help combat poverty in africa. the european union is not, however, a comprehensive development policy. today, the eu is giving resources with one hand, to take with the other. this is not credible behavior. the first proposal should be for the eu to carry out a radical reorganization of its agricultural, fisheries and trade policies. unfortunately, there is no political will in this house to do so. the british presidency and the commission have put forward the priority of development issues in africa as a policy area. nevertheless, the council, the commission and the european parliament all defend the eu's fisheries agreements. these agreements are aimed primarily at safeguarding the eu's short-term economic interests and generate very limited resources for those who need them, namely poor fishermen in developing countries. it is essential to avoid fragmentation and duplication of aid. i do not, however, believe that the eu should play a leading role in the world. we do not need more aid agencies, but rather we should seek to concentrate our joint efforts within existing agencies. the eu's primary task should be to reform the union's destructive policies. if this is not done, the developing countries will never have a fair chance of achieving greater prosperity and development.
rapporteur. – mr president, before we start the debate i should like to ask you, on behalf of my group and in agreement with the other groups, to hold the debate but to postpone the vote. we agreed, for a number of reasons, to do that and i would ask you to give us that opportunity before i start my speech.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we are now going to discuss a report which is quite unique to this parliament. for the first time, the european parliament has been invited to play an active part in shaping eu development policy. when the current policy was decided, parliament was not involved in any way. the current process, which aims at a tripartite agreement between the commission, the council and parliament, is therefore, from our point of view, a major step forward. today's report is parliament's response to the original draft policy statement and our contribution to the negotiations. since the adoption of the original development policy in 2000, our world has undergone a series of changes. there are therefore strong reasons for changing and designing a new policy. the agenda has changed, and cohesion, coordination, etc. have been given increasing prominence. i think it's perfectly fine. our report refers to the commitments to increase aid made by a number of member states, which will mean that the union's aid will increase substantially in the coming years. this is, of course, excellent. however, it is equally important to discuss quality as it is to discuss quantity. we are trying to do this in a constructive way in our report. the eu as a whole is the world's largest donor. however, the effectiveness and quality of aid so far have not been commensurate with the size of the aid, as commissioner michel himself pointed out a number of major shortcomings, not least in the coordination in the past. eu member states are poorly coordinated with each other, with the commission and with other donors. this means high transaction costs, a lot of duplication of work and difficulties for our partner countries. the new development policy which we are now discussing offers good prospects for progress in this area. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: in addition to donor coordination and cohesion, our report highlights a number of substantive issues to which we wish to attach particular importance. these are democracy and human rights, action against corruption, the important role of civil society and also of national parliaments, the importance of strengthening the role of women in development work, the importance of paying greater attention to the importance of natural resources, i.e. forests, arable land, water resources and marine resources, not least for the poorest countries in the world. we also discuss the importance of health and education, an issue we have been debating for many years in parliament, and increased efforts to combat the hiv epidemic. finally, we mention in particular the major problems facing many countries in tropical areas because of the more unstable climate. i would like to thank my colleagues for a very good cooperation. as i see it, we have produced a kind of memorandum of understanding. we are particularly pleased, i think, that in the process we have managed, together with the commission and the council, to bring about a constructive debate in which we have tried, as far as possible, to define the specific role of the commission in the development context. the commission will not be like a 26th member state in terms of donors but will of course use its special powers, its special capacity, its neutrality and its multilateral character to push on and do some things better than others can do. in this process we have identified a number of areas where the commission, if i may put it that way, can make a difference and add value. finally, i would like to thank you for a good cooperation. i hope that in the coming weeks we will reach a final document and that we will eventually be able to return to this issue here in parliament and have a wider debate in which the council will also participate in order to discuss together how we can then best start implementing the new development policy and this policy statement.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the most important contribution the eu can make to the hong kong meeting is to pledge to remove its export subsidies for agriculture. it would provide development and livelihood opportunities for millions of people in the world's poorest countries. at first glance, the proposals made by the eu on agricultural policy seem quite good, but if you read the fine print, it turns out that this proposal from the eu side contains quite a lot of air. the biggest mistake the eu can make in the run-up to the hong kong negotiations and during the negotiations themselves is to link the agricultural reforms to the condition that the developing countries open up their service sectors. this is the kind of blackmail that could bring down the whole hong kong round. i believe that the demands of the third world to protect vital parts of their services sector from competition are fully legitimate in view of their own development.
madam president, i would like to thank you. there are many who are pessimistic about what the hong kong meeting can actually bring, but i am basically optimistic. we must succeed in reaching ambitious agreements in several of the areas we are negotiating. europe and the world cannot afford anything else. but for us to succeed, it's going to take a lot more than that from everyone involved. blaming each other, as we do today, has proved to be fruitless. it is particularly important that we achieve substantial liberalisations of trade in agricultural products. many members of this house tend to go against these very liberalisations, but they are wrong. both consumers and farmers in europe deserve access to a global market, and we need the pressure for change that liberalisation brings to strengthen our competitiveness. the agricultural negotiations are also - or perhaps above all - a moral issue. they are the basis for the development of many poor countries. it is therefore regrettable that it is precisely the agricultural negotiations which are hindering progress in the negotiations on industrial goods, but also on the trade in services, which is so important for europe. all those who say that commissioner mandelson has already gone too far and who think that we cannot liberalize agriculture further must realise that their policy has a high price, that it will cost europe - the world's largest trading partner - dearly if we do not get new market access. we must remember that it is not agriculture but the service sector which today accounts for 70 per cent of growth in europe. it is quite paradoxical that the country which refuses to give more in either the agricultural or the services negotiations is today the one with the largest global players in the services sector. they're the ones who should be the first to realize the need for success in hong kong. the politicians and the voluntary movements, like attack and others, who are trying to put the cart before the horse in the negotiations are doing so at the expense of the poor. less trade liberalisation and more quotas and tariffs or allowing developing countries to hide behind high protection walls is beneficial for the already privileged, but the people - the consumers in the poor countries - need investment. we must give them the same right to low tariffs and prices as consumers have here in europe. if we succeed in agreeing substantial trade liberalisation, european consumers will feel it in their wallets. if we fail, it will be a matter of life and death.
madam president, i would like to thank you. for many years the eu has had an unfair trade policy. it is time to listen to the needs of developing countries and to put an end to selfish trade policies. if you listen, you will hear that they are calling for an immediate end to export subsidies. if you listen to them, they say that our agricultural reforms are small, that they think our insistence on open trade in services is wrong. what the commissioner calls 'some sectors' is in fact a requirement for 93 out of 163 sectors to open up the market. what is 'benchmarking'? what benchmark is needed to push these countries down the poverty line? i don't even understand how the word "benchmarking" is used here. let them decide for themselves, it is right to give them police space! it's time to stop demanding retaliation and start giving, so i urge you, mandelson, to pick your best hong kong christmas spirit and enjoy giving without conditioning your gifts.
this resolution deals with a policy area which should be outside the eu's competence. the resolution stresses that sport organisations have a social significance and are important for creating tolerance, cooperation, teamwork and solidarity. this may be true, but why should the eu be concerned with this? there is no reason for the eu to create a budget line for development programmes and projects based on sport. nor is there any reason for the eu to set up programmes aimed at increasing the knowledge and experience of sports teachers in the field of development and sport. this is a national or purely regional issue. the resolution calls for the training of sports journalists with eu funds to help them avoid clichés, discriminatory language and racist expressions. it is important to work against racism and discrimination, but it is not necessary to take eu action for this type of programme. these are matters which each country must decide independently and in which the eu should have no say at all. the eu should work on genuinely crossborder issues which are not being dealt with satisfactorily by existing international organisations.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. this is about human rights the right to survive versus intellectual property rights the right to get paid for something you've produced. clearly, then, the right to life is more important than any other right. this is a first step in defending the right to life. the commission proposal is too weak and i think that parliament has made improvements to it, particularly as regards the possibility for ngos to use the directive. another improvement is that we are criticizing the need for compensation and how it should be defined - we need to criticize that - and that we are extending it to include countries which are not members of the wto, whose citizens' lives are as valuable as those of wto members. as far as adequate compensation is concerned, if you have no money, you have no adequate compensation, so there will be none, it must be clear. i hope that this will be done as soon as possible, because i do not think that this legislation is sufficient and the only way for us to know whether it is is to get it through quickly so that we can test it.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. since our shadow rapporteur, dan jørgensen, cannot be here today, i have the pleasure of speaking on behalf of the pse group. i would like to begin by thanking mr blokland for his excellent work. you have tabled many amendments which our group supports. the use of batteries is increasing and therefore it is particularly urgent to act now to ensure that the most environmentally friendly technology is used and that the most environmentally harmful batteries are banned and phased out. we therefore welcome the proposal before us, although we believe that the level of ambition should be higher than that proposed by the council. the use of the heavy metals mercury, cadmium and lead in batteries must be limited as far as possible. that is why parliament must tighten up the council's proposal now pending. cadmium, mercury and lead are already prohibited in materials and parts of vehicles placed on the market after 1 july 2003 and in new electrical and electronic equipment placed on the market after 1 july 2006, etc. it is therefore only natural that we now go ahead with the ban on the use of cadmium in batteries. it is of particular importance that the exemptions for cadmium in batteries and accumulators used in hand tools be amended to include a prohibition after a transitional period of four years. there are good alternatives to cadmium in these tools. i have this in my hand (sound of a hand tool) and it works -- which i hope you all hear -- perfectly. it's just to look closer here so you don't fall for what the lobbyists are trying to trick you into in the hallways. there are functional alternatives. they are made by several manufacturers, not only for private use but also heavy tools for professional use. don't think otherwise. just go to the manufacturers' websites and see for yourself. why would we be dumping a lot of cadmium into the air if we don't need to? why not insist on the use of the most environmentally friendly alternatives? a ban on cadmium in batteries in these tools would be a major environmental gain. in addition, it is important for european competitiveness that we stimulate the development of new technologies through legislation on environmentally friendly technologies when the time is right, and it is indeed now. we also think that the collection targets proposed by the council are too low. we want to raise them and we think, as does the rapporteur, that the legal basis for this proposal is article 175, because it is aimed at a better environment and nothing else.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. thank you, mr blokland, for trying to improve this proposal. this is an environmental issue and article 175 is natural. it is also about consumers and the confidence they can have in the products they buy. that is why i want amendment no 38 on guaranteed capacity labelling to be introduced on all batteries. today i have these two with me. on this one (holding up a blue screwdriver), you couldn't tell from the packaging how long the batteries lasted, but you could on this one (holding up a red screwdriver). this (blue screwdriver) is nickel-cadmium, while this (red screwdriver) is nickel-metal hydride. this blue one can only pull this many screws (holds up a board of screws), each screw representing ten. the red nickel metal hydride managed all these screws - that's more than twice as many. but you don't know that when you buy this. here, bad, old-fashioned technology is sold to consumers, and they're being duped by corporations today. without a tag, they're even more deceiving. i'm not the only one who's done this test. swedish consumer organisations have looked at nickel-metal hydride batteries after 500 charges. so it's life expectancy and the economy. it turns out that they last on average more than twice as long after 500 charges. why should europe lag behind in terms of technology transfer? what kind of export market are we going to have with old-fashioned bad technology? it's time to dump nickel-cadmium, it's time to vote for amendment 54. the industry says it's necessary in these types of machines. the best machine on the market can handle 18 minutes and 20 seconds instead of 2 minutes and 45 seconds of maximum use. it is new battery technology that benefits consumers and is good for the environment, not old technology that worsens for consumers and the environment. 75 percent of all refined cadmium is actually used in batteries, which is a small fraction of the total amount of cadmium. but the rest of the cadmium use is not intentional, it's a side effect that we'll treat and eliminate by other means. now it's about batteries, and then it's gone.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the junilista supports the proposal on the four freedoms of the internal market. the free movement of capital is fundamental to the proper functioning of the internal market. for this to work satisfactorily, a common regulatory framework is required. this must be designed in a simple way and must not undermine the possibility of institutional competition between member states. the extension of the deadlines in this report is welcome, as it will facilitate preparations for entry into force. we shall therefore vote in favour of the report. the commission's proposal for amendments, however, mentions that the european parliament has asked for an equal role for parliament and the council in monitoring the way the commission exercises its powers to implement the amendments. parliament is once again trying to extend its own power at the expense of the member states. the commission should not be a federal government and should be primarily supervised by the member states. it is unfortunate that parliament, in the interest of reaching an acceptable compromise with the council, has chosen to engage in an intra-institutional power struggle in this way. we shall therefore vote against amendments nos 1, 2, 4 and 11.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is a misunderstanding in this house to think that high tax rates provide social security. it's not. it is growing investment, new jobs and strong and growing tax revenues that create social security. in the country in europe with one of the highest corporate tax rates, germany, you get less tax revenue in relation to the economy than you get in, say, slovakia, where you have a low corporate tax rate but a flat tax rate. it is not the low-tax countries that receive new investment and new jobs that threaten security. i think that in a europe where we have 20 million unemployed there is reason to be self-critical of the way in which the policy has worked. the point is that a tax system that is clear, transparent and predictable creates good conditions for investment and new jobs. we see it in the countries that are growing the fastest in the european economy. having a common tax base and a common method of calculating it does not mean harmonizing taxes. on the contrary, it is good that each country can choose its corporate tax rate that best suits its circumstances. it's good we have competition. it is also very good that we have transparency so that we can see who de facto has the high taxes and who de facto has the low taxes. it is not as the former chancellor gerhard schröder in germany pointed out that some of the new member states are dumping their taxes. on the contrary, it is those countries with many loopholes and uneven and unpredictable tax systems that hinder small business and new investment. i therefore support this report and congratulate mr bersani on a job well done.
in writing. in all 18 reports, the rapporteur has proposed that the european parliament should be involved in the appointment and renewal of the executive directors of the various agencies. the commission has also proposed that the council should be able to adopt a decision on the basis of the opinion of the european parliament. we have therefore voted against the reports.
in writing. the eu has an important role to play in strengthening democracy and human rights in its neighbourhood. enlargement contributes to this positive development. that is why we are voting in favour of the malmström report. but we are against the attempt to use the eu's neighbourhood and partnership instruments to strengthen and develop the common foreign and security policy. we also do not believe that the instruments should be used to extend the eu's sphere of interest to areas which cannot be described as its neighbourhood.
in writing. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has also called for the introduction of a new directive on the protection of the environment. the position of the committee on the environment sets out clear priorities and clarifies the objectives of the directive. the junilist supports eu action on cross-border environmental issues. emissions of heavy metals are one such area. we therefore voted in favour of this report and broadly support the position of the committee on the environment on this matter. we are, however, against individual amendments. we are, for example, critical of the european parliament's decision to set up public information campaigns on the collection and recycling of batteries and of its views on how these campaigns should be financed (amendment 33). we have full confidence in the ability of the member states to achieve the requirements of the directive on their own. the member state concerned shall decide whether information campaigns, research or other measures are necessary for this purpose.
in writing. we have voted against all the amendments because we do not think that the european parliament should have any say in how energy is saved in the member states. this is best handled by the member states themselves.
in writing. building issues in the autonomous community of valencia are not a matter for the european parliament or the european union in general. the reasoning of the report is that all political issues fall within the competence of the eu institutions. this means that the elected assemblies of the member states, various types of elected regional assemblies, with several local assemblies elected by the citizens, could be overrun. we shall vote against this report.
in writing. i believe that the eu should not interfere in how a country manages its detailed planning. but in this case, the green politicians of the country concerned have explicitly pleaded with me to vote in favour of this as their democracy is threatened. building owners bribe politicians and they ignore both legislation and democracy. they believe that the problem must be brought to the attention of the outside world in order to get a reaction from the establishment. that is why i am voting in favour of the opinion on the petition sent to parliament by 15 000 people.
the committee on the internal market and consumer protection has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. democracy and freedom are not defended by restrictions on human rights. in totalitarian states, people are guilty until proven guilty. in a state of law, suspicion is the basis for restrictions of freedom. we're discussing a bill that allows for the surveillance of those the law claims to protect. at least do an independent impact assessment, and do it before the legislation comes into force. human rights are not something to be rushed and neglected. legislate honestly. don't pretend it's about terrorism when it's really about file sharing, census, taxes, trade and refugees. do not create new competition problems in europe. follow the harmonisation requirement of the data protection directive, follow article 95. the measure does not prevent terrorism. it risks damaging the eu's credibility if it is found to be contrary to the rule of law and the rights and freedoms of citizens. if the necessity of the measure cannot be demonstrated, the eu will force the member states to vote against their own constitutions, which include article 8 (2) of the council of europe convention. a constitutional crisis could arise. it is necessary to enshrine the rights and freedoms of the eu in the treaty before it goes too far. it's easy to get carried away with hatred for terrorists, i realize that. but to trample on liberty and rights is to give in to the assault on the rule of law, to abandon our values. preserve the right to privacy. don't make a big hole in our security. our european data protection is a european right to be proud of.

for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. the eu's foreign policy is based on the defence of peace, democracy, the rule of law and human rights, which is why we attach such great importance to the political part of the copenhagen criteria when negotiating with the new member states. here too, the eu has been very successful in spreading these values. of course, other countries are more difficult. although human rights are always on the table, it is difficult to achieve a dialogue. we have clauses, but parliament has, as you know, advocated that they should be made compulsory, that they should be activated. we cannot be satisfied with a vague protest when events such as those in tunisia occur, when fundamental human rights are grossly violated. tunisia is a country closely associated with the eu. the human rights dialogue with china and iran is going badly, we all know that. russia is not doing well either. today we have seen how our sakharov prize winners, the ladies from cuba, are not allowed to come here. i hope that the council and the commission will be able to strengthen the protests against the dictator in havana, who continues to howl all over the world. there is much more to be done in zimbabwe, belarus, burma, saudi arabia, etc. we know that the eu as a soft power is incredibly powerful. we are the only international actor that can really achieve great things if we are united in the defence of human rights and values. citizens around the world expect us to have a common foreign policy, to play a role on the international stage and to speak with one voice. this foreign policy, which takes the form of trade, aid, political and economic cooperation, must be guided at all times by respect for human rights. we as democracies have a responsibility to ensure that we fight injustice and spread democracy and human rights right into the villages of colombia, zimbabwe and north korea. this report is therefore an important and useful tool. i only hope that in the future it can become something more strategic, so that we have an even more coherent human rights policy.
madam president, i would like to thank you. a non-discriminatory system of tolling kilometres on european roads is important for environmental reasons but also for competition reasons. at the same time, it is important that the new 'euro vignette' takes account of the different conditions prevailing in europe, for example in the peripheral member states. it is therefore good that this compromise firstly takes account of different geographical locations, secondly enables socioeconomically fair pricing of infrastructure and thirdly makes it possible to differentiate the tariffs between different euro classes. this is important in countries where long distances and slow traffic make it impossible to achieve full cost recovery. i therefore think that the compromise is acceptable, since it takes account of the countries in europe's outer circle. thank you, madam president, and thank you all for a long and good job.
member of the commission. madam president, the message from nearly all the honourable members is loud and clear and makes sense. i am grateful for the constructive and very positive involvement of nearly all of you. i can assure you that the commission welcomes the amendments proposed by your rapporteur. that said, i wish to make a few comments about your remarks. firstly, mr mulder wants to be kept informed about the whole project. i am sure the commission will be able to do that. he is very negative about how things will progress during the next few days. let us hope things will progress well. in any case, the future for the financial perspectives looks positive. mrs mcavan made some very inspiring remarks. however, i wish to correct her on one issue concerning klm. i think i know that airline a little better than her. even if there were a subsidy for the runway, it would never be given to an airline but to the airport. i am not sure whether that is the case, but i wanted to make that remark. nearly everyone commented on the sum of eur 40 million for 2006. some were more enthusiastic about the amount of money than others. however, that is the amount available. what happens after 2006? that is when the real work will begin, because that is when we have to fulfil our promises to those countries. i can assure you that when, and only when, an agreement is reached on the financial perspectives, the commission plans to allocate a figure of eur 190 million in addition to the existing development budget.
junilista welcomes the reform of the eu sugar sector and considers it positive that this sector is also adapted to the market. this should have happened sooner. we are critical of the eu's export subsidies to domestic sugar growers and are positive that trade in sugar will be able to take place under fairer conditions in the future. without going into the budgetary details, i would stress that substantial financial support alone will not solve the problems that the reform of the sugar sector may bring to the acp countries. financial support does not guarantee the long-term conversion and restructuring of acp sugar sectors and industries. i also call for clearer strategies and priorities for the specific use of financial resources. otherwise, there is a very real risk that we are merely offering the acp countries a form of artificial respiration and that the aid provided by the eu will not have the intended effect.
mr president, i would like to thank you. my time is short and my focus will therefore be on the agreement reached on eu aid policy on the subject of the european consensus. unlike the budget debate and the lack of leadership there, i can be unequivocally positive about this agreement and the process that has led to it. the dialogue between the commission, the council and parliament in the autumn was both positive and constructive. i thank mr barroso and mr alexander for their positive comments here today and would like to thank both commissioner michel and minister benn for what i think is a very good cooperation. the eu is, as has already been said here today, the world's largest single donor of aid. however, the effectiveness and quality of aid have so far not been commensurate with its size. the eu member states are poorly coordinated, both among themselves and with the commission. this means high transaction costs, duplication of work and great difficulties for many of our partner countries. the new development policy, now in two parts addressing the specific work of the eu and the commission, will bring major progress in this area. better donor coordination, better coherence, better efficiency are therefore the cornerstones of this policy. we have also reached agreement on defining the specific role of the commission in development cooperation, which i believe will be very important. on the substantive issues, the fight against poverty remains a high priority. we are also giving greater attention to issues such as democracy and human rights, the importance of sustainable use of natural resources, the role of women in development work, etc. in conclusion, i would like to thank my colleagues on the committee on development for the very good cooperation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is ironic that the sakharov prize for transparency and human rights is awarded by parliament immediately after the 'surveillance directive' was voted. on the same day that we build walls against the freedom of our citizens, we give credit to those who break down those walls in the outside world. this directive, according to the spin doctors, is supposed to free us from murder and terror and organised crime, but in reality it is rather freeing us from the ideal of democracy. the trick of democracy is that you should be able to communicate with whoever you want, whenever you want, without worrying about those communications. proponents argue that this surveillance is necessary for the prosecution of the most serious crimes, but they are wrong. this applies to practically all offences covered by the european arrest warrant, i.e. hunting also of poachers. that is why the big media companies are now cheering this new directive. they don't have to change technology to more modern technology that would save money for consumers, but keep the old. the directive requires data to be stored for between 6 and 24 months, and 80 per cent of all e-mails are so-called spam. this is a junk directive that stores junk mail, which is hardly the most effective way to fight organized crime. it costs money and takes resources away from more effective measures. it also makes it easier for professional criminals to avoid being caught in the net. in the united states, the police freeze data. it's much more effective and simple and only affects people who are concrete suspects of a crime. where were you on march 17th? who did you meet? who were you sitting next to? you sat next to a known felon? how do you prove you didn't have contact? how do you know that the people you regularly e-mail are not large-scale felt-sharers who are being hunted by the police? how do you prove that the copyrighted material you sent wasn't copyrighted? do you know the hidden sides of the people you communicate with? in the future, too, the rule of law will exist and people will not usually be innocently convicted, but may be cleared of suspicion only after abusive interrogations and house searches. the greens voted against this directive.
in writing. i voted today in favour of the green amendment rejecting the proposed directive on data retention. i think the proposal is too far-reaching in terms of personal privacy, ineffective - it will not lead to an improved fight against terrorism - and it has been pushed through with frightening speed in a way that does not bode well for the future. i therefore voted against the final vote on the proposal.
in writing. in principle, the junilists believe that a decision of this magnitude for the citizens should not be taken by qualified majority. instead, the procedure should be directly submitted to national parliaments and decisions taken at intergovernmental level by the council acting unanimously in accordance with police and judicial cooperation in criminal matters. we also believe that the proposal goes far beyond the expected benefits. member states should facilitate the exchange of information between themselves in order to combat terrorism and organised crime more effectively. however, such cooperation does not require harmonisation at eu level for the storage of this huge amount of data. the proposal also raises considerable concerns about civil liberties and rights, where citizens are generally suspected. the junilist does not want to contribute to the creation of a european control society. if a member state wishes to introduce mandatory data retention, it should do so only after a proper national debate with decisions in national parliaments. to sneak such a sweeping legislation into the back room is highly unreasonable from a democratic point of view.
in writing. good laws are not made under time pressure and with insufficient decision-making. i am very critical of the way the process around the proposal for a decision on the storage of traffic data has been handled. it is a difficult question to answer, one that requires thought and a solid factual basis - both in terms of the privacy aspect, the technical implications and the real costs to telecom operators and thus consumers. we owe it to the citizens of europe. there is no doubt that traffic data can be of value in various criminal investigations. but that does not mean that we should give the go-ahead for a major stockpiling. traffic data should be used, but only in the context of very serious and well-defined crimes and following court decisions. the harmonization of eu rules is desirable, but far more restrictive than today's decision. the proposal to amend the directive adopted by parliament's committee on civil liberties and human rights was a step in the right direction. but this proposal was voted in favour of proposals that go too far. that is why i voted against the proposal for a directive. i would have liked to see a decision with shorter retention times, better privacy protection, the exclusion of e-mail and the use of traffic data only for certain serious and well-defined crimes.
in writing. good laws are not made under time pressure and with insufficient decision-making. i am very critical of the way in which the process surrounding the proposal for a decision on the storage of traffic data has been handled. it is a difficult question to decide, one that requires thought and solid factual support. we politicians owe it to the citizens of europe. undoubtedly, telephone records can be valuable in various criminal investigations. but that does not mean that we should give the go-ahead for a large-scale and systematic storage of boat calls and emails. traffic data should be used, but only in the context of very serious and well-defined crimes and following court decisions. the harmonization of eu rules is desirable, but far more restrictive than today's decision. in addition, telecommunications operators must be reasonably compensated for their additional costs. the proposal to amend the directive adopted by parliament's committee on civil liberties and human rights was a step in the right direction. but this proposal was voted in favour of a proposal identical to the one negotiated by the governments. that is why i voted against the proposal for a directive. i would have liked to see a decision with shorter retention periods, the exclusion of e-mail and the use of traffic data only for certain serious and well-defined crimes.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is good that the rules of the accession process are clear, and in my view they are. there is no doubt that both romania and bulgaria understand what is required of them. in my travels to these two countries and in my conversations with the people there, i have witnessed this for myself. everyone there knows that we are concerned about the remaining areas of improvement. everyone knows very well that we have eyes on them and are following every step of their development. but none of those with whom i have spoken, from government representatives to the people outside the cafes, are so strongly convinced that we really want to see them as members already in 2007, despite our saying so, despite our resolutions and reports. why is that? obviously, we send signals that are very clear about the problems, the difficulties, the demands, the negativity, but our signals of encouragement, of appreciation and, most of all, our sincerity in welcoming them are not as clear. i think it is good that we are closely monitoring the reforms that are being undertaken and how romania and bulgaria are meeting the requirements for membership. with so many repetitions in this chamber of words such as safeguard clauses, delaying accession, the debate on the eu's external borders, absorption capacity, i am afraid that the signal we are sending not only to romania and bulgaria but to the whole region is that europe has changed into a more closed and more cold europe. that is not my europe, that is not the europe i am fighting for, working for and defending. i believe that all the reforms that romania and bulgaria have carried out in recent times are proof of the efforts of these countries and the fact that they give high priority to their membership. the accession of romania and bulgaria to the eu is a strong catalyst and a factor of security for the whole region. i am convinced that with our help these countries can join in time for january 2007. i am also convinced that this will not be possible without our help and support and without a positive outlook.

in writing. we swedish social democrats have today voted in favour of the agreement on the 2006 budget reached between the commission, the council and parliament. the eu budget is primarily used to create full employment in europe and to contribute to sustainable social, economic and environmental development. we particularly welcome the response to our repeated calls for investment in the lisbon strategy, including more money for student exchange programmes and for eu research and development programmes. the payments in the agricultural category are reduced compared to the original proposal, which we think is reasonable. direct agricultural aid is oversized and leads to inefficient farming which damages the environment and widen the gap between the world's rich and poor. we cannot therefore support the writings deploring the cuts in agricultural aid decided by the council.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to increase the number of member states that have not yet ratified the convention to include the european union. in budgetary matters, parliament's role should be limited to priorities within the budgetary framework proposed by the council. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover the whole of the european union. as a result of these proposed increases in expenditure, new negotiations with the council took place. unfortunately, the council has accepted some increases in expenditure during the negotiations that have taken place. the commission is also proposing to increase the number of member states that have not yet adopted the euro. this development is extremely regrettable and shows the importance of not increasing the european parliament's influence.
in writing. i voted against because of the grounds relating to growth and turkey. i am saying no to short-term growth and yes to turkey, not the other way around. i regret that i could not support this proposal to reject the budget.
in writing. we moderates want to develop and modernise the budget of the european union. this means that we want to seek less subsidies and more investment in what the modern economy, global competition, requires, while at the same time we want to strengthen the union's capacity to be a strong international partner for freedom, peace and security. the current budgetary focus is old-fashioned and places too much emphasis on agriculture and subsidies to the old member states for tasks which they themselves should be able to fulfil. the new member states and the poorest regions are the first to benefit from the structural and cohesion funds. we have therefore worked against support for tobacco growing, against export subsidies and for the phasing out of agricultural aid. in return, we want to increase support for research and development, as well as security and neighbourhood policy development. the union needs a budget and we therefore vote in favour of the proposal, even if we believe that it needs substantial changes.
in writing. this report deals with the issue of heavy goods vehicle charges, which is of great importance for the proper functioning of the internal market. at present, there are several different models of road tolls in the european union, which can distort competition. road tolls may be eligible to charge for road wear and environmental pollution. the junilista has voted today to support several of the amendments in the report. we believe that heavy goods transport is an important part of the internal market, and that environmental problems are transnational and that there is therefore an added value in dealing with these issues at eu level.
in writing. the junilists welcome the reform of the eu sugar regime and consider it positive that this sector is being brought more closely into line with the market. we are critical of the eu's export subsidies to domestic sugar growers and are positive that trade in sugar will be able to take place under more reasonable conditions in the future. the acp countries have enjoyed special access to the eu market through preferential agreements. the new sugar market organization will therefore have consequences for certain acp countries. however, the change in the market organisation is necessary and in the long term in the interest of all parties. the eu parliament's rapporteur recommends that significant financial adjustment aid be paid to the acp countries. we would stress that such measures hardly guarantee the necessary restructuring. we call for clearer strategies and priorities for the specific use of financial resources. otherwise, there is a significant risk that the long-term restructuring needs will not be met to the desired extent.
in writing. the junilist believes that the eu should immediately stop concluding fisheries agreements with third countries. the common fisheries policy has led to the overfishing of many european waters and irreversible damage to sensitive marine environments. moreover, fisheries policy has devastating global consequences. the eu's fisheries agreements with third countries generate short-term revenue for the contracting countries. in the longer term, however, the agreements lead to overfishing and to difficulties in the development of local fisheries. the compensation paid goes to the local population to a limited extent. the agreements should be financed by the countries or fleets that choose to use them. fisheries agreements should not be concluded within the eu framework at all. the report contains a positive amendment, namely that the financial contributions should also be used for the benefit of the coastal population which lives off fishing (amendment 2). the report as a whole, however, stresses that the agreements are beneficial for both the eu and third countries. we do not share this view and have therefore voted against this report. the position expressed in this explanation of vote is our position of principle on this matter. the explanation of vote is thus relevant to future cases of a similar nature.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament. we believe that the commission's main priorities should be to develop the internal market, strengthen the eu's foreign and security policy, continue the work on the planned enlargement and work to strengthen the eu's competitiveness. however, we are doubtful about certain parts of the resolution which do not respect the principle of subsidiarity, such as the desire to review family leave in the eu and to increase efforts in the field of tourism.
in writing. the rapporteur believes that the public and businesses in the 10 new member states are not aware of the rights and opportunities offered by the harmonised eu legislation in terms of consumer protection. we fully share the view that consumer protection is an important issue. however, we are critical of several of the measures proposed in the report, such as the following: that the rapporteur advocates that consumer awareness should be given higher priority in school curricula throughout europe, which we believe should be decided at national rather than eu level; the commission should carry out a 'strategic information and education campaign' to prepare consumers for the introduction of the euro in the new member states; the commission should draw up a specific strategy to promote the development of a consumer culture in the new member states. this can be done independently by the respective member state; the report also stresses that the commission's proposal is not a simple one, but that it is a comprehensive one. that the governments of the new member states be called upon to finance the formation of consumer organizations, which we believe should be decided at national level; we regret that the european parliament is once again using a pressing political issue to introduce new budget lines and overreaching eu measures.
in writing. this report deals with the question of state aid for the development of disadvantaged regions. the junist party's position is that regional policy can be conducted at national level as long as it does not distort competition in the internal market.
in writing. we share the view that it is urgent to make eu aid more effective and that there is a need to coordinate existing aid efforts. it is good that the resolution addresses these aspects of development policy. the junilists, however, believe that the eu should not be involved in aid issues. this important area is to be dealt with primarily at national level. international coordination of action will be carried out within the framework of existing international organisations with legitimacy and experience. we are critical of certain phrases in the resolution, such as the eu's extension of current aid efforts. for the reasons stated above, we have decided to abstain on this question.
in writing. the resolution raises a serious issue. however, at this point, what are speculation and empty claims and what are facts? we welcome the establishment of a special committee of inquiry in the european parliament. any investigation that seeks to establish the facts of the case is welcome. but various party groups are now trying to make politics of this issue. we feel that there are insufficient facts to express our firm opinion on one side or the other. the european parliament's debate on motions for resolutions and amendments is, as usual, late in the day. so at this point we have tried to avoid taking too emotional a position before the facts of the case are known. we only wish to express our firm opinion that cooperation in the field of intelligence is a matter for the member states and is something they themselves decide in bilateral and multilateral relations.
in writing. this report calls for far-reaching eu action to improve the situation of women, particularly in the fisheries sector. the junilist believes that the eu is a union of values and that women and men should be treated equally and have the same opportunities and rights. we believe, however, that research projects, action programmes, specific vocational training and women's networks should be developed at national or local level and not regulated and supervised by the commission and the eu. we have therefore voted against this report.
